In short, the claim is that the contribution from descendant fields in the OPE can be expressed as the contribution from primary fields acted upon by some function determined solely by conformal symmetry.
We will see below that this is indeed correct.
In the following, for simplicity, we consider the OPE coefficients of level 1 descendants, but the OPE coefficients of any descendants can be obtained with the same procedure.
Rewrite (
2.134
) using the state-operator correspondence as follows:

\phi_{i}(z,{\bar{z}})\ket{h_{j}}\otimes\ket{\bar{h}_{j}}=\sum_{p}\sum_{\{\bm{n},\bar{\bm{n}}\}}C_{ijp\{\bm{n},\bar{\bm{n}}\}}z^{h_{p}+N-h_{i}-h_{j}}{\bar{z}}^{\bar{h}_{p}+\bar{N}-\bar{h}_{i}-\bar{h}_{j}}L_{-\bm{n}}\ket{h_{p}}\otimes\bar{L}_{-\bar{\bm{n}}}\ket{\bar{h}_{p}}.

(2.137)

Since different eigenstates are orthogonal to each other,
multiplying from the left by
\bra{h_{k}}L_{1}\otimes\bra{\bar{h}_{k}}
leads to

\bra{h_{k}}L_{1}\otimes\bra{\bar{h}_{k}}\phi_{i}(z,{\bar{z}})\ket{h_{j}}\otimes\ket{\bar{h}_{j}}=C_{ijk\{1,0\}}z^{h_{k}+1-h_{i}-h_{j}}{\bar{z}}^{\bar{h}_{k}-\bar{h}_{i}-\bar{h}_{j}}\bra{h_{k}}L_{1}L_{-1}\ket{h_{k}}\braket{\bar{h}_{k}|\bar{h}_{k}}.

(2.138)

From the OPE of primary fields (
2.104
) and the Ward-Takahashi identity (
2.88
),
we obtain

[L_{n},\phi_{i}(z,{\bar{z}})]=h_{i}(n+1)z^{n}\phi_{i}(z,{\bar{z}})+z^{n+1}{\partial}\phi_{i}(z,{\bar{z}}),

(2.139)

and using this, the left-hand side of (
2.138
) is given by

h_{k}C_{ijk}z^{h_{k}+1-h_{i}-h_{j}}{\bar{z}}^{\bar{h}_{k}-\bar{h}_{i}-\bar{h}_{j}}.

(2.140)

Also, from the Virasoro algebra (
2.33
), the right-hand side of (
2.138
) is given by

2h_{k}C_{ijk\{(1),0\}}z^{h_{k}+1-h_{i}-h_{j}}{\bar{z}}^{\bar{h}_{k}-\bar{h}_{i}-\bar{h}_{j}}.

(2.141)

Thus, we obtain

C_{ijk\{(1),0\}}=\frac{1}{2}C_{ijk}.

(2.142)

Similarly, the OPE coefficients of any descendant fields can be calculated solely from conformal symmetry.

So far, we have explained that thanks to conformal symmetry, the structure of the OPE becomes relatively simple.
This is indeed an important property, but there is another essential property to discuss the usefulness of OPE in CFT.
Let us consider calculating
n
-point correlation functions using OPE,

\braket{\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\cdots}=\sum_{p}C_{ijp}\sum_{\{\bm{n},\bar{\bm{n}}\}}\beta(h_{i},h_{j},h_{p};\bm{n})\beta(\bar{h}_{i},\bar{h}_{j},\bar{h}_{p};\bar{\bm{n}})z^{h_{p}+N-h_{i}-h_{j}}{\bar{z}}^{\bar{h}_{p}+\bar{N}-\bar{h}_{i}-\bar{h}_{j}}\braket{\phi_{p}^{\{\bm{n},\bar{\bm{n}}\}}\cdots}.

(2.143)

As seen from this equation,
using OPE, the
n
-point correlation function can be expressed in terms of
(n-1)
-point correlation functions.
The most important point here is that in CFT, this OPE expansion is not an asymptotic expansion but
a series expansion with a finite radius of convergence
.

The key to the finite radius of convergence in CFT lies in the state-operator correspondence.
Let us explain this below.
As explained in Section
2.6
,
one can construct states from local operators,

\ket{\Psi}=\phi_{i}(x)\phi_{j}(y)\ket{0}.

(2.144)

Any state in the Hilbert space can be expanded in terms of energy eigenstates.

\ket{\Psi}=\sum_{p}C_{p}(x-y)\ket{E_{p}}.

(2.145)

Here, the energy eigenstate
\ket{E_{p}}
is defined through the radial quantization centered at
y
.
Due to the properties of the Hilbert space, this expansion converges.
This holds in general QFT.
The crucial point is that in CFT, one can reconstruct local operators from states,

\ket{\Psi}=\sum_{p}C_{p}(x-y)\mathcal{O}_{p}(y)\ket{0},\ \ \ \ \ \mathcal{O}_{p}(y)\ket{0}=\ket{E_{p}}.

(2.146)

As one quickly realizes, this expansion in terms of local operators is the OPE itself.
Thus, from the equivalence of OPE and the energy eigenstate expansion, and the convergence of the energy eigenstate expansion,
the convergence of OPE can be proven.
Furthermore, as seen from this proof, the radius of convergence is the distance to the nearest insertion point among all other local operators excluding
\phi_{i}(x)
.
In other words, the condition for convergence is, letting
\{z_{i}\}
be the insertion points of the remaining operators, as follows:

\left|x-y\right|<\min_{i}\left|z_{i}-y\right|.

(2.147)

From this convergence of OPE, we obtain the following two important consequences:

•

As long as the OPE coefficients are known, any
n
-point correlation function can be calculated to any desired precision.
In general QFT, the OPE is an asymptotic expansion, so the OPE calculation method was not mainstream.
However, in CFT, the OPE becomes the common calculation method, which can be understood in this section.

•

OPE satisfies associativity.
When expanding
\braket{\phi_{i}(x)\phi_{j}(y)\phi_{k}(z)\cdots}

in terms of
\left|x-y\right|
and in terms of
\left|y-z\right|
,
one notices that there is an overlap in the radius of convergence.
This leads to the following
OPE associativity
,

\left(\phi_{i}(x)\phi_{j}(y)\right)\phi_{k}(z)=\phi_{i}(x)\left(\phi_{j}(y)\phi_{k}(z)\right).

(2.148)

The program for determining the OPE coefficients with this OPE associativity is called the
conformal bootstrap
.
This conformal bootstrap provides a non-perturbative calculation method that is entirely different from the traditional method based on Lagrangian,
bringing about remarkable progress in the understanding of field theory.

2.13
Four-Point Function and Conformal Bootstrap

Here, let us briefly explain the conformal bootstrap.
The detailed explanation will be lengthy, so we will save it for later,
and here we aim to give a sense of what it is about.

Figure 8:
Moving the topological operator corresponding to Virasoro generator.

The simplest example of the conformal bootstrap is obtained by imposing the OPE associativity on a four-point function on the plane.
Here, before discussing the four-point function, let us first consider
N<4
point functions.
Correlation functions with three points or less are trivial in the sense that their functional form is completely determined by conformal symmetry.
This can be immediately seen from the OPE,

\bra{h_{i},\bar{h}_{i}}\phi_{j}(z,{\bar{z}})\ket{h_{k},\bar{h}_{k}}=C_{ijk}z^{h_{i}-h_{j}-h_{k}}{\bar{z}}^{\bar{h}_{i}-\bar{h}_{j}-\bar{h}_{k}}.

(2.149)

More directly, it can also be derived as follows:

\displaystyle\bra{0}XL_{-n}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}\oint_{z_{i}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}z^{1-n}\braket{T(z)X}

(2.150)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}\oint_{z_{i}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}z^{1-n}\left(\frac{h_{i}}{(z-z_{i})^{2}}+\frac{1}{z-z_{i}}{\partial}_{z_{i}}\right)\braket{X}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left(\frac{(n-1)h_{i}}{z_{i}^{n}}-\frac{1}{z_{i}^{n-1}}{\partial}_{z_{i}}\right)\braket{X}.

Here, in the first equation, we moved the Virasoro generator as shown in Figure
8
.
In the second equation, we used the OPE (
2.104
) between the primary field and the energy-momentum tensor.
In the third equation, we used the residue theorem.
Recall here that the vacuum is invariant under global conformal transformations,

L_{n}\ket{0}=0,\ \ \ \ \ \text{if}\ n=-1,0,1.

(2.151)

Combining (
2.150
) and (
2.151
), we obtain the following three equations:

\displaystyle\sum_{i}{\partial}_{z_{i}}\braket{X}

\displaystyle=0,

(2.152)

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\left(h_{i}+z_{i}{\partial}_{z_{i}}\right)\braket{X}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\left(2h_{i}z_{i}+z_{i}^{2}{\partial}_{z_{i}}\right)\braket{X}

\displaystyle=0.

Since this only looks at the part of the conformal Ward-Takahashi identity that pertains to global conformal transformations,
it is sometimes called the
global Ward-Takahashi identity
.
The global Ward-Takahashi identity for correlation functions with three points or less can be solved exactly.

\displaystyle\braket{\phi_{i}(z_{i},{\bar{z}}_{i})}

\displaystyle=0,

(2.153)

\displaystyle\braket{\phi_{i}(z_{i},{\bar{z}}_{i})\phi_{j}(z_{j},{\bar{z}}_{j})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{ij}}{z_{ij}^{2h_{i}}{\bar{z}}_{ij}^{2\bar{h}_{i}}},

\displaystyle\braket{\phi_{i}(z_{i},{\bar{z}}_{i})\phi_{j}(z_{j},{\bar{z}}_{j})\phi_{k}(z_{k},{\bar{z}}_{k})}

\displaystyle=C_{ijk}z_{ij}^{h_{k}-h_{i}-h_{j}}z_{jk}^{h_{i}-h_{j}-h_{k}}z_{ik}^{h_{j}-h_{i}-h_{k}}\times(\text{anti-holomorphic part}).

Here,
z_{ij}\equiv z_{i}-z_{j}
.
Although the four-point function is not determined by conformal symmetry alone,
using the OPE (
2.143
), which expresses it as a sum of three-point functions, we obtain the following expression:

\displaystyle\braket{\phi_{4}(z_{4},{\bar{z}}_{4})\phi_{3}(z_{3},{\bar{z}}_{3})\phi_{2}(z_{2},{\bar{z}}_{2})\phi_{1}(z_{1},{\bar{z}}_{1})}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i>j}z_{ij}^{\frac{h}{3}-h_{i}-h_{j}}{\bar{z}}_{ij}^{\frac{\bar{h}}{3}-\bar{h}_{i}-\bar{h}_{j}}G^{21}_{34}(z,{\bar{z}}),

(2.154)

\displaystyle G^{ji}_{kl}(z,{\bar{z}})\equiv\braket{\phi_{l}|\phi_{k}(1,1)\phi_{j}(z,{\bar{z}})|\phi_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p}C_{ijp}C_{klp}\mathcal{F}^{ji}_{kl}(h_{p}|z)\overline{\mathcal{F}^{ji}_{kl}(h_{p}|z)}.

Here,
h\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{4}
.
z
is called the
cross ratio
, defined as
z\equiv\frac{z_{12}z_{34}}{z_{13}z_{24}}
.

\mathcal{F}^{ji}_{kl}(h_{p}|z)
is a function of
\{h_{i},h_{j},h_{k},h_{l},h_{p},c,z\}
called the
Virasoro block
, defined as follows:

28
28

28

Unlike in two dimensions, in higher dimensions only global conformal symmetry exists. Thus operators get organized into primaries and descendants based on the global conformal group. The function capturing the contribution of descendants, given that of a global conformal primary in the intermediate channel, to the four-point function, is called the global conformal block.
To distinguish it from the global conformal block, what is organized by Virasoro symmetry is often called the Virasoro block, not just the conformal block.

Virasoro block

\mathcal{F}^{ji}_{kl}(h_{p}|z)\equiv z^{h_{p}-h_{i}-h_{j}}\sum_{\bm{n}}\beta(h_{i},h_{j},h_{p},\bm{n})z^{N}\frac{\braket{\phi_{l}|\phi_{k}(1,1)L_{-\bm{n}}|\phi_{p}}}{\braket{\phi_{l}|\phi_{k}(1,1)|\phi_{p}}}.

(2.155)

The Virasoro block plays the role of organizing the contributions of descendant fields.
The theory dependence from the three-point functions cancels out between the denominator and numerator, so the Virasoro block is a function independent of the theory.
Just as we expanded the solutions to the Schrödinger equation using special functions in quantum mechanics,
the correlation functions in CFT are spanned by a functional basis called Virasoro blocks.
The expansion coefficients of these correlation functions are the OPE coefficients.

The radius of convergence for the
\phi_{1}\phi_{2}
OPE in the four-point function
\braket{\phi_{4}|\phi_{3}(1,1)\phi_{2}(z,{\bar{z}})|\phi_{1}}
is
0<\left|z\right|<1
.
On the other hand, the radius of convergence for the
\phi_{2}\phi_{3}
OPE in the four-point function transformed by the conformal transformation
z\to 1-z
,
\braket{\phi_{4}|\phi_{1}(1,1)\phi_{2}(1-z,1-{\bar{z}})|\phi_{3}}
, is
0<\left|1-z\right|<1
.
Since there is overlap in these radii of convergence, the following equality holds:

Conformal bootstrap equation (Crossing equation)

\sum_{p}C_{12p}C_{34p}\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)\overline{\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)}=\sum_{p}C_{32p}C_{14p}\mathcal{F}^{23}_{14}(h_{p}|1-z)\overline{\mathcal{F}^{23}_{14}(h_{p}|1-z)}.

(2.156)

This is called the crossing equation, representing the associativity of the OPE.
The crossing equation is a non-trivial equation for the OPE coefficients,
and the approach of determining the OPE coefficients by solving this equation is called the
conformal bootstrap
.
To distinguish the Virasoro block expansions appearing on the left-hand side and right-hand side of this equation,
the Virasoro blocks on the left-hand side are called the
s-channel
, and those on the right-hand side are called the
t-channel
.

The conformal bootstrap equation is invariant under the rescaling of fields and OPE coefficients:

•

\phi_{i}\to\lambda_{i}\phi_{i}
,

•

C_{ijk}\to\kappa C_{ijk}
.

Therefore, the solution to the conformal bootstrap has the ambiguity due to the rescaling.
For CFTs including the vacuum state, this ambiguity is generally removed by choosing the normalization as follows:

\displaystyle\braket{\phi|\phi}

\displaystyle=1,

(2.157)

\displaystyle C_{ii\mathbb{I}}

\displaystyle=1.

Finally, a note on notation. The overline indicates that all parameters are anti-holomorphic.

\overline{\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)}\equiv\mathcal{F}^{\bar{2}\bar{1}}_{\bar{3}\bar{4}}(\bar{h}_{p}|{\bar{z}}).

(2.158)

To avoid long expressions, it is common to abbreviate the expression (holomorphic)
\times
(anti-holomorphic) as the absolute value.
For example, many references use the following abbreviated notation:

\left|\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)\right|^{2}\equiv\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)\overline{\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)}.

(2.159)

However, note that the anti-holomorphic part is not necessarily the complex conjugate of the holomorphic part and can take independent values.

Furthermore, due to the analogy with Feynman diagrams,
the Virasoro block is sometimes represented by the following graph:

\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)\equiv\parbox{85.35826pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=85.35826pt]{s.pdf}}}\equiv\parbox{71.13188pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=71.13188pt]{sd.pdf}}}.

(2.160)

The second graph is the dual graph of the first one (a graph where the vertices correspond to the faces of the original graph).
Using this diagrammatic notation, the conformal bootstrap equation can be expressed as follows:

\sum_{p}C_{12p}C_{34p}\left|\parbox{85.35826pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=85.35826pt]{s.pdf}}}\right|^{2}=\sum_{p}C_{32p}C_{14p}\left|\parbox{62.59596pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=62.59596pt]{t.pdf}}}\right|^{2}.

(2.161)

2.14
Belavin-Polyakov-Zamolodchikov Equation

Recall the process of removing null states when constructing the irreducible representation of the Virasoro algebra in terms of Verma modules.
A similar process applies to the calculation of correlation functions,
leading to the requirement that correlation functions involving null states vanish.
This is called
Belavin-Polyakov-Zamolodchikov (BPZ) equation
[
9
]
.
This requirement is generally independent of the Ward-Takahashi identity.

As the simplest example of the BPZ equation, consider the equation obtained from the level 1 null state,

\braket{\chi_{11}(z)\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})}=0.

(2.162)

Correlation functions involving descendant fields can generally be written as derivatives of correlation functions of primary fields.

\displaystyle\braket{(L_{-n}\phi_{1})(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2}),\cdots}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{z_{1}}\frac{dw}{(w-z_{1})^{n-1}}\braket{T(w)\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})\cdots}

(2.163)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{i=2}^{k}\oint_{z_{i}}\frac{dw}{(w-z_{1})^{n-1}}\left(\frac{h_{i}}{(w-z_{i})^{2}}+\frac{{\partial}_{i}}{w-z_{i}}\right)\braket{\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})\cdots}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=2}^{k}\left(\frac{(n-1)h_{i}}{(z_{i}-z_{1})^{n}}-\frac{{\partial}_{i}}{(z_{i}-z_{1})^{n-1}}\right)\braket{\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})\cdots}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{L}_{-n}\braket{\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})\cdots}.

In the first equation, we used (
2.92
); in the second equation, we used (
2.104
); and in the third equation, we moved the integral contour (see Figure
8
).
More generally, the following holds,

\braket{(L_{-{n_{1}}}L_{-{n_{2}}}\cdots\phi_{1})(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2}),\cdots}=\mathcal{L}_{-{n_{1}}}\mathcal{L}_{-{n_{2}}}\cdots\braket{\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})\cdots}.

(2.164)

Using this result, (
2.162
) can be rewritten as follows:

{\partial}_{z}\left((z-z_{1})^{h_{2}-h_{1}}(z-z_{2})^{h_{1}-h_{2}}(z_{1}-z_{2})^{-h_{1}-h_{2}}\right)=0.

(2.165)

Here, we used
h_{11}=0
. This equation implies that
\braket{\phi_{11}(z)\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})}=\braket{\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})}
is nonzero only when
h_{1}=h_{2}
. This result is consistent with the global Ward-Takahashi identity result (
2.153
).

As a more non-trivial example, consider the BPZ equation obtained from the level 2 null state (
2.62
),

\braket{\chi_{21}(z)\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})}=\left(\mathcal{L}_{-2}+\frac{1}{b^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{-1}^{2}\right)\braket{\phi_{21}(z)\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})}=0.

(2.166)

This equation means that
\braket{\phi_{21}(z)\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})}
is nonzero only under the following condition:

\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}\pm\frac{b}{2},\ \ \ \ \ \ h_{i}\equiv\frac{Q^{2}}{4}-\lambda_{i}^{2}.

(2.167)

This implies that the primary fields appearing in the
\phi_{21}\phi_{1}
OPE are restricted to only two types of primary fields that satisfy the equation (
2.167
). This represents a constraint on the dynamical data, i.e. the OPE, a restriction that cannot be obtained from the Ward-Takahashi identity.

The BPZ equation is a powerful tool for determining CFT data, but it is important to note that it can only be applied in special theories where the spectrum includes the zeros of the Kac determinant
\{h_{rs}\}
.

29
29

29

A theory with a spectrum composed of
\{h_{rs}\}
, called the minimal model, can be regarded as one where all information is determined solely by symmetry. Consequently, the BPZ equation, which is a consequence of symmetry, determines the correlation functions, which are generally dynamical objects.

In CFTs with
c>1
,
h_{rs}
is non-positive, therefore, no fields other than the vacuum lead to the BPZ equation in unitary CFTs with
c>1
. As a result, to investigate the CFT data of
c>1
unitary CFTs, other methods, such as conformal bootstrap, must be employed.

3
Virasoro Blocks and Conformal Bootstrap

Whether calculating correlation functions or solving the conformal bootstrap,
it is essential to understand the details of the Virasoro blocks, which form the functional basis.
However, Virasoro blocks are extremely complicated special functions that cannot be expressed in closed form,
requiring various techniques to understand their properties.
Here, we will introduce some particularly important ones.

3.1
OPE
=
Inserting the Identity Operator

OPE involves expanding the product of two local operators as a sum of single local operators.
This “mapping” can be constructed using the identity operator

1=\sum_{p:\text{primary}}\Pi_{h_{p}}\otimes\bar{\Pi}_{\bar{h}_{p}},

(3.1)

as

\sum_{p:\text{primary}}\Pi_{h_{p}}\otimes\bar{\Pi}_{\bar{h}_{p}}\phi_{i}(z_{i},{\bar{z}}_{i})\phi_{j}(z_{j},{\bar{z}}_{j})\ket{0}.

(3.2)

Here,
\Pi_{h_{p}}
is the projection operator onto the Verma module
\mathcal{V}(c,h_{p})
.

\Pi_{h_{p}}\equiv\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{n},\bm{m}\\
N=M\end{subarray}}\ket{h_{p},\bm{n}}[G^{(N)}_{c,h_{p}}]_{\bm{n},\bm{m}}^{-1}\bra{h_{p},\bm{m}}.

(3.3)

In fact, (
3.2
) is a sum of local operators.

\sum_{p:\text{primary}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{n},\bar{\bm{n}}\end{subarray}}a_{p,\bm{n},\bar{\bm{n}}}\ket{h_{p},\bm{n}}\otimes\ket{\bar{h}_{p},\bar{\bm{n}}}=\sum_{p:\text{primary}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{n},\bar{\bm{n}}\end{subarray}}a_{p,\bm{n},\bar{\bm{n}}}L_{-\bm{n}}\bar{L}_{-\bar{\bm{n}}}\phi_{p}(0,0)\ket{0},

(3.4)

where the expansion coefficients are defined as follows:

a_{p,\bm{n},\bar{\bm{n}}}\equiv\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{m}\\
M=N\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{\bm{m}}\\
\bar{M}=\bar{N}\end{subarray}}[G^{(N)}_{c,h_{p}}]_{\bm{n},\bm{m}}^{-1}[G^{(\bar{N})}_{c,\bar{h}_{p}}]_{\bar{\bm{n}},\bar{\bm{m}}}^{-1}\bra{h_{p},\bm{m}}\otimes\bra{\bar{h}_{p},\bar{\bm{m}}}\phi_{i}(z_{i},{\bar{z}}_{i})\phi_{j}(z_{j},{\bar{z}}_{j})\ket{0}.

(3.5)

In other words, performing the OPE is equivalent to inserting the identity operator.
From this perspective, the conformal bootstrap equation simply becomes a relation that connects two different ways to insert the identity operator (see Figure
9
).
While this may seem trivial, the fact that the time axes are taken differently, meaning the quantization methods differ,
makes it a non-trivial relation between different quantizations.
As a result, it becomes a non-trivial relation concerning the states on the Hilbert spaces associated with each quantization.
Considering the OPE as the insertion of the identity operator is more convenient for dealing with general conformal bootstrap equations,
so this lecture mainly adopts this viewpoint.

Figure 9:
Conformal bootstrap equation from two different insertions of the identity operator.

Inserting the identity operator into a four-point function naturally yields an expansion into each Verma module.

\braket{h_{4},\overline{h}_{4}|\phi_{3}(1,1)\phi_{2}(z,\overline{z})|h_{1},\overline{h}_{1}}=\braket{h_{4},\overline{h}_{4}|\phi_{3}(1,1)\biggl(\sum_{p}\Pi_{h_{p}}\otimes\bar{\Pi}_{\bar{h}_{p}}\biggr)\phi_{2}(z,\overline{z})|h_{1},\overline{h}_{1}}.

(3.6)

This is precisely the expansion of the correlation function in terms of Virasoro blocks.
Here, the Virasoro block is interpreted as the partial wave projected onto a single Verma module.

\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)\overline{\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)}\propto\braket{h_{4},\overline{h}_{4}|\phi_{3}(1,1)\Pi_{h_{p}}\otimes\bar{\Pi}_{\bar{h}_{p}}\phi_{2}(z,\overline{z})|h_{1},\overline{h}_{1}}.

(3.7)

This partial wave is universal, except for a constant factor.
Since the constant part is proportional to the OPE coefficients, it cannot be determined from conformal symmetry alone.
By normalizing the Virasoro block as follows:

\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)\xrightarrow[z\to 0]{}z^{h_{p}-h_{1}-h_{2}},

(3.8)

the relationship between the Virasoro block and the projection of the correlation function onto the Verma module can be written as:

\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)\overline{\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)}=\frac{\braket{h_{4},\overline{h}_{4}|\phi_{3}(1,1)\Pi_{h_{p}}\otimes\bar{\Pi}_{\bar{h}_{p}}\phi_{2}(z,\overline{z})|h_{1},\overline{h}_{1}}}{C_{12p}C_{34p}}.

(3.9)

3.2
Notation

In studying the properties of Virasoro blocks,
various notations are introduced.
Here, we summarize this notation for reference.

Primary Field (
\nu,\bar{\nu}
):

\lim_{z,{\bar{z}}\to 0}\phi_{i}(z,{\bar{z}})\ket{0}=\ket{\nu_{i}\otimes\bar{\nu}_{i}}.

(3.10)

In this notation, descendant states within the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{h_{i}}
are described as follows:

\ket{\nu_{i,\bm{m}}}=L_{-\bm{m}}\ket{\nu_{i}}\equiv L_{-m_{1}}\dots L_{-m_{j}}\ket{\nu_{i}}.

(3.11)

Here,
\bm{m}
is an ordered set
\bm{m}=\{m_{1},m_{2},\dots,m_{j}\in\mathbb{N}|m_{1}\leq m_{2}\leq\dots\leq m_{j}\text{ and }M=m_{1}+m_{2}+\cdots+m_{j}\}
.
Descendant fields defined by the state-operator correspondence are denoted as
\phi_{i,\bm{m}}
.
In the following, we use
\nu_{i}
to denote primary states, while
\xi_{i}
denotes general states.

Three-Point Block
\rho(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}|z)
:

For states
\xi_{i}
in the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{h_{i}}
, the three-point block is defined as follows:

\braket{\xi_{1},\bar{\xi}_{1}|\phi_{2}(z,{\bar{z}})|\xi_{3},\bar{\xi}_{3}}=\rho(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}|z)\rho(\bar{\xi}_{1},\bar{\xi}_{2},\bar{\xi}_{3}|{\bar{z}})C_{123}.

(3.12)

Assuming
\xi_{i}
are eigenstates of
L_{0}
,

L_{0}\xi_{i}=\Delta(\xi_{i})\xi_{i}.

(3.13)

Here, we explicitly denote the eigenvalue dependence on the eigenstate to cover the most general situations.
From the Ward-Takahashi identity for dilatation, we get the following
z
dependence:

\rho(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}|z)=z^{{\Delta}(\xi_{1})-{\Delta}(\xi_{2})-{\Delta}(\xi_{3})}\rho(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}|1).

(3.14)

By definition, the three-point block for primary fields
\nu_{i}
is given by

\rho(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3}|1)=1.

(3.15)

The key point is that the three-point block is defined by removing the OPE coefficients, the only parameters that depend on the theory, from the three-point function.
Thus, by definition, the three-point block is merely a function that does not depend on the theory.

Gram Matrix
G^{(N)}_{c,h}:

\left[G^{(N)}_{i}\right]_{\bm{n}\bm{m}}=\braket{\nu_{i,\bm{n}}|\nu_{i,\bm{m}}}.

(3.16)

Here,
N=M
must be satisfied.
Since the central charge remains constant within a single CFT,
we omit the central charge from the Gram matrix’s subscript and retain only the primary field’s label
i
.

Using the above notation, the Virasoro block can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{n},\bm{m}\\
N=M\end{subarray}}\rho(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},\nu_{p,\bm{n}}|1)\left[G_{p}^{(N)}\right]_{\bm{n},\bm{m}}^{-1}\rho(\nu_{p,\bm{m}},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|z).

(3.17)

This expression is equivalent to the definition of the Virasoro block (
2.155
) that appeared in Section
2
.
In older literature such as the Yellow book
[
1
]
, the Virasoro block is defined by (
2.155
),
but the expression (
3.17
) where the Gram matrix explicitly appears is more convenient for calculations.
Therefore, in practical 2D CFT research, the expression (
3.17
) is used in most cases.

As seen from the expression (
3.17
),
the sum over states
\nu_{p,\bm{n}}\in V(c,h_{p})
within the Verma module is very complicated,
making the calculation of Virasoro blocks generally difficult.
This often becomes the bottleneck in solving the conformal bootstrap for 2D CFT.
Conversely, since the Virasoro block contains much information about the theory,
clarifying the properties of the Virasoro block often directly leads to understanding the universal properties of the theory.
Additionally, the fact that the parameters identifying a 2D CFT are very few is due to the benefit of having much information packed into the infinite sum in the Virasoro block.
Therefore, the complexity of the Virasoro block can be seen as both a disadvantage and an advantage of 2D CFT.

3.3
Brute Force Approach

Using the Ward-Takahashi identity,
we obtain the following formula for the three-point blocks:

\rho(L_{-n}\xi_{3},\xi_{2},\xi_{1}|z)=\rho(\xi_{3},\xi_{2},L_{n}\xi_{1}|z)+\sum^{l(n)}_{m=-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}n+1\\
m+1\\
\end{array}\right)z^{n-m}\rho(\xi_{3},L_{m}\xi_{2},\xi_{1}|z).

(3.18)

Here, when
n\geq-1
,
l(n)=n
, otherwise
l(n)=\infty
.
Similarly, the Ward-Takahashi identity also provides the following formulas:

\displaystyle\rho(\xi_{3},L_{-1}\xi_{2},\xi_{1}|z)

\displaystyle={\partial}_{z}\rho(\xi_{3},\xi_{2},\xi_{1}|z),

(3.19)

\displaystyle\rho(\xi_{3},L_{-n}\xi_{2},\xi_{1}|z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}n-2+m\\
n-2\\
\end{array}\right)\left(z^{m}\rho(L_{n+m}\xi_{3},\xi_{2},\xi_{1}|z)+(-1)^{n}z^{1-n-m}\rho(\xi_{3},\xi_{2},L_{m-1}\xi_{1}|z)\right),\ \ \ \ \ n\geq 2.

By combining these formulas to convert raising operators one by one into lowering operators,
the Virasoro block can be calculated in a finite number of steps.
This procedure is essentially the same as the calculation of
\beta
performed in Section
2.12
.

Can this procedure be used to calculate the Virasoro block with high precision?
From (
3.18
), the calculation of the level
N
three-point block is expected to be completed in polynomial time with respect to
N
.
Furthermore, when the dimension of the subspace at level
N
is
d_{N}
, it is necessary to perform the above three-point block calculation
d_{N}
times.
The Gram matrix also appears in the definition of the Virasoro block, and its calculation can be made in polynomial time with respect to
d_{N}

[
10
]
.
The calculation of the inverse matrix is known to be
O(d_{N}^{3})
.
Therefore, the overall computation time will be polynomial with respect to
d_{N}
.
However, in reality, it is not practical to perform this on a laptop.
This is because the dimension of the representation space increases exponentially as the level
N
increases, as indicated by the Hardy-Ramanujan formula (
2.42
).
In general, exponential algorithms are only feasible for small numbers.

30
30

30

In general, a laptop can perform about
10^{6}\sim 10^{8}
operations (such as
+
or
\times
) per second.
Therefore, for an
O(2^{N})
exponential algorithm, even
N=60
would take many years.

In this case, only a few initial terms (low-level terms) of the Virasoro block can be computed.
Such coarsely calculated Virasoro blocks are often insufficient for 2D CFT research.

To avoid exponential growth in computation,
it is necessary to perform level
N
calculations with a computation cost independent of the dimension of the representation space.
This efficient algorithm was realized by Zamolodchikov
[
11
,
12
]
.
This computational method is widely used not only in numerical calculations, but also in analytical calculations and the discovery of new dualities.
Therefore, it will be introduced in this lecture.

3.4
Zamolodchikov Recursion Relation

The properties of the inverse Gram matrix are fundamentally important in the calculation of Virasoro blocks.
For convenience, let us summarize the properties of the Gram matrix here:

Kac Formula:

This was introduced in Section
2.5
,

\det G^{(N)}_{c,h}\propto\prod^{N}_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,s=1\\
1\leq rs\leq N\end{subarray}}\left(h-h_{rs}\right)^{p(N-rs)}.

(3.20)

Here,

\displaystyle h_{rs}\equiv\frac{Q^{2}}{4}-\frac{\left(rb+\frac{s}{b}\right)^{2}}{4},\ \ \ \ c\equiv 1+6Q^{2},\ \ \ \ Q\equiv\left(b+\frac{1}{b}\right).

(3.21)

Below, we generally write the primary state with conformal dimension
h_{rs}
as
\nu_{rs}
.
Among the descendant states of
\nu_{rs}
, we denote null states specifically as
\chi_{rs}
.

Poles of the Inverse Gram Matrix:

The inverse Gram matrix has simple poles at
h=h_{rs}
.
It has no other poles.
Alternatively, when viewed as a function of
c
with
h
fixed,
when the solution of (
3.21
) is denoted by
c_{rs}
,
the inverse Gram matrix has simple poles at
c=c_{rs}
.
It has no other poles.

The key idea is to focus on the fact that the Virasoro block is a meromorphic function of
c
(i.e. an analytic function with only isolated singularities) and to perform a Mittag-Leffler expansion.
The Mittag-Leffler expansion represents a function as an expansion around its discrete set of poles
\{z_{i}\}
,

f(z)=c_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{i}}{z-z_{i}}.

(3.22)

Here,
c_{i}
are constants, with
c_{i\geq 1}
being residues.
For this expansion to be valid, the following conditions must be satisfied:

\left|\frac{f(z)}{z}\right|\xrightarrow[z\to\infty]{}0.

(3.23)

The Virasoro block satisfies this condition,
and its limit can be written as

\lim_{c\to\infty}\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)=z^{h_{p}-h_{1}-h_{2}}{}_{2}F_{1}(h_{p}+h_{2}-h_{1},h_{p}+h_{3}-h_{4};2h_{p};z),

(3.24)

where
{}_{2}F_{1}
are the hypergeometric functions.
Although this section focuses on the Mittag-Leffler expansion of the Virasoro block,
the large
c
Virasoro block is important for various reasons,
so its derivation will be detailed in Section
3.7
.

Let us determine the coefficients of the Mittag-Leffler expansion.
This corresponds to calculating the following residue,

\lim_{h\to h_{rs}}(h-h_{rs})\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h|z).

(3.25)

Since the three-point block does not have a pole,
the only surviving contribution in the limit
h\to h_{rs}
comes from the inverse Gram with poles at
h_{rs}
.
In particular, only the components of the inverse of the Gram matrix with the null descendant label survive. Roughly speaking, this is because the norm of the null descendant is zero.

31
31

31

Let us consider constructing the inverse matrix from the cofactor matrix.
Recall that the cofactor
A_{\bm{n},\bm{m}}
is composed of the determinant of the minor matrix obtained by removing the
\bm{n}
-th row and the
\bm{m}
-th column.
When the label
\bm{n}
or
\bm{m}
of the cofactor matrix corresponds to a null descendant,
the order of the zero decreases by the extent to which the null descendant is excluded in constructing the cofactor.
In other words, the elements of the inverse of the Gram matrix have poles in
\{h_{rs}\}
when
\bm{n}
or
\bm{m}
is a null descendant.
Otherwise, the order of the zero of the cofactor matches that of the Kac determinant, and thus no pole appears.

Thus, we obtain the following result for the residue,

\lim_{h\to h_{rs}}(h-h_{rs})\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h|z)=A_{rs}^{c}\sum_{N=M}z^{h_{rs}-h_{1}-h_{2}+rs+N}\rho(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},L_{-\bm{n}}\chi_{rs}|1)[G^{(N)}_{c,h_{rs}+rs}]^{-1}_{\bm{n},\bm{m}}\rho(L_{-\bm{m}}\chi_{rs},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|1).

(3.26)

Here, we use the fact (
2.43
) that in the limit
h\to h_{rs}
,
\chi_{rs}
behaves like a primary field.
Although the norm of
\chi_{rs}
becomes zero in the limit
h\to h_{rs}
,
we are multiplying by
h-h_{rs}
, so

\lim_{h\to h_{rs}}\frac{\braket{\chi_{rs}|\chi_{rs}}}{h-h_{rs}}\equiv\left(A^{c}_{rs}\right)^{-1}

(3.27)

remains.
A_{rs}^{c}
can be calculated from the Virasoro algebra and is written as follows,

A_{rs}^{c}=\frac{1}{2}\prod_{m=1-r}^{r}\prod_{n=1-s}^{s}\left(mb+\frac{n}{b}\right)^{-1},\ \ \ (m,n)\neq 0,(r,s).

(3.28)

Moreover, it is known that the three-point block involving a null state is given by the following
fusion polynomial
,

\displaystyle\rho(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\chi_{rs})=\rho(\chi_{rs},\nu_{2},\nu_{1})=P^{rs}_{c}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}h_{1}\\
h_{2}\\
\end{array}\right]\equiv\prod^{r-1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=1-r\\
p+r=1\mod 2\end{subarray}}\prod^{s-1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=1-s\\
q+s=1\mod 2\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{p,q}\right)\left(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{p,q}\right).

(3.29)

Here,
\lambda_{i}
is defined as follows,

h_{i}\equiv\frac{Q^{2}}{4}-\lambda_{i}^{2}.

(3.30)

Also,
\lambda_{p,q}
corresponds to
\lambda
for the zeros
h_{pq}
of the Kac determinant,

h_{pq}=\frac{Q^{2}}{4}-\lambda_{p,q}^{2},\ \ \ \ \ \lambda_{p,q}=\frac{1}{2}\left(pb+\frac{q}{b}\right).

(3.31)

Finally, for the calculation of the three-point block involving the null descendant field,
the three-point block has the following “factorization”,

\rho(L_{-\bm{n}}\chi_{rs},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|1)=\rho(L_{-\bm{n}}\nu_{h_{rs}+rs},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|1)\rho(\chi_{rs},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|1).

(3.32)

Here,
\nu_{h_{rs}+rs}
is a primary field with conformal dimension
h_{rs}+rs
(normalized by (
3.27
)).
Combining these, we obtain the following result,

\lim_{h\to h_{rs}}(h-h_{rs})\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h|z)=A^{c}_{rs}P^{rs}_{c}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}h_{1}\\
h_{2}\\
\end{array}\right]P^{rs}_{c}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}h_{4}\\
h_{3}\\
\end{array}\right]\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{rs}+rs|z).

(3.33)

Let us rewrite this as a function of
c
using (
3.21
),

\text{Res}_{c=c_{rs}(h)}{\mathcal{F}_{c}}^{21}_{34}(h|z)=-\frac{{\partial}c_{rs}(h)}{{\partial}h}A^{c_{rs}}_{rs}P^{rs}_{c_{rs}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}h_{1}\\
h_{2}\\
\end{array}\right]P^{rs}_{c_{rs}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}h_{4}\\
h_{3}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathcal{F}_{c_{rs}}}^{21}_{34}(h_{rs}+rs|z).

(3.34)

Here, the dependence of the Virasoro block on the central charge is written explicitly.

Finally, the Mittag-Leffler expansion of the Virasoro block is given as follows.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}_{c}}^{21}_{34}(h|z)

\displaystyle=z^{h-h_{1}-h_{2}}{}_{2}F_{1}(h+h_{2}-h_{1},h+h_{3}-h_{4};2h;z)

(3.35)

\displaystyle-\sum_{r\geq 2,s\geq 1}\frac{1}{c-c_{rs}(h)}\frac{{\partial}c_{rs}(h)}{{\partial}h}A^{c_{rs}}_{rs}P^{rs}_{c_{rs}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}h_{1}\\
h_{2}\\
\end{array}\right]P^{rs}_{c_{rs}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}h_{4}\\
h_{3}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathcal{F}_{c_{rs}}}^{21}_{34}(h+rs|z).

Let us recall that the Virasoro block has the following expansion in terms of
z
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{n},\bm{m}\\
N=M\end{subarray}}\rho(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},\nu_{p,\bm{n}}|1)\left[G_{p}^{(N)}\right]_{\bm{n},\bm{m}}^{-1}\rho(\nu_{p,\bm{m}},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|z)

(3.36)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{n},\bm{m}\\
N=M\end{subarray}}\rho(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},\nu_{p,\bm{n}}|1)\left[G_{p}^{(N)}\right]_{\bm{n},\bm{m}}^{-1}\rho(\nu_{p,\bm{m}},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|1)z^{h_{p}-h_{1}-h_{2}+N}

\displaystyle\equiv z^{h_{p}-h_{1}-h_{2}}\sum_{N}\mathcal{F}^{(N)}_{c,h_{p}}z^{N}.

If we know the coefficients
\mathcal{F}^{(N)}_{c,h_{p}}
of this expansion up to
N=k
for any
c
and
h_{p}
,
by substituting this into (
3.35
),
we can determine
\mathcal{F}^{(N+2)}_{c,h_{p}}
for any
c
and
h_{p}
.
In other words, (
3.35
) derives a recurrence relation for the coefficients of the Virasoro block,

\mathcal{F}^{(N)}_{c,h}=\sum_{k>1}^{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,s\in\mathbb{N}\\
rs=k\end{subarray}}\alpha^{rs}_{c_{rs},h}\mathcal{F}^{(N-k)}_{c_{rs},h+rs}+\beta^{(N)}_{h}.

(3.37)

Here, we define

\displaystyle\alpha^{rs}_{c_{rs},h}

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{{\partial}c_{rs}(h)}{{\partial}h}A^{c_{rs}}_{rs}P^{rs}_{c_{rs}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}h_{1}\\
h_{2}\\
\end{array}\right]P^{rs}_{c_{rs}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}h_{4}\\
h_{3}\\
\end{array}\right],

(3.38)

\displaystyle\beta^{(N)}_{h}

\displaystyle\equiv[z^{N}]{}_{2}F_{1}(h+h_{2}-h_{1},h+h_{3}-h_{4};2h;z),

where,
[z^{N}]f(z)
denotes the coefficient of
z^{N}
in the series expansion of
f(z)
.

Since the number of pairs
(r,s)
that satisfy
k=rs
is given by the number of divisors of
k
, there are
O(k\ln k)
such pairs.
By summing these
O(k\ln k)
terms for each
k=2,\dots,n
,
we need
O(n^{2}\ln n)
operations to calculate
\mathcal{F}^{(n)}_{c,h}
.
Since we sequentially calculate for
n=1,\dots N-1
to obtain
\mathcal{F}^{(N)}_{c,h}
,
we finally perform
O(N^{3}\ln N)
operations.

32
32

32

By storing the frequently used calculation results in memory,
we avoid repeated calculations.
Such an algorithm is called
dynamic programming
.

As this is a polynomial-time algorithm,
it is sufficiently efficient compared to the exponential algorithm introduced in Section
3.3
.
Specifically, while the exponential algorithm takes an astronomical amount of time to compute the
z^{100}
term,
the polynomial algorithm mentioned above can compute the
z^{1000}
term in a realistic time (as demonstrated in the author’s research
[
13
,
14
,
15
]
).

3.5
Virasoro Characters and Modular Bootstrap

So far, we have looked at the Virasoro block expansion of the four-point function on the Riemann sphere.
In fact, the Virasoro block expansion can be performed for
N
-point functions on a Riemann surface of arbitrary genus.
Recalling that the conformal bootstrap is a relation between different ways of expanding a correlation function in the Virasoro block basis,
we notice that the conformal bootstrap equations can be obtained from various correlation functions other than the four-point function.

33
33

33

Not all of these conformal bootstrap equations are independent,
so we do not need to solve all of them.
In fact, it is known that solving the conformal bootstrap for the four-point function on the sphere and the one-point function on the torus is sufficient to determine all the CFT data.

Here, as an example of the Virasoro block expansion other than the four-point function, we consider the Virasoro block expansion of the torus zero-point function, i.e. the partition function.

Figure 10:
Construction of the torus partition function.
(Left) Imaginary time evolution by
\omega_{1}
with
H=2\pi\left(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{12}\right)
.
(Middle) Rotation by
\omega_{2}
with
P=2\pi i(L_{0}-\bar{L}_{0})
.
(Right) The torus partition function is created by joining both ends (i.e. taking the trace).

The partition function can be constructed from a cylinder evolved in imaginary time by
\omega_{1}
with
H=2\pi\left(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{12}\right)
(see the left of Figure
10
).
Since we are now considering time evolution on a cylinder with period
1
,
we add the Casimir energy
-2\pi\frac{c}{12}
to the Hamiltonian as seen in (
2.124
).
To create a torus, we need to join both ends of the cylinder,
and in doing so, we can twist the cylinder (see the middle of Figure
10
).
This twist is realized by rotating by
\omega_{2}
with
P=2\pi i(L_{0}-\bar{L}_{0})
.
Finally, the torus is completed by joining both ends of the cylinder (i.e. taking the trace) (see the right of Figure
10
).
Setting
(\tau,\bar{\tau})\equiv(-\omega_{2}+i\omega_{1},-\omega_{2}-i\omega_{1})
,
the partition function can be written as follows,

Z(\tau)\equiv{\text{tr}}_{\mathcal{H}}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}},\ \ \ \ \ \ q\equiv\mathrm{e}^{2\pi i\tau},\bar{q}\equiv\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi i\bar{\tau}}.

(3.39)

Here,
\mathcal{H}
is the Hilbert space of the CFT (
2.46
).
The parameter
\tau
of the Riemann surface is called the
moduli
.

There are countless ways to take the time axis on the torus, and they should all be equivalent.
The mapping that shifts to a different choice of the time axis is called the
modular transformation
.
The modular transformation is generated by the following two transformations.

Figure 11:

A diagram illustrating the modular
S
transformation.
As shown in the left figure, the torus is represented by two periods
\{1,\tau\}
.
We refer to
1
and
\tau
as the angular cycle and the thermal cycle, respectively.
The modular
S
transformation corresponds to exchanging the angular cycle and the thermal cycle.
The torus transformed by the modular
S
transformation is depicted in the right figure, which is equivalent to the torus in the left figure up to a rescaling.
Due to scale invariance, the path integrals on these two tori yield the same result.
This is called the modular
S
invariance of the partition function.

Figure 12:
Modular bootstrap equation.
This figure shows that the result of the calculation does not change depending on which cycle the identity operator is inserted along.

T
Transformation

T:\tau\to\tau+1.

(3.40)

Let us see how the partition function changes under this transformation,

Z(\tau+1)={\text{tr}}_{\mathcal{H}}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi i(L_{0}-\bar{L}_{0})}.

(3.41)

Since the eigenvalues of
L_{0}-\bar{L}_{0}
correspond to the spin,
the invariance under the modular
T
transformation implies that the spin must be an integer.

34
34

34

In this discussion, we have assumed the CFT is bosonic. For fermionic CFTs, the situation is slightly more subtle. Fermionic theories have half-integer spin and need a spin structure to be defined. On the torus, this means specifying periodic/anti-periodic boundary conditions along both the time and space circles. The four boundary conditions transform as a triplet and a singlet under the modular group. This more intricate structure can potentially lead to more powerful constraints from modular invariance
[
16
]
.

S
Transformation

S:\tau\to-\frac{1}{\tau}.

(3.42)

Roughly speaking, this corresponds to the exchange of two periods of the torus, that is, the angular cycle and the thermal cycle (see Figure
11
).
This exchange does not change the result of the path integral on the torus.
When expressing the partition function in the operator formalism,
we insert the identity operator,
and by swapping the time axis and the space axis, it changes which cycle of the torus the identity operator is inserted along (see Figure
12
).
However, the result of the calculation does not change depending on how the identity operator is inserted,
so these are equivalent,

Z(\tau)=Z\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right).

(3.43)

This is exactly the torus partition function version of the conformal bootstrap equation in the same spirit as Figure
9
.

By combining the
S
and
T
transformations,
we can derive various conformal bootstrap equations from the partition function.
These are specifically called
modular bootstrap equations
.
While the modular
T
invariance merely implies the integrality of the spin,
the modular
S
invariance non-trivially constrains the spectrum.
Therefore, when referring to the modular bootstrap equation,
it generally means (
3.43
).

The partition function can be expressed using Virasoro characters as follows,

Z(\tau)=\sum_{p}\chi_{p}(\tau)\overline{\chi_{p}(\tau)}.

(3.44)

Here, the Virasoro character is a special function defined by

\chi_{i}(\tau)\equiv{\text{tr}}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}=\sum_{n}d(n)q^{h_{i}+n-\frac{c}{24}},

(3.45)

where
d(n)
is the number of descendant states at level
n
in the (irreducible) Verma module.
If the Verma module does not contain null states,
d(n)=p(n)
.
Note that this function is a special function determined solely by the Virasoro algebra,
independent of the details of the theory.
While (
3.17
) refers to the Virasoro block for the four-point function on the Riemann sphere,
the Virasoro character is the Virasoro block for the zero-point function on the torus.

Here are some comments on the modular bootstrap equation:

•

The modular bootstrap equation constrains the spectrum,
but it does not uniquely determine the theory.
In fact, there are examples of different CFTs having the same partition function
[
17
]
.
To identify the theory (especially, the OPE coefficients of the theory), we need to solve the bootstrap equation for the four-point function.

•

The modular bootstrap is not a subset of the conformal bootstrap for four-point functions but an independent condition.
It is known that solving the conformal bootstrap for the four-point function and the one-point function on the torus is sufficient to completely determine the theory
[
18
]
.
The reason for this is simply that from the fusion transformation of the four-point Virasoro block and the modular transformation of the torus one-point block,
one can construct any fusion transformation (transformation between bases of functions for
N
-point functions on any Riemann surface).
This will be explained in Section
7.5
.

•

Unlike the conformal bootstrap equations for four-point functions or one-point functions on the torus,
the modular bootstrap equation does not involve OPE coefficients.
In this sense,
the modular bootstrap equation is much easier to handle.
Therefore, when investigating the possibility of the existence or universal properties of a given theory,
one often considers the modular bootstrap first.
In fact, many notable constraints have been obtained just by considering the modular bootstrap.

•

While the conformal bootstrap equation for four-point functions is easily generalized to higher dimensions,
the higher-dimensional version of the modular bootstrap equation is somewhat tricky.
Only recently has the higher-dimensional modular bootstrap equation been developed
[
19
]
.

3.6
Virasoro Block on Pillow Geometry

Here, we describe the method of handling the four-point function on a cylinder.
This method is useful when considering the convergence and approximation of the Virasoro block,
and it has been applied in many studies.

Figure 13:
Pillow geometry is the space
T^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
obtained by quotienting a torus by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Here, we consider a conformal transformation that maps the four points on the Riemann sphere to the corners of the pillow geometry.

The space obtained by quotienting a torus by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(denoted as
T^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
) is called
pillow geometry
.
We represent the complex coordinates on a torus using the following identification.

w\sim w+2\pi\sim w+2\pi\tau.

(3.46)

The pillow metric is obtained by the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
identification
w\sim-w
.
We consider mapping the four-point function on the Riemann sphere to the pillow metric.
Such a conformal transformation is given by the Weierstrass elliptic function (or its inverse function)
[
20
]
.

35
35

35

This conformal transformation is also used in studies of 2nd-Renyi mutual information (e.g. in the author’s papers
[
21
,
22
]
).

This conformal transformation maps the four points on the Riemann sphere to the corners of the pillow metric (see Figure
13
).
The moduli
\tau
is expressed as a function of the cross ratio
z
as follows,

\tau=i\frac{K(1-z)}{K(z)}.

(3.47)

Here,
K(z)
is the complete elliptic integral of the first kind,

36
36

36

In the particle physics field of research, this definition is mainly used (presumably because Mathematica is based on this definition).
On the other hand, in mathematical textbooks, the following definition is often used for the complete elliptic integral of the first kind,

K(z)\equiv\int^{\frac{\pi}{2}}_{0}\frac{d\theta}{\sqrt{1-z^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}}.

(3.48)

Be careful not to confuse results based on different definitions.

K(z)\equiv\int^{\frac{\pi}{2}}_{0}\frac{d\theta}{\sqrt{1-z\sin^{2}\theta}}=\frac{\pi}{2}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},1;z\right).

(3.49)

The inverse function can also be expressed as follows,

z=\left(\frac{\theta_{2}(\tau)}{\theta_{3}(\tau)}\right)^{4},

(3.50)

where the Jacobi theta functions
\theta_{i}
are defined using the
elliptic nome

q\equiv\mathrm{e}^{\pi i\tau}
as follows,

37
37

37

Note that
q
here is the elliptic nome
q\equiv\mathrm{e}^{\pi i\tau}
.
When using
q\equiv\mathrm{e}^{2\pi i\tau}
defined for the partition function, the expression changes slightly.

\displaystyle\theta_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}q^{n^{2}},

(3.51)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}q^{n^{2}},

\displaystyle\theta_{4}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{n}q^{n^{2}}.

In the pillow metric, the Virasoro block is interpreted as a propagator on the cylinder,

38
38

38

Since
\phi_{p}
is interpreted as an intermediate state,

h_{p}
is sometimes called the
internal weight
.
In contrast,
h_{i}
is called the
external weight
.

\braket{\phi_{34}^{p}|q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}|\phi_{12}^{p}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}q^{h_{p}+n-\frac{c}{24}},\ \ \ \ \ q\equiv\mathrm{e}^{\pi i\tau}.

(3.52)

Here,
\ket{\phi_{12}^{p}}
is the state created by inserting the operators
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
at the corners of the pillow and being projected onto the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{p}
.
As seen from this expression, in the pillow geometry, the
z
-expansion is replaced by the
q
-expansion.
Adding the conformal factor for the conformal transformation from the Riemann sphere to the pillow geometry,
the Virasoro block is expressed as follows,

\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)=\left(\theta_{3}(\tau)\right)^{\frac{c}{2}-4(h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}+h_{4})}z^{\frac{c}{24}-h_{1}-h_{2}}(1-z)^{\frac{c}{24}-h_{3}-h_{4}}\braket{\phi_{34}^{p}|q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}|\phi_{12}^{p}}.

(3.53)

One of the advantages of this expression is that it is expanded in
q
.
The radius of convergence for the
q
-series expansion is 1,

39
39

39

One may have encountered the statement “the convergence of the Virasoro block is not proven”.
As seen in the proof of the convergence of the OPE,
we implicitly assume that the state space is a Hilbert space.
Without this assumption, we can consider the problem of whether the
z
- or
q
-series expansion in the Virasoro block converges, where the series expansion coefficients are functions of
\{h_{i},h_{j},h_{k},h_{l},h_{p},c\}
determined by the Virasoro algebra.
This is unproven.
It converges in all analytically known examples and generally converges numerically,
so it should not be a problem when solving physical problems.

covering the entire complex plane.
In particular, the entire
z
-plane is mapped only to the eye-shaped region in the
q
-plane (see Figure
14
),
so the
q
-expansion converges better than the
z
-expansion.

Figure 14:
The entire
z
-plane with a branch cut along
[1,\infty)
maps to the interior of a unit circle in the
q
-plane, specifically to the eye-shaped region shown in blue.

When expressing the Virasoro block in terms of the elliptic nome, it is known that the limit
h_{p}\to\infty
can be expressed in a simple form as follows,

\braket{\phi_{34}^{p}|q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}|\phi_{12}^{p}}\xrightarrow[h_{p}\to\infty]{}\left(16q\right)^{h_{p}-\frac{c}{24}}\prod^{\infty}_{n=1}(1-q^{2n})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\left(16q\right)^{h_{p}-\frac{c}{24}}q^{\frac{1}{24}}\eta(\tau)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(3.54)

where the Dedekind eta function is defined as

\eta(\tau)\equiv q^{\frac{1}{12}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1-q^{2n}\right).

(3.55)

The Dedekind eta function has the following relationship with the Jacobi theta functions,

\eta(\tau)^{3}=\frac{1}{2}\theta_{2}(\tau)\theta_{3}(\tau)\theta_{4}(\tau).

(3.56)

Using this property along with (
3.50
) and
\theta_{2}^{2}+\theta_{4}^{2}=\theta_{3}^{2}
, we obtain

\eta(\tau)^{-\frac{1}{2}}=16^{\frac{1}{24}}\theta_{3}(\tau)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(z(1-z)\right)^{-\frac{1}{24}}.

(3.57)

Thus, the limit
h_{p}\to\infty
of the Virasoro block can be written as follows,

\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)\xrightarrow[h_{p}\to\infty]{}\left(16q\right)^{h_{p}-\frac{c-1}{24}}\left(\theta_{3}(\tau)\right)^{\frac{c-1}{2}-4(h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}+h_{4})}z^{\frac{c-1}{24}-h_{1}-h_{2}}(1-z)^{\frac{c-1}{24}-h_{3}-h_{4}}.

(3.58)

If we express the Virasro block as

\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)=\left(16q\right)^{h_{p}-\frac{c-1}{24}}\left(\theta_{3}(\tau)\right)^{\frac{c-1}{2}-4(h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}+h_{4})}z^{\frac{c-1}{24}-h_{1}-h_{2}}(1-z)^{\frac{c-1}{24}-h_{3}-h_{4}}H^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|q),

(3.59)

by applying (
3.23
) to
H^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|q)
and using (
3.33
), we obtain the following Mittag-Leffler expansion,

Zamolodchikov
h
-recursion relation

H^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|q)=1+\sum_{r,s\geq 1}\frac{q^{rs}}{h-h_{rs}}A^{c}_{rs}P^{rs}_{c}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}h_{1}\\
h_{2}\\
\end{array}\right]P^{rs}_{c}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}h_{4}\\
h_{3}\\
\end{array}\right]H^{21}_{34}(h_{rs}+rs|q).

(3.60)

This expansion in
h
is called the
Zamolodchikov
h
-recursion relation
,
and in contrast, (
3.35
) is called the
Zamolodchikov
c
-recursion relation
.
As explained in Figure
14
, the
q
-expansion converges better than the
z
-expansion,
providing a better approximation for the Virasoro block.
Therefore, unless there is a special reason, we use the Zamolodchikov
h
-recursion relation.
Code for calculating the Virasoro block using the Zamolodchikov
h
-recursion relation is provided in
[
23
]
.

3.7
Large-
c
Virasoro Block

In this section, we derive the large-
c
limit of the Virasoro block introduced in (
3.24
),

Large
c
Virasoro block

\lim_{c\to\infty}\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)=z^{h_{p}-h_{1}-h_{2}}{}_{2}F_{1}(h_{p}+h_{2}-h_{1},h_{p}+h_{3}-h_{4};2h_{p};z).

(3.61)

Recall that the Virasoro block is obtained by inserting the projection operator onto the Verma module

\Pi_{h_{p}}\equiv\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{n},\bm{m}\\
N=M\end{subarray}}\ket{h_{p},\bm{n}}[G^{(N)}_{c,h_{p}}]_{\bm{n},\bm{m}}^{-1}\bra{h_{p},\bm{m}}

(3.62)

into the four-point function.
We consider how this projection operator simplifies in the
c\to\infty
limit.

First, recall that the inverse matrix of
A
can be written using the cofactor matrix
\tilde{A}
as follows,

A^{-1}=\frac{1}{\det A}\tilde{A}.

(3.63)

From this, for the element
[G^{(N)}_{c,h_{p}}]_{\bm{n},\bm{m}}^{-1}
of the inverse matrix to be non-zero in the large
c
limit,
the cofactor
[\tilde{G}^{(N)}_{c,h_{p}}]_{\bm{n},\bm{m}}
must have the same maximum power of
c
as the determinant
\det G^{(N)}_{c,h_{p}}
.
Since the diagonal elements of
G^{(N)}_{c,h_{p}}
have the highest power of
c
,
the maximum power of
c
in
\det G^{(N)}_{c,h_{p}}
is the sum of the maximum powers of the diagonal elements.
For the cofactor to have the same maximum power as the determinant, the elements removed in constructing the cofactor must be
O(c^{0})
.
This occurs only when the labels
(\bm{n},\bm{m})
of the matrix elements are as follows,

\bm{n}=\bm{m}=(-1,-1,\cdots,-1).

(3.64)

This is because when
\bm{n}
contains
L_{n\leq-2}
, calculating its norm using the Virasoro algebra inevitably produces a central term (
=O(c)
).
In this case, removing elements with the highest power of 1 or more in constructing the cofactor
[\tilde{G}^{(N)}_{c,h_{p}}]_{\bm{n},\bm{m}}

results in the cofactor having a lower maximum power than the determinant.
Thus, in the large
c
limit, the only non-zero elements of the inverse Gram matrix are given by

\lim_{c\to\infty}[G^{(N)}_{c,h_{p}}]_{\bm{n},\bm{n}}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\braket{h|L_{1}^{N}L_{-1}^{N}|h}}=\frac{1}{N!(2h)_{N}}.

(3.65)

Here,
(a)_{n}\equiv\frac{\Gamma(a+n)}{\Gamma(a)}
is the Pochhammer symbol.
Additionally, using the Ward-Takahashi identity, we can show

\rho(L_{-1}^{n}\nu,\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|1)=(h+h_{2}-h_{1})_{n}.

(3.66)

Using these, we obtain the following expression,

\displaystyle\lim_{c\to\infty}\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{n}=\underbrace{(-1,-1,\cdots,-1)}_{N}\end{subarray}}\rho(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},\nu_{p,\bm{n}}|1)\left[G_{p}^{(N)}\right]_{\bm{n},\bm{n}}^{-1}\rho(\nu_{p,\bm{n}},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|z)

(3.67)

\displaystyle=\sum_{N}\frac{(h_{p}+h_{3}-h_{4})_{N}(h_{p}+h_{2}-h_{1})_{N}}{N!(2h_{p})_{N}}z^{h_{p}+N-h_{1}-h_{2}}

\displaystyle=z^{h_{p}-h_{1}-h_{2}}{}_{2}F_{1}(h_{p}+h_{2}-h_{1},h_{p}+h_{3}-h_{4};2h_{p};z).

This implies that in the large
c
limit, only the
L_{-1}^{N}
descendant states contribute.
Since
L_{-1}
is the raising operator of the global conformal algebra, this is the global conformal block.

The motivation for deriving the large
c
limit of the Virasoro block in this section was to obtain the Zamolodchikov
c
-recursion relation (
3.35
).
However, the application of the large
c
Virasoro block goes beyond the efficient computation of Virasoro blocks.
In fact, the large
c
limit corresponds to the classical limit in the context of the AdS/CFT correspondence,
making the large
c
Virasoro block extremely useful for studying semiclassical gravity.
This will be explained in the section on the AdS/CFT correspondence (Section
5
).

4
Classification of CFTs and Conformal Bootstrap

So far, the discussion has been abstract, so one may not have seen the physics clearly.
Therefore, in this section, we will review the results while touching on specific examples of CFTs.

4.1
Minimal Models

When the spectrum of a CFT consists of a finite number of irreducible representations of the Virasoro algebra,
the CFT is called a
minimal model
.
Minimal models are labeled by two coprime natural numbers
(p,q)
and are denoted as
\mathcal{M}(p,q)
.
The main CFT data of
\mathcal{M}(p,q)
are as follows:

Central Charge

c=1-6\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{pq},\ \ \ \ \ p>q\in\{2,3,\cdots\}.

(4.1)

Spectrum

\mathcal{H}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r<q\\
1\leq s<p\end{subarray}}\mathcal{V}_{h_{rs}}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{V}}_{h_{rs}}.

(4.2)

Here,
h_{rs}
are the zeros of the Kac determinant (
2.60
),

\displaystyle h_{rs}\equiv\frac{Q^{2}}{4}-\frac{\left(rb+\frac{s}{b}\right)^{2}}{4},\ \ \ \ \ c\equiv 1+6Q^{2},\ \ \ \ Q\equiv\left(b+\frac{1}{b}\right).

(4.3)

In other words, all primary fields appearing in a minimal model are degenerate.

40
40

40

Generally, there are infinitely many primary fields that can appear in the OPE,
but in minimal models, since all primary fields are degenerate,
the BPZ equation significantly restricts the primary fields that can appear in the OPE,
realizing the finiteness of the minimal model.

4.1.1
Conformal Bootstrap in Minimal Models

The simplest example of minimal models is the Ising model
\mathcal{M}(4,3)
.
The Ising model has the following three primary fields:

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}:(h_{\mathbb{I}},\bar{h}_{\mathbb{I}})=(h_{11},h_{11})=(0,0),

(4.4)

\displaystyle\sigma:(h_{\sigma},\bar{h}_{\sigma})=(h_{12},h_{12})=\left(\frac{1}{16},\frac{1}{16}\right),

\displaystyle\epsilon:(h_{\epsilon},\bar{h}_{\epsilon})=(h_{21},h_{21})=\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right).

Let us evaluate a four-point function using the conformal bootstrap in this theory.
From the BPZ equation, the four-point function of
\sigma
satisfies the following equation,

\left(\mathcal{L}_{-2}-\frac{4}{3}\mathcal{L}_{-1}^{2}\right)\braket{\sigma(\infty)\sigma(1)\sigma(z,{\bar{z}})\sigma(0)}=0.

(4.5)

The following Virasoro blocks are obtained as two linearly independent solutions to this equation,

41
41

41

One may think that these can be directly derived from the Zamolodchikov recursion relation.
However, when one tries it, divergent terms appear when the central charge is rational.
Since the Virasoro block itself does not diverge, each divergence must cancel out nicely.
Therefore, it is theoretically possible to obtain the Virasoro blocks for rational central charges by taking the appropriate limit from the Zamolodchikov recursion relation.
However, due to technical difficulties, this remains an open problem.
In actual numerical calculations, the Virasoro block for rational central charges is prepared approximately by slightly shifting from the rational value.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mathbb{I}|z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\sqrt{1+\sqrt{1-z}}}{\left(z(1-z)\right)^{\frac{1}{8}}},

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon|z)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\sqrt{1-\sqrt{1-z}}}{\left(z(1-z)\right)^{\frac{1}{8}}}.

As we saw in Section
3
, generally, the Virasoro block cannot be written in a closed form.
However, when the Verma module contains null descendant states,
the process of removing invariant subspaces from the Verma module reduces the number of descendant states in the irreducible Verma module.
As a result, the expression of the Virasoro block, which is a function that organizes the contributions from descendant states, can sometimes be simplified.
In this case, the reason the Virasoro block of the Ising model could be written in a closed form is precisely because of this.
Moreover, the fact that the solution space is spanned by only two Virasoro blocks is due to the unique property of minimal models (i.e. finite spectrum).
In general, correlation functions are described by infinite sums or integrals of Virasoro blocks.

In terms of these Virasoro blocks, the four-point function can be expressed as follows,

\braket{\sigma(\infty)\sigma(1)\sigma(z,{\bar{z}})\sigma(0)}=\left|\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mathbb{I}|z)\right|^{2}+C_{\sigma\sigma\epsilon}^{2}\left|\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon|z)\right|^{2}.

(4.7)

Note that the coefficient for the vacuum block is given by
1
because of the normalization (
2.157
).
The conformal bootstrap equation for this correlation function is

\left|\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mathbb{I}|z)\right|^{2}+C_{\sigma\sigma\epsilon}^{2}\left|\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon|z)\right|^{2}=\left|\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mathbb{I}|1-z)\right|^{2}+C_{\sigma\sigma\epsilon}^{2}\left|\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon|1-z)\right|^{2}.

(4.8)

Since
\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mathbb{I}|1-z)
and
\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon|1-z)
are also the solutions to the same BPZ equation (
4.5
),
they should be expandable in terms of
\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mathbb{I}|z)
and
\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon|z)
.
There is a transformation corresponding to the change of the basis of the Virasoro blocks as follows,

42
42

42

It can be written in the form of a sum only when the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{p}
is degenerate.
In general, it becomes an integral transformation.
This will be explained later.

Fusion transformation

\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)=\sum_{q}{\mathbb{F}}_{h_{p},h_{q}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}h_{2}&h_{1}\\
h_{3}&h_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]\mathcal{F}^{23}_{14}(h_{q}|1-z).

(4.9)

Such a transformation is called a
fusion transformation
, and the coefficients
{\mathbb{F}}_{h_{p},h_{q}}
are called
fusion matrices
.
The fusion matrices for the Virasoro blocks appearing in the Ising model (
4.6
) can be obtained using the following identities:

\displaystyle\sqrt{1+\sqrt{1-z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\sqrt{1+\sqrt{z}}+\sqrt{1-\sqrt{z}}\right),

(4.10)

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-\sqrt{1-z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\sqrt{1+\sqrt{z}}-\sqrt{1-\sqrt{z}}\right).

From these identities, the fusion transformations are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mathbb{I}|z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mathbb{I}|1-z)+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon|1-z),

(4.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon|z)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2}}\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mathbb{I}|1-z)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon|1-z).

Substituting these into the conformal bootstrap equation (
4.8
) and comparing the coefficients, we obtain

C_{\sigma\sigma\epsilon}=\frac{1}{2}.

(4.12)

Similarly, all the CFT data of the Ising model can be determined by the conformal bootstrap.
For reference, the CFT data of the Ising model are listed in Appendix
A
.

4.1.2
Modular Bootstrap in Minimal Models

The partition function of a minimal model can be written as follows:

Z(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\sum_{(h,\bar{h})\in\mathcal{S}}M_{h,\bar{h}}\chi_{h}(\tau)\bar{\chi}_{\bar{h}}(\bar{\tau}).

(4.13)

An important point is that
\mathcal{S}
is a finite discrete set.
Let us see what can be obtained from this assumption and the modular bootstrap alone.
To consider inequalities, let us set
\tau=-\bar{\tau}\in i\mathbb{R}
.
Recalling that an irreducible Verma module is obtained by removing the invariant subspaces from the original Verma module, we obtain the following inequality:

\chi_{h}(\tau)=q^{h-\frac{c}{24}}\sum_{n\geq 0}d(n)q^{n}\leq q^{h-\frac{c}{24}}\sum_{n\geq 0}p(n)q^{n}.

(4.14)

Here, we set
q\equiv\mathrm{e}^{2\pi i\tau}
.
The right-hand side of this inequality is a formal power series (i.e. generating function) with coefficients representing the number of level
n
descendants that can be constructed from
L_{-1},L_{-2},L_{-3},\cdots
.
As a warm-up, let us consider the generating function for the number of level
n
descendants that can be constructed from only
L_{-k}
. Since there is only one way to construct a level
n
state using
L_{-k}^{m}
when
n=mk\ \ (n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
, we have

1+q^{k}+q^{2k}+q^{3k}+\cdots=\frac{1}{1-q^{k}}.

(4.15)

Similarly, the formal power series with coefficients representing the number of level
n
descendants that can be constructed from
L_{-1},L_{-2},L_{-3},\cdots
is

\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1-q^{k}}=q^{-\frac{1}{24}}\eta(\tau).

(4.16)

Here,
\eta(\tau)
is the Dedekind eta function defined in (
3.55
). Since we are using the notation
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
, the expression differs slightly from (
3.55
). Thus, we obtain

\sum_{n\geq 0}p(n)q^{n}=q^{-\frac{1}{24}}\eta(\tau).

(4.17)

Using these results and the modular bootstrap equation, we obtain the following inequality:

\sum_{(h,\bar{h})\in\mathcal{S}}M_{h,\bar{h}}\chi_{h}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)\bar{\chi}_{\bar{h}}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\eta(\tau)^{2}}\sum_{(h,\bar{h})\in\mathcal{S}}M_{h,\bar{h}}q^{h+\bar{h}-\frac{c-1}{12}}.

(4.18)

Taking the limit
\tau\to i0^{+}
of this inequality,

\beta^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}\left(h_{\text{min}}-\frac{c-1}{24}\right)}\leq\sum_{(h,\bar{h})\in\mathcal{S}}M_{h,\bar{h}}.

(4.19)

Here, for convenience, we introduced the temperature
\beta\equiv 2\pi i\tau\in\mathbb{R}
.

h_{\text{min}}
is the minimum conformal dimension appearing in the spectrum.
We also used the property of the Dedekind eta function,
\eta\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\eta(\tau)
.
Since the assumption that the number of irreducible representations appearing in the spectrum is finite implies that the right-hand side is finite, we obtain

h_{\text{min}}>\frac{c-1}{24}.

(4.20)

In particular, assuming that the theory has a vacuum state, we have
h_{\text{min}}\leq 0
, thus
c<1
.

Next, we show that (
4.20
) is satisfied only when
c=1-6\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{pq}
.
Using the Virasoro algebra, we can obtain the following lemma about the Virasoro character:

\chi_{h}(\tau)\geq\frac{q^{h-\frac{c-1}{24}}}{\eta(\tau)}(1-q),\ \ \ \ \ \text{if }c\neq 1-6\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{pq}.

(4.21)

From this inequality and the modular bootstrap equation in the limit
\tau\to i0
, we obtain

\beta^{-3}\mathrm{e}^{-2\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}\left(h_{\text{min}}-\frac{c-1}{24}\right)}\geq\sum_{(h,\bar{h})\in\mathcal{S}}M_{h,\bar{h}}.

(4.22)

Consequently, we obtain

h_{\text{min}}<\frac{c-1}{24},

(4.23)

which contradicts (
4.20
).
Thus, from the modular bootstrap, we obtain the following conclusion:

When the spectrum is composed of a finite number of irreducible representations of the Virasoro algebra,
c<1
is always satisfied, and in particular, the central charge takes the following discrete values:

c=1-6\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{pq},\ \ \ \ \ p>q\in\{2,3,\cdots\}.

(4.24)

4.1.3
Comments on Minimal Models

Finally, here are some comments on minimal models.

•

Minimal models are unitary only when
\left|p-q\right|=1
.
This can be confirmed as follows.
Substituting the central charge expression for minimal models into (
4.3
), we obtain

h_{rs}=\frac{(pr-qs)^{2}-(p-q)^{2}}{4pq}.

(4.25)

By Bézout’s identity, for coprime integers
p
and
q
, there exist integers
x
and
y
that satisfy the following,

xp+yq=1.

(4.26)

Therefore, for the theory to be unitary, the following inequality must be satisfied,

h_{\mathrm{min}}=h_{xy}=\frac{1-(p-q)^{2}}{4pq}\geq 0.

(4.27)

This is satisfied only when
\left|p-q\right|=1
.

•

Specifying
p,q
is enough to specify the chiral algebra, but it is not enough to specify the full CFT. From modular invariance, one can classify all possible minimal model CFTs. This classification is known as the
ADE classification
:

–

A
-series: the diagonal modular invariant, which exists for any coprime integers
p,q\geq 2
.

–

D
-series: the non-diagonal modular invariant, which exists for any even
q\geq 6
and odd
p
(or vice versa), with
p,q
coprime.

–

E
-series: the exceptional (non-diagonal) modular invariant, which exists for
p
or
q=12,18,30

Note that if we assume the theory to be unitary, the
A
-series always exists; the
D
-series exists for all values of
c<1
except for
c=\frac{1}{2},\frac{7}{10}
; and the E-series only exists for
(p,p+1)=(11,12),(12,13),(17,18),(18,19),(29,30),(30,31)
. For unitary minimal models, the first
D
-series minimal model is the three-states Potts model, which occurs at
c=\frac{4}{5}
.

•

The
A
-series minimal models are believed to be the IR fixed points under RG flow of the relevant deformation
\phi^{2m}
of the
c=1
free theory
[
24
]
. By now there is strong evidence for this conjecture. For example for a supersymmetric generalization of this flow, there is strong evidence in
[
25
]
. There is even a very recent generalizations to RG flow of nonunitary theories in
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
.

•

The fewer the number of irreducible representations included in the spectrum,
the easier it is to compute physical quantities.
Therefore, when investigating the behavior of a certain physical quantity,
one often starts by examining minimal models (especially the Ising model).

•

On the other extreme, the
p\rightarrow\infty
limit for unitary minimal models is subtle and interesting. The central charge approaches
1
from below. The spectrum approaches that of Liouville CFT, which we will discuss in the next spectrum. However, the three point functions behave differently, and were studied by Runkel and Watts
[
29
]
. Moreover, even though at any finite
p
, the theory is completely rational, the spectrum exhibits some features of chaotic CFTs in what is known as the spectral form factor
[
30
]
. One piece of intuition for this is that the number of primary operators becomes very large, so the rationality of the conformal dimensions because harder to see at large
p
.

4.2
Liouville CFT

In Liouville CFT, it is convenient to use the following notation,

c=1+6Q^{2},\ \ \ \ \ Q=b+\frac{1}{b},\ \ \ \ \ h_{i}={\alpha}_{i}(Q-{\alpha}_{i}).

(4.28)

Here,
Q
is called the
background charge
,
b
is the
Liouville coupling
, and
\alpha_{i}
is the
Liouville momentum
.

Central Charge

Liouville CFT can be defined for any complex number
c\in\mathbb{C}
.
In particular, when the central charge is a real number
c>1
, Liouville CFT is a unitary CFT.

OPE Coefficients

The OPE coefficients of Liouville CFT are known as the DOZZ formula
[
31
,
32
]
,

C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}=\frac{\left(b^{\frac{2}{b}-2b}\lambda\right)^{Q-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}}\Upsilon_{b}^{\prime}(0)\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha_{2})\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha_{3})}{\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-Q)\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}.

(4.29)

Here,
\lambda
is an arbitrary normalization constant, and
\Upsilon_{b}(z)
is defined using the Barnes double Gamma function
\Gamma_{b}(x)

(see (
B.5
) in Appendix
B.2
) as follows:

43
43

43

Using (
B.6
), it can be shown that

\Upsilon_{b}^{\prime}(0)=\frac{2\pi}{\Gamma_{b}(Q)^{2}}.

(4.30)

Some references rewrite the DOZZ formula using this.

\Upsilon_{b}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{\Gamma_{b}(x)\Gamma_{b}(Q-x)}.

(4.31)

By definition,
\Upsilon_{b}(x)
has the following reflection symmetry:

\Upsilon_{b}(x)=\Upsilon_{b}(Q-x).

(4.32)

An important feature of the function
\Upsilon_{b}(x)
is the following:

\Upsilon_{b}(x)=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for }x\in\left(-b\mathbb{N}-\frac{1}{b}\mathbb{N}\right)\cup\left(Q+b\mathbb{N}+\frac{1}{b}\mathbb{N}\right).

(4.33)

In particular, these zeros are simple zeros (of order one).
Reflecting these zeros, the DOZZ formula also exhibits simple zeros and poles.

As stated in (
2.157
), the solution to the conformal bootstrap has an ambiguity up to a multiplicative constant.
In Liouville CFT, the following normalization is adopted:

\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}=Q}C_{123}=1.

(4.34)

Adopting the normalization differs from (
2.157
) because Liouville CFT has a continuous spectrum, as explained below.

Normalization

The two-point function can be defined from the
\alpha_{2}\to 0
limit of the DOZZ formula.
Expanding the DOZZ formula around
\alpha_{2}=\epsilon\ll 1
gives

C_{\alpha_{1},\epsilon,\alpha_{3}}=\frac{2\epsilon B_{\alpha_{1}}}{(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{3}+\epsilon)(\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1}+\epsilon)}+\frac{2\epsilon}{(Q-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{3}+\epsilon)(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{3}-Q+\epsilon)}.

(4.35)

Here,
B_{\alpha}
is defined as:

B_{\alpha}\equiv\left(b^{\frac{2}{b}-2b}\lambda\right)^{Q-2\alpha}\frac{\Upsilon_{b}(Q-2\alpha)}{\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha-Q)}.

(4.36)

From this expression,
C_{\alpha_{1},\epsilon,\alpha_{3}}
diverges in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
when
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}
or
\alpha_{3}=Q-\alpha_{1}
,
and vanishes otherwise.
Therefore, the two-point function is defined only in the distributional sense.

We consider defining the norm on the Hilbert space of Liouville CFT.
First, from the DOZZ formula, which satisfies
\left(C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}\right)^{*}=C_{\alpha_{1}^{*},\alpha_{2}^{*},\alpha_{3}^{*}}
,
the Hermitian conjugate of the fields in Liouville CFT is given by:

[V_{\alpha_{i}}(z,\bar{z})]^{\dagger}=\bar{z}^{-2h_{i}}z^{-2h_{i}}V_{\alpha_{i}^{*}}(1/\bar{z},1/z).

(4.37)

Thus, the inner product should be defined by

\braket{\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{j}}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}C_{\alpha_{i}^{*},\epsilon,\alpha_{j}}.

(4.38)

From the positivity of the norm, it follows that
\alpha\in\frac{Q}{2}+i\mathbb{R}_{+}
or
\alpha\in[0,\frac{Q}{2})
.
The upper limit
\frac{Q}{2}
in the second case is imposed using the following reflection formula for the DOZZ formula:

C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}=B_{\alpha_{1}}C_{Q-\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}.

(4.39)

The restriction
\alpha\in\frac{Q}{2}+i\mathbb{R}_{+}
or
\alpha\in[0,\frac{Q}{2})
on the Liouville momentum can also be understood from the reality of the conformal dimension
h_{i}=\alpha_{i}(Q-\alpha_{i})
.
Furthermore, when defining the scalar product using (
4.35
) in the distributional sense,
it is necessary to take
\Im\epsilon\neq 0
so that the regularization parameter
\epsilon
does not lie on the integration contour
\alpha\in[0,\frac{Q}{2})
.
This breaks the positivity of the norm, resulting in the inability to define well-defined states for
\alpha\in[0,\frac{Q}{2})
.
Finally, the spectrum of Liouville CFT is given as follows:

Spectrum

\mathcal{S}=\int_{\frac{Q}{2}+i0}^{\frac{Q}{2}+i\infty}d\alpha\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{V}}_{\alpha}.

(4.40)

In terms of conformal dimension,
\alpha\in\frac{Q}{2}+i\mathbb{R}_{+}
corresponds to

h\in\frac{c-1}{24}+\mathbb{R}_{+}.

(4.41)

This spectrum has the following important features:

•

continuous spectrum

•

irrational CFT

•

diagonal CFT

Lagrangian Formulation

Liouville CFT can be described by the following local action,

S[\phi]=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int d^{2}x\sqrt{g}\left(g^{\mu\nu}{\partial}_{\mu}\phi{\partial}_{\nu}\phi+QR\phi+\lambda^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{2b\phi}\right).

(4.42)

Here,
R
is the Ricci scalar.

Q
and
b
are the same as those that appeared in the notation of Liouville CFT (
4.28
).

\lambda^{\prime}
is related to
\lambda
, which appears in the OPE coefficient, as follows.

\lambda^{\prime}=4\frac{\Gamma(1-b^{2})}{\Gamma(b^{2})}\lambda^{b}.

(4.43)

The coupling constant is sometimes expressed as
\lambda^{\prime}=4\pi\mu
(e.g.
[
33
]
).
A primary field with conformal dimension
h=\alpha(Q-\alpha)
can be expressed using the Liouville field,

V_{\alpha}\equiv\mathrm{e}^{2\alpha\phi}.

(4.44)

Correlation functions are symmetric under the following transformations:

•

b\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{b}
,

•

\alpha\leftrightarrow Q-\alpha
.

While this is obvious from the CFT perspective,
it is non-trivial in the Lagrangian formulation.

4.2.1
Conformal Bootstrap in Liouville CFT

An important assumption in solving the conformal bootstrap in Liouville CFT is that it is a diagonal CFT.
In general, the spectrum is
(h,\bar{h})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
, but in the case of a diagonal CFT, there is only one degree of freedom
h\in\mathbb{C}
,
which significantly reduces the number of equations needed to determine the CFT data.
In fact, the CFT data of a diagonal CFT can be uniquely determined from the conformal bootstrap of the four-point function
[
34
]
.
Let us see this in detail below.

When solving the conformal bootstrap, it is sometimes convenient to use the fusion transformation.
To see this, let us rewrite the conformal bootstrap equation using the fusion transformation.
When the Verma module is originally irreducible, the fusion transformation is expressed as an integral transformation
[
35
,
36
,
33
]
,

Fusion transformation

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{{\alpha}_{s}}|z)=\int_{\frac{Q}{2}+i0}^{\frac{Q}{2}+i\infty}d{\alpha}_{t}{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{s},{\alpha}_{t}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]\mathcal{F}^{23}_{14}(h_{{\alpha}_{t}}|1-z).

(4.45)

The details of the fusion matrix
{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{s},{\alpha}_{t}}
are described in Appendix
D
.
Using this fusion transformation, the conformal bootstrap can be rewritten as follows,

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{Q}{2}+i0}^{\frac{Q}{2}+i\infty}d{\alpha}_{p}\frac{C_{12p}C_{34p}}{B_{p}}{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{p},{\alpha}_{q}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(p|z)\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{23}_{14}(p|1-{\bar{z}})

(4.46)

\displaystyle=\int_{\frac{Q}{2}+i0}^{\frac{Q}{2}+i\infty}d\alpha_{q}\frac{C_{14q}C_{23q}}{B_{q}}{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{q},{\alpha}_{p}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{3}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(q|z)\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{23}_{14}(q|1-{\bar{z}}).

Now, since both sides are expanded in the same functional basis, by comparing the coefficients, we obtain the following relation,

\frac{C_{12p}C_{34p}}{B_{p}}{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{p},{\alpha}_{q}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]=\frac{C_{14q}C_{23q}}{B_{q}}{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{q},{\alpha}_{p}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{3}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right].

(4.47)

This is equivalent to the conformal bootstrap equation, but since the fusion matrix is somewhat simpler than the Virasoro block, this expression can sometimes be easier to handle.

Figure 15:
Illustration of the derivation of the Pentagon identity using the dual graph representation of the Virasoro block.
