There are two distinct ways to transform the block from the blue graph to the red graph using the fusion transformation, as shown in the figure.
The Pentagon identity is obtained from the requirement that these two multiple fusion transformations must be equivalent.

Repeating the fusion transformation multiple times can return to the original Virasoro block, and the consistency of this fusion transformation leads to the following
pentagon identity
(See Figure
15
),

\int_{\frac{Q}{2}+i0}^{\frac{Q}{2}+i\infty}d\alpha_{s}{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{q},{\alpha}_{s}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{k}&{\alpha}_{b}\\
{\alpha}_{j}&{\alpha}_{p}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{p},{\alpha}_{l}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{s}&{\alpha}_{b}\\
{\alpha}_{i}&{\alpha}_{a}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{s},{\alpha}_{r}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{j}&{\alpha}_{k}\\
{\alpha}_{i}&{\alpha}_{l}\\
\end{array}\right]={\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{p},{\alpha}_{r}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{j}&{\alpha}_{q}\\
{\alpha}_{i}&{\alpha}_{a}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{q},{\alpha}_{l}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{k}&{\alpha}_{b}\\
{\alpha}_{r}&{\alpha}_{a}\\
\end{array}\right].

(4.48)

To relate this to the fusion matrix appearing on the left-hand side of (
4.47
), we perform the following relabeling,

(a,b,q,r,i,j,k,l)\to(3,p,2,q,3,2,1,4).

(4.49)

Furthermore, setting
p=\mathbb{I}
, we can show

{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{2},{\alpha}_{s}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{p}\\
{\alpha}_{2}&0\\
\end{array}\right]=\delta_{\alpha_{s},\alpha_{p}}.

(4.50)

Substituting this into the pentagon identity allows us to remove the integral over
\alpha_{s}
,

{\mathbb{F}}_{0,{\alpha}_{4}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{p}&{\alpha}_{p}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{3}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{p},{\alpha}_{q}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]={\mathbb{F}}_{0,{\alpha}_{q}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{3}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{2},{\alpha}_{4}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{p}\\
{\alpha}_{q}&{\alpha}_{3}\\
\end{array}\right].

(4.51)

Furthermore, to relate this to the fusion matrix appearing on the right-hand side of (
4.47
),
we consider the relabeling
p\leftrightarrow q
and
1\leftrightarrow 3
in (
4.51
),

{\mathbb{F}}_{0,{\alpha}_{4}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{q}&{\alpha}_{q}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{q},{\alpha}_{p}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{4}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
\end{array}\right]={\mathbb{F}}_{0,{\alpha}_{p}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{2},{\alpha}_{4}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{p}\\
{\alpha}_{q}&{\alpha}_{3}\\
\end{array}\right].

(4.52)

Here, we used the fact that the fusion matrix is symmetric under the following permutations,

{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{p},{\alpha}_{q}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]={\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{p},{\alpha}_{q}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
{\alpha}_{4}&{\alpha}_{3}\\
\end{array}\right]={\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{p},{\alpha}_{q}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right].

(4.53)

Finally, since (
4.51
) and (
4.52
) share the common factor

{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{2},{\alpha}_{4}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{p}\\
{\alpha}_{q}&{\alpha}_{3}\\
\end{array}\right],

we obtain

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathbb{F}}_{0,{\alpha}_{4}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{p}&{\alpha}_{p}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{3}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{0,{\alpha}_{p}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{p},{\alpha}_{q}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]

(4.54)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{F}}_{0,{\alpha}_{4}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{q}&{\alpha}_{q}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{0,{\alpha}_{q}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{3}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{q},{\alpha}_{p}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{4}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
\end{array}\right].

We find that this is the solution to (
4.47
).
The fusion matrix is generally complicated, but in this case, we can use the following result,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{F}}_{0,{\alpha}_{t}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
{\alpha}_{4}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{{\alpha}_{s}=0}\lim_{{\alpha}_{2}={\alpha}_{1}}\lim_{{\alpha}_{3}={\alpha}_{4}}{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{s},{\alpha}_{t}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]

(4.55)

\displaystyle\propto\frac{1}{\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{t}-Q)\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{4}-\alpha_{t})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{t}-\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{t}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{4}).}

Therefore, the solution to the conformal bootstrap equation for a diagonal CFT without degenerate fields is

C_{123}\propto\frac{1}{\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-Q)\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}).}.

(4.56)

The conformal bootstrap equation is invariant under field redefinitions, so the ambiguity due to normalization constants cannot be removed.
Moreover, since the spectrum of Liouville CFT does not contain the vacuum state, the ambiguity cannot be removed by the standard normalization (
2.157
).
Instead, we adopt the following normalization,

\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}=Q}C_{123}=1.

(4.57)

Under this normalization, the OPE coefficient can be written as follows,

DOZZ formula

C_{123}=\frac{\left(b^{\frac{2}{b}-2b}\lambda\right)^{Q-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}}\Upsilon_{b}^{\prime}(0)\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha_{2})\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha_{3})}{\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-Q)\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}.

(4.58)

This is the DOZZ formula (
4.29
).
The overall factor
\left(b^{\frac{2}{b}-2b}\lambda\right)^{Q-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}}
comes from the convention in the Lagrangian formulation.

4.2.2
Comments on Liouville CFT

•

Liouville CFT is often studied as an example of a solvable ICFT.
However, it has unique properties that differ from typical physical systems, such as having a continuous spectrum and the Hilbert space not containing the vacuum state.

•

Since the central charge can be arbitrary and the conformal dimensions are continuous,
Liouville CFT correlation functions include conformal blocks over a very wide range of parameters.
Therefore, the correlation functions of Liouville CFT are sometimes used to study the properties of Virasoro blocks.

•

The Hilbert space of Liouville CFT does not contain the vacuum state in the standard sense.
However, appropriately restricting the dual space makes it possible to ”define” a vacuum state.
Once the vacuum state is defined in this way, the state-operator correspondence can also be established.
Some references describe this as ”the state-operator correspondence is broken in Liouville CFT”, in the sense that the relationship between eigenstates in the Hilbert space and local operators differs slightly from the standard interpretation.
For more details on the state-operator correspondence in Liouville CFT, see
[
33
]
.

4.3
Free Boson CFT

A free boson CFT is a CFT without interactions. It is often defined using a Lagrangian, but it can also be defined without a Lagrangian as a CFT with
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
symmetry.

Central Charge

A free boson CFT can be defined with any complex central charge
c
. However, the most commonly considered case is the
c=1
free boson CFT. When
c=1
, it has the feature that it can be compactified on a circle of any radius
S^{1}
.

Lagrangian Formulation

A free boson CFT can be described by the following local action:

S[\phi]=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int d^{2}x\sqrt{g}\left(g^{\mu\nu}{\partial}_{\mu}\phi{\partial}_{\nu}\phi+QR\phi\right).

(4.59)

Here,
R
is the Ricci scalar, and
Q\in\mathbb{C}
is the background charge. The primary fields can be expressed using the bosonic field
\phi
as

V_{\alpha}=\mathrm{e}^{2\alpha\phi}.

(4.60)

OPE

Due to the affine
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
symmetry, the momentum
\alpha
is conserved. This imposes a strong constraint on the OPE:

V_{\alpha_{1},\bar{\alpha}_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\alpha_{2},\bar{\alpha}_{2}}(z_{2})=\left|(z_{1}-z_{2})^{h_{3}-h_{1}-h_{2}}\right|^{2}V_{\alpha_{3},\bar{\alpha}_{3}}(z_{2})+(\text{descendants}).

(4.61)

Here,
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
. In general, various primary fields can appear in the OPE, but in the free boson CFT, the additive property of momentum ensures that only one primary field appears. This non-interacting nature is why it is called a “free” boson CFT. Thanks to the simplification of the OPE, the correlation functions of the free boson CFT can be calculated very easily. This systematic calculation method is known as
Wick’s theorem
.

Spectrum

Several different spectra are allowed for the free boson CFT. These will be explained in detail in Section
4.3.2
.

4.3.1
Affine Lie Algebra

A characteristic feature of the free boson CFT is the presence of the following conserved currents:

J={\partial}\phi,\ \ \ \ \ \ \bar{J}=\bar{{\partial}}\phi.

(4.62)

These currents satisfy the conservation laws:

{\partial}\bar{J}=\bar{{\partial}}J=0.

(4.63)

These conserved currents generate the abelian affine Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
.
The OPE of the conserved currents is

J(z)J(w)=-\frac{1}{2(z-w)^{2}}.

(4.64)

Expressing
J(z)
in terms of its modes,

J(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{J_{n}}{z^{n+1}},

(4.65)

we obtain the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
commutation relations:

[J_{n},J_{m}]=\frac{1}{2}n\delta_{n+m,0}.

(4.66)

From this
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
algebra, we can construct the Virasoro algebra as a subalgebra:

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}J_{n-m}J_{m}+Q(n+1)J_{n},\ \ \ \ n\neq 0,

(4.67)

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}J_{-m}J_{m}-J_{0}^{2}+QJ_{0}.

These satisfy the Virasoro commutation relations with
c=1+6Q^{2}
.
The commutation relation between the Virasoro algebra and the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
algebra is

[L_{n},J_{m}]=-nJ_{n+m}-\frac{Q}{2}n(n+1)\delta_{n+m,0}.

(4.68)

Let us construct the energy-momentum tensor from the Virasoro algebra,

T(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{L_{n}}{z^{n+2}}=-(JJ)(z)-Q{\partial}J(z).

(4.69)

Here,
(AB)(z)
denotes the normal ordered product, defined as

(AB)(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{z}\frac{dw}{w-z}A(w)B(z).

(4.70)

This energy-momentum tensor matches the one obtained from the action (
4.59
).
When a CFT has affine Lie algebra symmetry, it is often more convenient to consider the irreducible representations of the affine Lie algebra instead of the Virasoro algebra. The irreducible representation decomposition of the affine Lie algebra introduces the concept of
affine primary states
,

J_{n>0}\ket{\alpha}=0,\ \ \ \ \ J_{0}\ket{\alpha}=\alpha\ket{\alpha}.

(4.71)

Since the Virasoro algebra is a subalgebra of the affine Lie algebra, affine primary fields are also (Virasoro) primary fields. Conversely, (Virasoro) primary fields are not necessarily affine primary fields. From the affine primary fields, we can define affine descendant fields similarly to the (Virasoro) descendant fields. To distinguish the Verma module
\mathcal{V}
of the Virasoro algebra, we denote the Verma module composed of the affine primary field
V_{\alpha}
and its descendant fields as
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
.

Rewriting in terms of OPE,
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
primary field are defined as follows,

J(z)V_{\alpha}(w)=\frac{\alpha V_{\alpha}}{z-w}.

(4.72)

The (local)
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
Ward-Takahashi identity for the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
primary fields is given by,

\braket{J(z)\prod_{i}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\alpha_{i}}{z-z_{i}}\braket{\prod_{i}^{N}V_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i})}.

(4.73)

Moreover, from the global Ward-Takahashi identity, we obtain the charge conservation law,

\sum_{i}^{N}\alpha_{i}=Q.

(4.74)

Consider the following general form of OPE,

V_{\alpha_{1},\bar{\alpha}_{1}}(z_{1})V_{\alpha_{2},\bar{\alpha}_{2}}(z_{2})=\sum_{p}C_{12p}\left|(z_{1}-z_{2})^{h_{p}-h_{1}-h_{2}}\right|^{2}V_{\alpha_{p},\bar{\alpha}_{p}}(z_{2})+(\text{descendants}).

(4.75)

By inserting
J(z)
on both sides of this OPE and using the Ward-Takahashi identity, we obtain the following selection rule,

\alpha_{p}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}.

(4.76)

In other words, the OPE of the free boson CFT has additivity (
4.61
).

Similar to Section
2
, various theorems can be derived from the representation theory of affine Lie algebras,
but explaining them would require a vast number of pages and would deviate from the purpose of these lecture notes,
so the details are left to textbooks, such as the Yellow book
[
1
]
.

4.3.2
Spectrum

Define the free boson CFT as a CFT with
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
symmetry.
Due to the additivity of the OPE, when there exists a primary field
\bm{\alpha}=(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,

n\bm{\alpha}\ \ (n\in\mathbb{N})
must also exist in the spectrum.
Moreover, all these primary fields must satisfy the integrality of spin,

h-\bar{h}=(\alpha-\bar{\alpha})(Q-\alpha-\bar{\alpha})\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.77)

This condition imposes strong constraints on the spectrum of the free boson CFT
[
34
]
.

As a result, the possible spectrum is as follows:

non-compactified free boson

\mathcal{S}=\int_{Q\mathbb{R}}d\alpha\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}.

(4.78)

The integral contour is in the same direction as
Q\in\mathbb{C}
.
When
Q=0
, the integral contour can be any straight line with an arbitrary angle.
However, it is unitary only when
Q=0
and the integral contour is along the imaginary axis.
As seen from the spectrum, the non-compactified boson is an ICFT,
especially possessing a continuous spectrum.

compactified free boson

\mathcal{S}_{R}=\bigoplus_{(n,w)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathcal{U}_{\frac{i}{2}\left(\frac{n}{R}+Rw\right)}\otimes\bar{\mathcal{U}}_{\frac{i}{2}\left(\frac{n}{R}-Rw\right)}.

(4.79)

When
Q\neq 0
, the spin generally does not satisfy the integer condition (
4.77
).

44
44

44

In fact, even when
Q\neq 0
, it is possible to satisfy the integer spin condition by choosing a special value for
R\in\mathbb{C}
.
This means that the compactification is possible even for
c\neq 1
.
For readers interested in the details, please refer to
[
34
]
.

Therefore, this spectrum is only possible when
Q=0
.
This spectrum is parameterized by
R\in\mathbb{C}
.
In particular, when
R\in\mathbb{R}
, it becomes a unitary theory.
This spectrum has a symmetry
R\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{R}
, which is called the
T
-duality
.

This theory is called the
compactified boson
.
Starting from the Lagrangian formulation,
consider the canonical quantization of the free boson on a cylinder of period
2\pi
.
Let us identify
\phi
with
\phi+2\pi R
(compactification to
S^{1}
with radius
R
).
In this case, the following periodic boundary condition can be imposed on the free boson field,

\phi(x+2\pi,t)=\phi(x,t)+2\pi wR,\ \ \ \ w\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.80)

Since this represents how many times the free boson wraps around
S^{1}_{R}
,

w
is called the
winding number
.
Imposing periodic boundary conditions quantizes the momentum, which corresponds to the label
n
.
The spectrum of this compactified boson CFT exactly matches (
4.79
).
For details, refer to Section 6.3.5 of the Yellow book
[
1
]
.

4.3.3
Comments on Free Boson CFT

•

The most significant feature of the free boson CFT is the absence of interactions.
In other words, the OPE has a very simple structure as shown in (
4.61
).
Thanks to this simplification, various physical quantities can be easily calculated in the free boson CFT.
For these reasons, when discovering a property expected to hold in any CFT, one often starts by verifying whether it holds in the free boson CFT.

•

There is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
outer automorphism (
J\to-J
) in
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
.
By gauging this
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry, a new CFT can be constructed.
In general, CFTs obtained by gauging (also called orbifolding) are called
orbifold CFTs
.
For details, refer to Section 17.B of the Yellow book
[
1
]
.

•

As a generalization of the
S^{1}
compactification of the free boson CFT,
one can consider the
T^{N}
compactification of
N
copies of free boson CFTs.
This series of CFTs parameterized by the moduli of the
N
-dimensional torus
T^{N}
is called the
Narain CFT
.
Due to the discovery that the “averaged” CFT of Narain CFT over the moduli is equivalent to
U(1)
Chern-Simons gravity,
it is also actively studied in the context of AdS/CFT correspondence
[
37
,
38
,
39
]
.

•

When
R^{2}\in\mathbb{Q}
, it is known that the
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
symmetry is enlarged (see Section 14.4.4 of the Yellow book
[
1
]
).
Considering the representations of this extended
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
symmetry, it can be shown that they can be described by a finite number of irreducible representations.
In other words, when
R^{2}\in\mathbb{Q}
, the compactified boson CFT becomes an RCFT.
Based on this fact, there are studies examining how certain physical quantities change during the process of changing the radius
R
(called exactly marginal deformation),
to compare the behavior of physical quantities in ICFT and RCFT.

4.4
Wess-Zumino-Witten Model

We have stated that the free boson CFT is a CFT with
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
symmetry,
but more generally, we can consider a CFT with level
k
affine Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
symmetry,

[J^{a}_{n},J^{b}_{m}]=\sum_{c}if_{abc}J^{c}_{n+m}+kn\delta_{ab}\delta_{n+m,0},

(4.81)

where
J^{a}_{n}
are the
n
-th mode of the current.
By generalizing
\hat{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}
to non-abelian, new terms with non-trivial structure constants
f_{abc}
appear.
The energy-momentum tensor can be constructed in almost the same way as in the case of the free boson,

T(z)\sim(JJ)(z).

(4.82)

This is called the
Sugawara construction
.
It can be shown that this involves a Virasoro algebra as a subalgebra.
The CFT constructed from this energy-momentum tensor is called
\hat{G}_{k}

Wess-Zumino-Witten (WZW) model
.
The WZW model consists of a finite number of
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
affine primary fields,
and hence is an RCFT.
However, note that while the representation of the affine Lie symmetry is described by a finite number of irreducible representations,
it does not mean that the representation of the Virasoro symmetry is described by a finite number of irreducible representations.
In general, there are infinitely many Virasoro primary fields in the Hilbert space of the WZW model.

Properly defining the WZW model requires an explanation of the affine Lie algebra,
but as this would deviate from the purpose of this lecture, it is not introduced here.
Interested readers should refer to Chapter 15 of the Yellow book
[
1
]
.

4.5
RCFT and ICFT

For an irreducible representation
R_{i}
of the maximal current algebra
\mathcal{A}
of a CFT, the character is defined as follows,

\chi_{i}(\tau)\equiv{\text{tr}}_{R_{i}}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}.

(4.83)

In particular, when the maximal current algebra is the Virasoro algebra, this is the Virasoro character.
The partition function can be written as

Z(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\sum_{i,\bar{i}}M_{i,\bar{i}}\chi_{i}(\tau)\bar{\chi}_{\bar{i}}(\bar{\tau}).

(4.84)

When this partition function can be written as a finite sum, the CFT is called an
RCFT
.
All CFTs other than RCFT are called
ICFT
.

Below are some comments on RCFT and ICFT.

•

The central charge and conformal dimensions of an RCFT are always rational numbers
[
40
]
.

45
45

45

It is not known whether the converse is true (as far as the author knows).

•

A complete classification of RCFTs is not known.
The RCFTs known so far are of two types:

–

Coset construction, gauging, and tensor products of WZW models

–

Rational points on the moduli space under exactly marginal deformations (such as Narain CFT)

•

The proper construction of ICFTs is more difficult compared to that of RCFTs.
Examples of exactly constructed ICFTs include the free boson CFT and Liouville CFT introduced in this section.

•

The CFTs appearing in the AdS/CFT correspondence are ICFTs.
In particular, the CFT dual to the simplest gravity without matter is considered to be a unitary compact ICFT (i.e. a unitary CFT with a discrete spectrum and the vacuum state) with only Virasoro symmetry.
This ICFT will be explained in the following sections.

5
AdS/CFT Correspondence

This section will explain the AdS/CFT correspondence.
Since this lecture does not assume specialized knowledge of general relativity,
only the claims directly related to this lecture will be addressed.

5.1
Anti-de Sitter Space

AdS spacetime is a solution to the Einstein equation with a negative cosmological constant and the maximum symmetry.

46
46

46

Having the maximum symmetry means that the number of independent Killing vectors is the maximum possible, that is, when the dimension of the space is
d
, there are
\frac{d(d+1)}{2}
Killing vectors.
Intuitively, this means there are
d
Killing vectors generating translations in the
d
directions and
\frac{d(d-1)}{2}
Killing vectors generating rotations around a point, totaling
\frac{d(d+1)}{2}
.
A familiar example is the symmetry of Minkowski space.
From the point of view of the hyperboloid (
5.2
), it is clear that AdS
d
has the symmetry
\mathrm{SO}(2,d-1)
.
Therefore, the number of independent Killing vectors is
\frac{d(d+1)}{2}
, the same as the number of its generators, confirming that AdS
d
indeed has the maximum symmetry.

There are multiple ways to represent AdS
d+1
spacetime.
The simplest introduction is to realize it as a hyperboloid embedded in the space
\mathbb{R}^{2,d}
,

ds^{2}=-{dX_{0}}^{2}-{X_{d+1}}^{2}+{dX_{1}}^{2}+{dX_{2}}^{2}+\cdots+{dX_{d}}^{2}.

(5.1)

The hyperboloid is given by

{X_{0}}^{2}+{X_{d+1}}^{2}={X_{1}}^{2}+{X_{2}}^{2}+\cdots+{X_{d}}^{2}+l^{2},

(5.2)

where
l
is called the
AdS radius
.
To solve the constraint (
5.2
), the following coordinates are useful,

\displaystyle X_{0}

\displaystyle=l\cosh\rho\cos t,

(5.3)

\displaystyle X_{d+1}

\displaystyle=l\cosh\rho\sin t,

\displaystyle X_{k}

\displaystyle=l\sinh\rho\Omega_{k},

where
\Omega_{k}
represents the coordinates of the
d-1
dimensional unit sphere.
The metric of AdS
d+1
can be expressed as follows,

ds^{2}=l^{2}(-\cosh^{2}\rho dt^{2}+d\rho^{2}+\sinh^{2}\rho{d\Omega_{d-1}}^{2}).

(5.4)

This coordinate system covers a wider range
t\in[-\infty,\infty]
than the original hyperbolic space
t\in[0,2\pi]
, hence it is called the
global coordinates
.
As a metric representing the global coordinates, the following metric is also often used,

ds^{2}=-\left(1+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)d\tau^{2}+\frac{dr^{2}}{\left(1+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)}+r^{2}{d\Omega_{d-1}}^{2}.

(5.5)

This metric can be obtained by setting
r=l\sinh\rho
and
\tau=lt
.

In the context of AdS/CFT, it is sometimes convenient to use the following
Poincaré coordinates
,

ds^{2}=l^{2}\left(\frac{dz^{2}-dt^{2}+d\bm{x}^{2}}{z^{2}}\right).

(5.6)

This can be obtained from the following coordinate transformation,

\displaystyle X_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{z}{2}\left(1+\frac{l^{2}+\bm{x}^{2}-x_{0}^{2}}{z^{2}}\right),

(5.7)

\displaystyle X_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{lx_{i}}{z},

\displaystyle X_{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{z}{2}\left(1-\frac{l^{2}-\bm{x}^{2}+x_{0}^{2}}{z^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle X_{d+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{lx_{0}}{z}.

This coordinate system has an asymptotic boundary at
z=0
.
Therefore, one considers either
z>0
or
z<0
.
If we take
z>0
,

\frac{X_{0}-X_{d}}{l^{2}}=\frac{1}{z}>0\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \cos t>\Omega_{d-1}\tanh\rho.

(5.8)

Thus, the Poincaré coordinates cover only a part of the global coordinates.

5.2
AdS/CFT Correspondence

The AdS/CFT correspondence refers to the following conjecture
[
41
,
42
,
43
]
,

AdS/CFT

Quantum gravity on AdS
d+1
is equivalent to CFT
d
.

Since a CFT is a field theory that can be treated non-perturbatively,
the AdS/CFT correspondence can be regarded as a non-perturbative formulation of quantum gravity.

Evidence of the AdS/CFT correspondence comes from the fact that both share the same symmetry
\mathrm{SO}(2,d)
.
In this section, by the detailed comparison between AdS and CFT, we will see how the AdS/CFT correspondence indeed works nicely.

5.2.1
Virasoro Symmetry

Specifically, let us focus on AdS
3
/CFT
2
and see if the Virasoro symmetry can be reproduced from the gravity side.
Three-dimensional gravity with a negative cosmological constant is described by the following action:

S=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{N}}\int_{M}dx^{3}\ \sqrt{g}\left(R+\frac{2}{l^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{8\pi G_{N}}\int_{{\partial}M}dx^{2}\ \sqrt{h}\left(K-\frac{1}{l}\right).

(5.9)

The first term is the Einstein-Hilbert action and the second term is the Gibbons-Hawking term on the asymptotic boundary.

G_{N}
is the Newton gravitational constant,
g
is the metric, and
R
is the scalar curvature.

h
is the induced metric on the boundary
{\partial}M
of
M
.

K\equiv h^{ab}K_{ab}
is defined as the trace of the extrinsic curvature
K_{ab}
, which is given by the following definition:

47
47

47

Using Gaussian normal coordinates

ds^{2}=dr^{2}+h_{ab}dx^{a}dx^{b},

(5.10)

the extrinsic curvature can be expressed in a simple form as:

K_{ab}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial h_{ab}}{\partial r}.

(5.11)

K_{ab}\equiv\nabla_{a}n_{b}.

(5.12)

Here,
n
denotes a unit vector normal to
\partial M
.
This action, which does not include any matter, is the simplest AdS gravity and is called the
pure AdS
.
The energy-momentum tensor for this action is defined as follows
[
44
]
:

T^{ij}\equiv-\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{h}}\frac{\delta S}{\delta h_{ij}}.

(5.13)

Below, we show that the transformation law of this stress tensor reproduces the Virasoro symmetry on the CFT side.

The transformations corresponding to the Virasoro symmetry on the gravity side should be those that do not change the metric near the asymptotic boundary.
To see this, expand the metric (
5.5
) with
z=\tau+l\phi,{\bar{z}}=\tau-l\phi
, and
r\to\infty
,

ds^{2}\simeq\frac{l^{2}}{r^{2}}dr^{2}+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}dzd{\bar{z}}.

(5.14)

The possible asymptotic AdS metric consistent with this asymptotics has the following form:

ds^{2}=\frac{l^{2}}{r^{2}}dr^{2}+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}dzd{\bar{z}}+h_{zz}dz^{2}+h_{{\bar{z}}{\bar{z}}}d{\bar{z}}^{2}+2h_{z{\bar{z}}}dzd{\bar{z}}.

(5.15)

This metric does not include all possible terms, but other possible terms can be eliminated by coordinate transformations.
By substituting this metric into (
5.13
), we find that the energy-momentum tensor can be expressed as follows:

T_{zz}=-\frac{1}{4lG_{N}}h_{zz},\ \ \ \ \ T_{{\bar{z}}{\bar{z}}}=-\frac{1}{4lG_{N}}h_{{\bar{z}}{\bar{z}}}.

(5.16)

Here, we used the following Einstein equation (i.e. the equation of motion for the gravitational action):

h_{z{\bar{z}}}=0,\ \ \ \ \ {\partial}h_{{\bar{z}}{\bar{z}}}=\bar{{\partial}}h_{zz}=0.

(5.17)

The infinitesimal coordinate transformations that preserve the form of the metric (
5.15
) can be expressed using arbitrary functions
\epsilon(z),\bar{\epsilon}({\bar{z}})
as follows:

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\rightarrow z+{\epsilon}(z)-\frac{{l}^{4}}{2r^{2}}\bar{{\epsilon}}^{\prime\prime}({\bar{z}}),

(5.18)

\displaystyle{\bar{z}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\bar{z}}+\bar{{\epsilon}}({\bar{z}})-\frac{{l}^{4}}{2r^{2}}{\epsilon}^{\prime\prime}(z),

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle\rightarrow r-\frac{r}{2}{\epsilon}^{\prime}(z)-\frac{r}{2}\bar{{\epsilon}}^{\prime}({\bar{z}}).

This transformation corresponds exactly to the infinitesimal conformal transformation (
2.6
) of the CFT on the asymptotic boundary.
Under this coordinate transformation, the metric changes as follows:

\delta h_{zz}=-2\epsilon^{\prime}(z)h_{zz}-\epsilon(z)h^{\prime}_{zz}+\frac{l^{2}}{2}\epsilon^{\prime\prime\prime}(z).

(5.19)

By converting this to the energy-momentum tensor using (
5.16
), we obtain

\delta T_{zz}=-2\epsilon^{\prime}(z)T_{zz}-\epsilon(z)T^{\prime}_{zz}-\frac{l}{8G_{N}}\epsilon^{\prime\prime\prime}(z).

(5.20)

This matches the transformation law of the energy-momentum tensor obtained earlier (
2.118
).
Comparing the anomaly terms, we find that the central charge is given by:

Brown-Henneaux central charge

c=\frac{3l}{2G_{N}}.

(5.21)

This result is called the
Brown-Henneaux central charge
, named after the authors who have shown the equivalence between the asymptotic symmetry of AdS and the Virasoro symmetry
[
45
]
.

The Brown-Henneaux central charge shows that the classical limit
G_{N}\to 0
corresponds to
c\to\infty
.
In other words,

Einstein gravity is dual to a large
c
CFT.

This CFT is called the
holographic CFT
.
It is important to note that there is still no “precise definition” of the holographic CFT.
The CFT data of the holographic CFT are still unknown,
and revealing its CFT data is one of the important challenges in the AdS/CFT correspondence.
An approach that is useful when only partial information such as large
c
is known is the conformal bootstrap.

5.2.2
GKPW Dictionary

The basic objects in QFT are correlation functions.
What is the gravitational counterpart of correlation functions in the AdS/CFT correspondence?
This is given by the Gubser-Klebanov-Polyakov-Witten (GKPW) dictionary
[
43
,
42
]
:

GKPW dictionary

Z_{\text{grav}}[\phi_{i}]=\Braket{\mathrm{e}^{i\sum_{i}\int d^{d}xO_{i}(x)J_{i}(x)}}_{\text{CFT}}.

(5.22)

Here, each symbol is defined as follows:

\displaystyle O_{i}

\displaystyle\cdots\text{Field in CFT}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\phi_{i}

\displaystyle\cdots\text{Field in gravity}

\displaystyle J_{i}

\displaystyle\cdots\text{Boundary condition for }\phi_{i}:\phi_{i}|_{\text{boundary}}\propto J_{i}

\displaystyle Z_{\text{grav}}[\phi_{i}]

\displaystyle\cdots\text{Partition function of quantum gravity}

Let us first focus on the path integral on the gravity side on the left-hand side.
Since AdS space has a boundary, boundary conditions must be specified to define the path integral.
These are
J_{i}
in (
5.23
).
In Poincaré coordinates, this boundary condition is expressed as follows:

\phi_{i}(z,x)\xrightarrow[z\to 0]{}z^{d-{\Delta}}J_{i}(x).

(5.24)

If we consider the equation of motion for a scalar field
\phi_{i}
with mass
m
, the following equation is obtained in the limit
z\to 0
:

l^{2}m^{2}={\Delta}({\Delta}-d),\ \ \ \ \ {\Delta}=\frac{d}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{d^{2}}{4}+m^{2}R^{2}}.

(5.25)

In fact, this
{\Delta}
is the conformal dimension of the field
O_{i}
in the CFT.
The scale transformation on the CFT side corresponds to the following on the gravity side:

x_{i}\to{\lambda}x_{i},\ \ \ z\to{\lambda}z.

(5.26)

From this fact and (
5.24
), we can understand the behavior of
J_{i}(x)
under the renormalization group.
For the coupling term

\int d^{d}xO_{i}(x)J_{i}(x)

(5.27)

to be invariant under the renormalization group, the conformal dimension of
O_{i}(x)
must be
\Delta
.

The right-hand side of (
5.22
) is the generating functional of the CFT,
and the correlation functions of the CFT are obtained as follows:

\braket{O_{1}(x_{1})\cdots O_{n}(x_{n})}_{\text{CFT}}=\frac{{\delta}^{n}}{{\delta}J_{1}(x_{1})\cdots{\delta}J_{n}(x(n))}\Braket{\mathrm{e}^{i\sum_{i}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}xO_{i}(x)J_{i}(x)}}_{\text{CFT}}.

(5.28)

As seen from the GKPW dictionary, the CFT appears as a “boundary condition” of the quantum gravity on AdS,
so it is often explained that “the CFT lives on the asymptotic boundary of AdS”.
While this is convenient for intuitive interpretation,
it should be noted that the entire AdS quantum gravity corresponds to the CFT,
and the CFT does not simultaneously exist on AdS.

5.2.3
Energy

In this section, we will compare the energy in AdS and CFT.
Let us first calculate the ADM mass of the global AdS geometry.
The ADM mass in AdS is obtained by integrating the boundary energy-momentum tensor
[
44
]
,

M_{\mathrm{ADM}}=\int^{2\pi}_{0}d\phi T_{\tau\tau}.

(5.29)

The boundary energy-momentum tensor for the global AdS geometry is given by

T_{\tau\tau}=-\frac{1}{16\pi G_{N}},

(5.30)

so the ADM mass is

M_{\mathrm{ADM}}=-\frac{1}{8G_{N}}.

(5.31)

Using the Brown-Henneaux central charge, we obtain

E=lM_{\mathrm{ADM}}=-\frac{c}{12}.

(5.32)

It can be confirmed that this ADM mass of the vacuum AdS matches the vacuum energy of the CFT, i.e. the Casimir energy (
2.125
).

Next, consider a more non-trivial case, Euclidean conical defect geometry:

ds^{2}=\left(1+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)d\tau^{2}+\frac{dr^{2}}{\left(1+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)}+r^{2}d\phi^{2},\ \ \ \ \ \phi\in[0,2\pi\chi).

(5.33)

This is the same as the global AdS metric (
5.5
),
but the range of
\phi
is different.
Since this geometry has a deficit angle of
\delta\phi\equiv 2\pi(1-\chi)
,
it has a singularity at the origin.
Moreover, since there is no horizon, this is a naked singularity.
This naked singularity can be interpreted as a point particle.
In fact, the conical defect geometry is an on-shell solution when the following point particle action is added to the Einstein-Hilbert action,

S_{\mathrm{particle}}=-m\int_{\Gamma}\sqrt{\gamma}.

(5.34)

Here,
\Gamma
represents the worldline of the point particle.
Note that the mass
m
of the point particle is related to the deficit angle as

\delta\phi=2\pi(1-\chi)=8\pi G_{N}m.

(5.35)

Let us evaluate the ADM mass of this geometry.
Recall that CFT states are defined on the cylinder with period of
2\pi
.
As can be seen from the expression (
5.29
), the ADM mass is not a scalar quantity, therefore, in order to compare the ADM mass with the energy of a CFT state, it is necessary to evaluate the ADM mass in coordinates whose asymptotic boundary is the cylinder with period of
2\pi
.
For this purpose, we perform the following coordinate transformation to map the metric (
5.33
) into the appropriate coordinate,

\tau\to\frac{\tau}{\chi},\ \ \ \ \phi\to\frac{\phi}{\chi}.

(5.36)

Under this coordinate transformation, the metric takes the following form:

ds^{2}=\left(\chi^{2}+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)d\tau^{2}+\frac{dr^{2}}{\left(\chi^{2}+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)}+r^{2}d\phi^{2},\ \ \ \ \ \phi\in[0,2\pi).

(5.37)

Also, the energy-momentum tensor changes as follows:

T_{\tau\tau}=-\frac{\chi^{2}}{8G_{N}}.

(5.38)

Thus, the ADM mass is given by

E=lM_{\mathrm{ADM}}=-\frac{c}{12}\chi^{2}.

(5.39)

Using (
5.35
), (
5.39
), and the relation
E=2h-\frac{c}{12}
between the energy of the scalar field in the CFT on the cylinder with period
2\pi
and the conformal dimension, we obtain the following relation between the mass
m
of the point particle and the conformal dimension
h
,

m=\frac{c}{6}\left(1-\sqrt{1-\frac{24h}{c}}\right).

(5.40)

This equation can be regarded as an extension of (
5.25
) to
h=O(c)
.
In fact, these two equations match in the regime
1\ll h\ll c
.

Finally, let us consider the black hole solution.
The solution corresponding to the black hole with mass
M
is given by the Banados-Teitelboim-Zanelli (BTZ) metric:

ds^{2}=-\left(\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}-8G_{N}M\right)dt^{2}+\frac{dr^{2}}{\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}-8G_{N}M}+r^{2}d\phi^{2}.

(5.41)

This solution can be regarded as an analytic continuation of the deficit angle of the conical defect geometry to
\chi^{2}\to-8G_{N}M
.
Thus, the ADM mass of the BTZ black hole can be obtained by the analytic continuation of (
5.39
),

M_{\mathrm{ADM}}=M.

(5.42)

In other words, the mass of the BTZ black hole is the ADM mass itself.

Since the conical defect geometry has a negative ADM mass and the BTZ black hole has a positive ADM mass,
using the relation
\Delta-\frac{c}{12}=E
, we obtain the following conclusion:

\displaystyle\text{BTZ\ BH}

\displaystyle=\text{primary state with }\Delta>\frac{c}{12},

(5.43)

\displaystyle\text{conical\ defect}

\displaystyle=\text{primary state with }\Delta<\frac{c}{12}.

From this fact,
\frac{c}{12}
is called the
BTZ threshold
.
Note that since a descendant state is created by the action of Virasoro generators, a descendant state corresponds to an excitation caused by a diffeomorphism (a boundary graviton) on the gravity side.

5.2.4
Thermodynamic Quantities

The GKPW dictionary for the partition function is the simplest example,

Z_{\mathrm{CFT}}(\beta)=Z_{\mathrm{grav}}(\beta).

(5.44)

Here, the left-hand side is the partition function of the CFT defined on the manifold
S^{1}_{2\pi}\times S^{1}_{\beta}
,
and is expressed as
Z_{\mathrm{CFT}}(\beta)\equiv{\text{tr}}\ \mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_{\mathrm{CFT}}}
.

Z_{\mathrm{grav}}(\beta)
is the quantity evaluated by the gravitational path integral on AdS whose asymptotic boundary is given by
S^{1}_{2\pi}\times S^{1}_{\beta}
,
and in particular, in the semiclassical approximation, it can be written as follows using the on-shell Euclidean action,

Z_{\mathrm{grav}}(\beta)=\mathrm{e}^{-S_{E}[g_{1}]}+\mathrm{e}^{-S_{E}[g_{2}]}+\cdots,

(5.45)

where
g_{i}
are solutions to the Einstein equation.

The agreement of these partition functions implies that the thermodynamic quantities on both sides match.
On the gravity side, by evaluating the on-shell Euclidean action, we can get the free energy
F\equiv-\beta^{-1}\ln Z
.
Thus, we can evaluate the thermodynamic quantities including the black hole entropy.
In fact, it can be shown that the black hole entropy calculated in this way matches the entropy calculated on the CFT side (i.e. Cardy formula).
The calculation details on the CFT side are explained in Section
6.1
.

The semiclassical approximation of the gravitational path integral is given by

Z_{\mathrm{grav}}(\beta)=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{c}{12}\beta}+\mathrm{e}^{\frac{c}{12}\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}}+\cdots,

(5.46)

where the two solutions are the thermal AdS and the BTZ black hole.
In the classical limit
c\to\infty
, it is approximated by the larger of these two terms.
This implies that there is a first-order phase transition at the critical temperature
\beta=2\pi
,

\displaystyle\ln Z_{\mathrm{grav}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{c}{12}\beta,&\text{if }\beta<2\pi,\\
\frac{c}{12}\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta},&\text{if }\beta>2\pi.\\
\end{array}\right.

(5.47)

This phase transition is called the Hawking-Page transition
[
46
]
.

5.2.5
Maloney-Witten-Keller Partition Function

The two terms appearing in the semiclassical approximation of the gravitational path integral (
5.46
) are exactly related by the modular
S
transformation.
This reflects the modular invariance of
Z_{\mathrm{CFT}}(\beta)
.
From this expression of the gravitational path integral, one might guess that the partition function of the CFT dual to pure AdS can be written in the following simple form,

Z(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\left|\chi_{\mathbb{I}}(\tau)\right|^{2}+\left|\chi_{\mathbb{I}}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)\right|^{2}.

(5.48)

However, although this is invariant under the modular
S
transformation, it is not invariant under all modular transformations including the modular
T
transformation.
Nevertheless, if it can be updated to a modular invariant partition function in some way,
it may be possible to reveal the CFT dual to the pure AdS.

This attempt to concretely construct a CFT dual to the pure AdS was initiated by Maloney-Witten-Keller
[
47
,
48
]
.
The idea of MWK is to sum over contributions with modular parameters obtained from modular transformations to realize modular invariance. In fact, each of the terms in the modular sum corresponds to a different Euclidean classical saddle
[
49
]
, so in this way, the partition function is a sum over classical solutions.

Z_{\mathrm{MWK}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})}\left|\chi_{\mathbb{I}}(\gamma\tau)\right|^{2}.

(5.49)

Here,
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
is the projective special linear group,

\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{array}\right)\in\mathrm{M}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\ |\ ad-bc=1\right\}/m\sim m^{\prime}:\Leftrightarrow m=-m^{\prime}.

(5.50)

For the modular invariance to be satisfied, this partition function must be convergent,
but in fact, this sum (called the Poincaré series) diverges.
MWK proposes a method to appropriately remove this divergence,
but the MWK partition function obtained in this way has the following problems
[
47
,
50
]
:

1.

continuous spectrum.

2.

non-unitary.

Regarding the second problem, it has been proposed that it can be resolved by adding other contributions (e.g. Poincaré series of non-vacuum characters) to the MWK partition function
[
48
,
50
,
51
,
52
]
.
Further advancing such improvements, ultimately constructing a unitary compact MWK partition function is one of the important challenges in the study of AdS/CFT.

5.3
Correlation Functions in Holographic CFT

In general, the correlation functions of a CFT are expressed as (infinite) sums of complicated special functions i.e. the Virasoro blocks,
which makes extracting physics from correlation functions complicated.
However, in holographic CFTs, the expression for correlation functions simplifies under certain assumptions.
The mechanism of simplification can be roughly described as follows:

•

In the large
c
limit, from the viewpoint of the saddle point approximation,
the correlation function can be approximated by a single Virasoro block.

•

In the large
c
limit, the expression of the Virasoro block simplifies.

Below, we explain the details of the simplification of the Virasoro block.

5.3.1
Heavy-Light Virasoro Block

Here, we explain the simplification of the Virasoro block in the large
c
limit.
In fact, we have already seen the simplification in a special case in Section
3.7
.
For convenience, we repost (
3.67
) below,

\displaystyle\lim_{c\to\infty}\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{n}=\underbrace{(-1,-,1,\cdots,-1)}_{N}\end{subarray}}\rho(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},\nu_{p,\bm{n}}|0)\left[G_{p}^{(N)}\right]_{\bm{n},\bm{n}}^{-1}\rho(\nu_{p,\bm{n}},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|z)

(5.51)

\displaystyle=\sum_{N}\frac{(h_{p}+h_{3}-h_{4})_{N}(h_{p}+h_{2}-h_{1})_{N}}{N!(2h_{p})_{N}}z^{h_{p}+N-h_{1}-h_{2}}

\displaystyle=z^{h_{p}-h_{1}-h_{2}}{}_{2}F_{1}(h_{p}+h_{2}-h_{1},h_{p}+h_{3}-h_{4};2h_{p};z).

Recall that when deriving this large
c
Virasoro block,
we took the
c\to\infty
limit while keeping all parameters (i.e.
\{h_{i}\}
and
z
) fixed.
Here, we generalize this and consider the Virasoro block in the
c\to\infty
limit under the following assumptions:

h_{3},h_{4}=O(c),\ \ \ \ h_{1},h_{2},\left|h_{3}-h_{4}\right|,h_{p},z=O(c^{0}).

(5.52)

Under these assumptions, the first equation in (
5.51
) (i.e. the assertion that
L_{n<-1}
descendants do not contribute in the large
c
limit) no longer holds.
This is because the 3-point block
\rho(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},\nu_{p,\bm{n}}|0)
becomes
O(c)
,
and the suppression by
\frac{1}{c}
from the inverse Gram matrix is canceled out.
We explain how to resolve this problem below.

For simplicity, let
h_{1}=h_{2}\equiv h_{L}
and
h_{3}=h_{4}\equiv h_{H}
.

H
stands for “Heavy” and
L
stands for “Light”.
In this case, by the Ward-Takahashi identity,

\braket{\phi_{H}(\infty)\phi_{H}(1)T(z)\phi_{h}(0)}=C_{HHh}\left(\frac{h_{H}}{(1-z)^{2}}+\frac{h}{(1-z)z^{2}}\right).

(5.53)

Recalling that the Virasoro generator corresponds to the Laurent coefficients of
T(z)
,
it is clear that the reason
\rho(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},\nu_{p,\bm{n}}|0)
is
O(c)
is essentially because the first term in (
5.53
) is proportional to
h_{H}=O(c)
.

Figure 16:
Insertion of the identity operator into the four-point function on the
w
coordinates. Note the presence of the branch cut.

We consider eliminating this term proportional to
c
by a conformal transformation.
The key is the transformation law (
2.119
) of the energy-momentum tensor
T(z)
:

T^{\prime}(w)=\left(\frac{dw}{dz}\right)^{-2}\left(T(z)-\frac{c}{12}\{w,z\}\right).

(5.54)

Consider the following conformal transformation:

z\to w=1-(1-z)^{\alpha},\ \ \ \ \ \alpha\equiv\sqrt{1-24\frac{h_{H}}{c}}.

(5.55)

Under this transformation, the Schwarzian derivative term exactly cancels the
c
-proportional term in (
5.53
):

\braket{\phi_{H}(\infty)\phi_{H}(1)T(w)\phi_{h}(0)}=C_{HHh}\left(h\,\frac{1-z}{z^{2}}\right).

(5.56)

With this fact in mind, we consider evaluating the Virasoro block on the
w
coordinates.
The Virasoro block on the
w
coordinates is also defined by inserting a projection operator onto the Verma module (see Figure
16
).
In this case, the Virasoro block can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\lim_{c\to\infty}\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{n}=\underbrace{(-1,-,1,\cdots,-1)}_{N}\end{subarray}}\tilde{\rho}(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},\nu_{p,\bm{n}}|0)\left[G_{p}^{(N)}\right]_{\bm{n},\bm{n}}^{-1}\rho(\nu_{p,\bm{n}},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|w).

(5.57)

The three-point block
\rho(\nu_{p,\bm{n}},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|w)
can be calculated in the same way as in the plane coordinates.
On the other hand, since the three-point block
\tilde{\rho}(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},\nu_{p,\bm{n}}|0)
includes a branch cut as seen in Figure
16
,
the action of the Virasoro algebra changes.
This is emphasized by adding a tilde.
As a result of the change in the action of the Virasoro algebra,
\tilde{\rho}(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},\nu_{p,\bm{n}}|0)
becomes
O(c^{0})
.
This is a consequence of (
5.56
).
It can be shown that the insertion of multiple energy-momentum tensors also results in
O(c^{0})
.
Therefore, all contributions from
L_{n<-1}
are suppressed by
\frac{1}{c}
by the inverse Gram matrix.
Thus, only the contributions from descendants consisting of
L_{-1}
remain, as shown in (
5.57
).

Next, we consider how
L_{-1}
acts on the three-point block with the branch cut.
From (
5.56
), we directly obtain the following result:

\tilde{\rho}(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},\nu_{p,\bm{n}}|0)=\alpha^{-h_{p}}\left(h_{p}+\frac{h_{3}-h_{4}}{\alpha}\right)_{N}.

(5.58)

Here, we have written a general expression including the case of
h_{1}\neq h_{2}
and
h_{3}\neq h_{4}
.
The three-point block
\rho(\nu_{p,\bm{n}},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|w)
including the Jacobian factor is

\rho(\nu_{p,\bm{n}},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|w)=\alpha^{h_{1}+h_{2}}(1-w)^{\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)h_{2}}(h_{p}+h_{2}-h_{1})_{N}w^{h_{p}+N-h_{1}-h_{2}}.

(5.59)

Thus, we finally obtain the following result
[
53
]
:

Heavy-Light Virasoro block

\displaystyle\lim_{c\to\infty}\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{n}=\underbrace{(-1,-,1,\cdots,-1)}_{N}\end{subarray}}\tilde{\rho}(\nu_{4},\nu_{3},\nu_{p,\bm{n}}|0)\left[G_{p}^{(N)}\right]_{\bm{n},\bm{n}}^{-1}\rho(\nu_{p,\bm{n}},\nu_{2},\nu_{1}|z)

(5.60)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-h_{p}+h_{1}+h_{2}}(1-w)^{\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)h_{2}}\sum_{N}\frac{\left(h_{p}+\frac{h_{3}-h_{4}}{\alpha}\right)_{N}(h_{p}+h_{2}-h_{1})_{N}}{N!(2h_{p})_{N}}w^{h_{p}+N-h_{1}-h_{2}}

\displaystyle=(1-w)^{\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)h_{2}}\left(\frac{w}{\alpha}\right)^{h_{p}-h_{1}-h_{2}}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(h_{p}+h_{2}-h_{1},h_{p}+\frac{h_{3}-h_{4}}{\alpha};2h_{p};w\right).

Since
\left|h_{3}-h_{4}\right|=O(c^{0})
,
\alpha
is defined similarly even for
h_{3}\neq h_{4}
:

\alpha\equiv\sqrt{1-24\frac{h_{3}}{c}}+O(c^{-1})=\sqrt{1-24\frac{h_{4}}{c}}+O(c^{-1}).

(5.61)

This expression for the Virasoro block in the large
c
limit is called the
Heavy-Light Virasoro block
or
HHLL Virasoro block
.

5.3.2
Heavy-Light Virasoro Block and Information Loss Problem

Figure 17:
Two-point correlation function in the black hole background geometry.

The two-point function
\braket{\phi_{L}(t)\phi_{L}(0)}_{BH}
in the black hole background geometry (see Figure
17
) decays exponentially under time evolution.
Intuitively, this reflects the fact that the information swallowed by the black hole is permanently lost.
This contradicts the unitarity of quantum mechanics, and this contradiction is known as
information loss problem
.
Resolving this information loss problem is one of the major goals in quantum gravity.

In Euclidean time, the information loss problem appears as the periodicity of the correlation function.
Using the AdS/CFT correspondence, we consider understanding the mechanism of periodicity in the semiclassical approximation from the CFT.
The two-point function in the black hole microstate depicted in Figure
17
corresponds to the following correlation function in the CFT:

\braket{\phi_{H}(\infty)\phi_{H}(1)\phi_{L}(z,{\bar{z}})\phi_{L}(0)}.

(5.62)

Although we would like to directly investigate this correlation function,
since the exact CFT data of holographic CFTs are unknown,
instead of examining the correlation function, we focus on the Virasoro block, which is a component of the correlation function.

The Virasoro block appearing in the Heavy-Light correlation function is precisely the Heavy-Light Virasoro block (
5.60
) obtained earlier.
First, to match the setup in Figure
17
, we consider the following coordinate transformation:

1-z=\mathrm{e}^{-t_{E}}.

(5.63)

In this case, the Heavy-Light vacuum block becomes:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{LL}_{HH}(0|t_{E})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-h_{L}t_{E}}\mathcal{F}^{LL}_{HH}(0|z)

(5.64)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\alpha}{2\sinh\frac{\alpha t_{E}}{2}}\right)^{2h_{L}}.

The factor appearing on the right-hand side of the first line is the Jacobi factor.
Recall from Section
5.2.4
that the state corresponding to the black hole had
h_{H}>\frac{c}{24}
.
The
\alpha=\sqrt{1-24\frac{h_{H}}{c}}
appearing in the Heavy-Light Virasoro block becomes purely imaginary above the BTZ threshold, i.e.
h_{H}>\frac{c}{24}
.
By setting
\alpha\equiv 2\pi iT_{H}
, we obtain the following expression:

\mathcal{F}^{LL}_{HH}(0|t_{E})=\left(\frac{\pi T_{H}}{\sin\left(\pi T_{H}t_{E}\right)}\right)^{2\Delta_{L}}.

(5.65)

As can be seen from the expression, this Virasoro block has a periodicity parameterized by
T_{E}
in the Euclidean time direction.
In other words, the expected “periodicity” appears precisely above the BTZ threshold.
Furthermore, by the Wick rotation
t_{E}\to it
on this expression, it can be observed that it decays exponentially as
\mathrm{e}^{-\pi T_{H}t}
for
t\to\infty
.

In fact, this functional form is expected to appear.
The Euclidean black hole has periodicity in the time direction,
implying that the corresponding CFT is none other than a finite-temperature CFT.
The two-point function on a finite-temperature CFT at temperature
T_{E}
is the two-point function on a cylinder with periodicity
T_{E}
in the time direction.
As seen in the state-operator correspondence, the cylinder and the plane are connected by a conformal transformation,
so this two-point function on the cylinder can be calculated from the two-point function on the plane (
2.153
).
Using the conformal transformation
z=1-\mathrm{e}^{t_{E}}
, we obtain:

\braket{\phi_{L}(t_{E})\phi_{L}(0)}_{T_{H}}=\left(\frac{\pi T_{H}}{\sin\left(\pi T_{H}t_{E}\right)}\right)^{2h_{L}}.

(5.66)

In other words, the Heavy-Light vacuum block (
5.65
) is none other than the two-point function on the finite-temperature CFT at temperature
T_{H}
.
This result suggests that pure states with conformal dimensions above the BTZ threshold and the mixed states of finite temperature are indistinguishable in the semiclassical limit.

One consequence of the periodicity of the Heavy-Light vacuum block is the existence of infinitely many singularities
z=1-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{n}{T_{H}}}\ \ \ (n\in\mathbb{Z})

[
54
]
.
The following translation is possible for the information loss problem:

Information loss problem
\xrightarrow[\mathrm{Euclidean}]{}
Periodicity
\to
Infinitely many singularities

On the other hand, as is clear from the discussion of the convergence of the OPE, the Heavy-Light correlation function (
5.62
) should have no singularities other than the OPE singularities.
The reason why these “forbidden singularities” appear is due to the large
c
limit.

In this section, we have clarified how (the Euclidian version of) the information loss problem is reproduced in the semiclassical approximation on the CFT side.
The mechanism for solving the information loss problem should be understood by investigating the
\frac{1}{c}
corrections in large
c
CFT.
This direction of research is ongoing, as seen in
[
23
]
, and continues to develop.

5.3.3
Entanglement Entropy

Figure 18:
Ryu-Takayanagi surface
{\gamma}_{A}
corresponding to the entanglement entropy of subsystem
A
on a constant-time slice of the CFT

Here, as an application of the Heavy-Light Virasoro block,
we introduce the calculation of entanglement entropy.

In these lecture notes, entanglement entropy appeared in Section
2.11
as one method of measuring the central charge.
In the context of the AdS/CFT correspondence, this entanglement entropy plays an important role.
One reason is that the gravitational dual of entanglement entropy is given by a simple formula:

S_{A}=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\gamma}_{A}\\
{\partial}{\gamma}_{A}={\partial}A\end{subarray}}\frac{\text{Area}({\gamma}_{A})}{4G_{N}}.

(5.67)

{\gamma}_{A}
is a surface homologous to the subsystem
A
on the boundary CFT.

48
48

48

Roughly speaking,
A
is homologous to
B
if
A
can be continuously deformed into
B
.

A depiction of
{\gamma}_{A}
is provided in Figure
18
.
This formula is known as the
Ryu-Takayanagi formula
, named after its discoverers
[
55
]
.
The minimal surface
{\gamma}_{A}
is called the
Ryu-Takayanagi surface
(or RT surface).

Let us compute the holographic entanglement entropy in the BTZ black hole geometry.
The RT surface is given by a surface as shown in Figure
19
.
Calculating the area of this surface in the BTZ black hole background, we obtain the following result:

\displaystyle S_{A}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{c}{3}\log\frac{\sinh\pi T_{H}R}{\pi T_{H}\epsilon},&\text{if \ }0<R<\pi,\\
\frac{c}{3}\log\frac{\sinh\pi T_{H}(2\pi-R)}{\pi T_{H}\epsilon},&\text{if \ }\pi<R<2\pi.\\
\end{array}\right.

(5.68)

Here, we used the Brown-Henneaux formula
c=\frac{3l}{2G_{N}}
.

R\in[0,2\pi]
is the length of the subsystem
A
.

2\pi T_{H}(=\sqrt{8M})
is the temperature of the black hole,
and rewriting it using the conformal dimension,
2\pi iT_{H}=\alpha\equiv\sqrt{1-\frac{24}{c}h_{H}}
.
Since the AdS metric diverges at the asymptotic boundary,
the area of the surface extending from the asymptotic boundary also diverges.
Therefore, a cutoff parameter
\epsilon
must be introduced near the asymptotic boundary to regularize the calculation.

49
49

49

A detailed explanation of how to introduce the cutoff can be found in
[
56
]
.

Figure 19:
RT surface in the BTZ black hole geometry

The entanglement entropy for a state
\rho=\ket{\phi_{H}}\bra{\phi_{H}}
can be calculated using the following formula
[
6
]
:

S_{A}=\lim_{n\to 1}\frac{1}{1-n}\log\braket{\phi_{H}^{\otimes n}|\bar{\sigma}_{n}(R)\sigma_{n}(0)|\phi_{H}^{\otimes n}},

(5.69)

where
\phi_{H}^{\otimes n}
is a primary operator with conformal dimension
nh_{H}
,
and the twist operator
\sigma_{n}
is a primary operator with conformal dimension
h_{n}\equiv\frac{c}{24}\left(n-\frac{1}{n}\right)
.
Since the information required for the calculations here is limited to the conformal dimensions,
the details of the notation can be ignored.

50
50

50

Let us denote the CFT as
\mathcal{M}
.
The twist operator is defined as the endpoint of a
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
symmetry defect in the tensor product
\mathcal{M}^{\otimes n}
.
The correlation function
\braket{\phi_{H}^{\otimes n}|\bar{\sigma}_{n}(R)\sigma_{n}(0)|\phi_{H}^{\otimes n}}

is defined not on
\mathcal{M}
but on
\mathcal{M}^{\otimes n}
,
where
\phi_{H}^{\otimes n}
is a tensor product of the primary operators defined on
\mathcal{M}^{\otimes n}
.
Further details would deviate significantly from the purpose of these lecture notes, so they will not be covered here. See
[
57
]
for more details.

The CFT is defined on a cylinder
w=t_{E}+i\theta
through the conformal transformation
z=1-\mathrm{e}^{-w}
,
and the twist operators are inserted at
\theta=0
and
\theta=R
.
Using the saddle-point approximation in the large
c
limit,
we can approximate the correlation function with a single Virasoro block.

\phi_{H}
represents a black hole microstate, which means
h_{H}=O(c)
.
Additionally, the conformal dimension of
\sigma_{n}
satisfies
h_{n}\ll 1
when
n-1\ll 1
.
Therefore, the correlation function appearing in (
5.69
) can ultimately be approximated by the Heavy-Light vacuum block (
5.65
).
Substituting
t_{E}=iR
into (
5.65
) and then into (
5.69
), we obtain:

S_{A}\simeq\frac{c}{3}\log\frac{\sinh\pi T_{H}R}{\pi T_{H}\epsilon},\ \ \ \ R\ll 2\pi.

(5.70)

Here, the UV cutoff parameter
\epsilon
is added through dimensional analysis.

51
51

51

To define entanglement entropy,
the Hilbert space needs to be decomposed as in (
2.128
),
but the Hilbert space of QFT cannot be decomposed in principle.
Therefore, by introducing a lattice size cutoff parameter,
the QFT Hilbert space is approximately decomposed like a lattice system.
As a result, the entanglement entropy in QFT always includes a cutoff parameter.
How to specifically introduce the cutoff is discussed in
[
58
]
.
A detailed explanation would be lengthy,
so here the UV cutoff parameter is heuristically determined by dimensional analysis.
In fact, it is known that the exact definition of the twist operator necessarily involves the cutoff parameter
[
59
]
.

The validity region of this result is limited because the saddle point approximation is used.
Although details are omitted here, by examining the correct choice of the saddle point and the validity region of the saddle point approximation,
the above result (
5.70
) is ultimately extended as follows
[
57
]
:

\displaystyle S_{A}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{c}{3}\log\frac{\sinh\pi T_{H}R}{\pi T_{H}\epsilon},&\text{if \ }0<R<\pi,\\
\frac{c}{3}\log\frac{\sinh\pi T_{H}(2\pi-R)}{\pi T_{H}\epsilon},&\text{if \ }\pi<R<2\pi.\\
\end{array}\right.

(5.71)

This result beautifully matches (
5.68
).

The Ryu-Takayanagi formula has brought a quantum information-theoretic approach to quantum gravity,
establishing a new field that merges quantum gravity and quantum information.
Practically, it contributes to understanding the properties of quantum gravity by bringing inequalities known in quantum information theory into quantum gravity.

5.3.4
Monodromy Method

Here, we introduce another method for obtaining the Virasoro block in the large
c
limit.
Since Liouville CFT has a continuous spectrum,
its correlation functions contain Virasoro blocks with a wide range of parameters.
The basic idea is to derive the expression for the Virasoro block by examining the correlation functions of Liouville CFT in the large
c
limit.

Here, we focus on the application to the large
h
Virasoro block (
3.58
),
but it is also applied to the Heavy-Light Virasoro block mentioned above and generalizations to
n
-point functions (e.g.
[
60
,
57
]
).

1.

BPZ equation

For the degenerate primary field
\Psi
with conformal dimension
h_{21}=-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{4}b^{2}
, we set up the BPZ equation (see Section
2.14
).

52
52

52

Although Liouville CFT does not have degenerate primary fields,
the analyticity of the correlation functions allows analytic continuation to
h_{i}\to h_{21}
,
and the correlation function after the analytic continuation satisfies the BPZ equation.
This method is also used in the derivation of the DOZZ formula.

\left[\frac{1}{b^{2}}\partial_{z}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\left(\frac{h_{i}}{(z-z_{i})^{2}}+\frac{1}{z-z_{i}}\partial_{i}\right)\right]\langle\phi_{4}(z_{4},{\bar{z}}_{4})\phi_{3}(z_{3},{\bar{z}}_{3})\Psi(z,{\bar{z}})\phi_{2}(z_{2},{\bar{z}}_{2})\phi_{1}(z_{1},{\bar{z}}_{1})\rangle=0.

(5.72)

2.

BPZ for each intermediate state

We consider the large
c
limit with
h_{i}=O(c)
.
In this limit, define
\Psi_{p}(z,{\bar{z}})
as follows:

\Psi_{p}(z,{\bar{z}})\equiv\frac{\braket{\phi_{4}(z_{4},{\bar{z}}_{4})\phi_{3}(z_{3},{\bar{z}}_{3})\Psi(z,{\bar{z}})\phi_{p,\bm{n}}(0,0)}}{\braket{\phi_{4}(z_{4},{\bar{z}}_{4})\phi_{3}(z_{3},{\bar{z}}_{3})\phi_{p,\bm{n}}(0,0)}}.

(5.73)

Here, it is important that
\Psi_{p}(z,{\bar{z}})
does not depend on the label
\bm{n}
of the descendant.
This is because,
by replacing the action of the Virasoro algebra on
\phi_{p}
with the action on
\phi_{4}
,
\phi_{3}
, and
\Psi
as in Figure
8
,
the action of the Virasoro algebra on
\Psi
is negligible compared to the other actions (
=O(c)
),
since the conformal dimension of
\Psi
is
O(c^{0})
.

By using
\Psi_{p}
, the five-point function can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{4}(z_{4},{\bar{z}}_{4})\phi_{3}(z_{3},{\bar{z}}_{3})\Psi(z,{\bar{z}})\phi_{2}(z_{2},{\bar{z}}_{2})\phi_{1}(z_{1},{\bar{z}}_{1})\rangle

(5.74)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p}\langle\phi_{4}(z_{4},{\bar{z}}_{4})\phi_{3}(z_{3},{\bar{z}}_{3})\Psi(z,{\bar{z}})\left(\Pi_{h_{p}}\otimes\bar{\Pi}_{\bar{h}_{p}}\right)\phi_{2}(z_{2},{\bar{z}}_{2})\phi_{1}(z_{1},{\bar{z}}_{1})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{p}\Psi_{p}(z,{\bar{z}})C_{12p}C_{34p}\left|\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})\right|^{2}.

Here,
\Pi_{h_{p}}
is the projection operator onto the Verma module
\mathcal{V}_{h_{p}}
.

Let us focus on the
monodromy
, which is a change of a function as we run around a singularity.
For example, the logarithmic function
\log z
has a singularity at
z=0
, and therefore, it exhibits a nontrivial monodromy around
z=0
:

\log z\to\log z+2\pi i.

(5.75)

The BPZ equation is invariant under a monodromy of
z_{2}
around
z_{1}
.
Therefore, when expressing the solution of the BPZ equation as a linear combination of functions with different monodromies around
z_{1}
,
each function is expected to be a solution separately.
When decomposing the solution as in (
5.74
), the monodromy of
z_{2}
around
z_{1}
for each
p
is given by the phase
2\pi(h_{p}-h_{1}-h_{2})
(see (
3.8
)),
so the following separate differential equation holds for each
p
:

\left[\frac{1}{b^{2}}\partial_{z}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\left(\frac{h_{i}}{(z-z_{i})^{2}}+\frac{1}{z-z_{i}}\partial_{i}\right)\right]\Psi_{p}(z,{\bar{z}})C_{12p}C_{34p}\left|\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})\right|^{2}=0.

(5.76)

3.

Ward–Takahashi identity

Define the accessory parameters
C_{i}
as follows:

C_{i}\equiv-b^{2}{\partial}_{i}\log\left(\Psi_{p}(z,{\bar{z}})C_{12p}C_{34p}\left|\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})\right|^{2}\right).

(5.77)

By following the same procedure as the global Ward-Takahashi identity (
2.152
),
we obtain the following system of equations for the accessory parameters:

\displaystyle\sum_{i}C_{i}=0,

(5.78)

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\left(C_{i}z_{i}-\delta_{i}\right)=0,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\left(C_{i}z_{i}^{2}-2\delta_{i}z_{i}\right)=0.

Here,
\delta_{i}\equiv b^{2}h_{i}
. Solving this system of equations and setting
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})\to(0,x,1,\infty)
, we obtain:

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=-(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}-\delta_{4})+C_{2}(x-1),

(5.79)

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle=\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}-\delta_{4}-C_{2}x,

\displaystyle C_{4}

\displaystyle=0.

Substituting these into (
5.76
) and setting
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})\to(0,x,1,\infty)
, we obtain the following differential equation:

\left[\partial_{z}^{2}+\frac{\delta_{1}}{z^{2}}+\frac{\delta_{2}}{(z-x)^{2}}+\frac{\delta_{3}}{(1-z)^{2}}+\frac{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}-\delta_{4}}{z(1-z)}-\frac{C_{2}x(1-x)}{z(z-x)(1-z)}\right]\Psi_{p}=0.

(5.80)

Assume that the Virasoro block behaves as follows in the large
c
limit:

\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(p|x)\sim\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{c}{6}f_{cl}(p|x)}.

(5.81)

This assumption is justified by the expression of the correlation function in Liouville CFT.
Thus, the accessory parameter is expressed as follows:

C_{2}={\partial}_{x}f_{cl}(p|x).

(5.82)

4.

Monodromy equation

Now, the problem of finding the Virasoro block has been reduced to the problem of determining the accessory parameter
C_{2}
.
In the next step, we use the
monodromy method
, which is a method to determine the accessory parameter so that it reproduces the known monodromy of the solution.
Since
\Psi
is a degenerate field with conformal dimension
h_{21}
, the BPZ equation gives the following OPE:

\Psi(z)\phi_{p}(0)\sim C_{+}\phi_{+}z^{\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\sqrt{1-4b^{2}h_{p}}\right)}\left(1+O(z)\right)+C_{-}\phi_{-}z^{\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\sqrt{1-4b^{2}h_{p}}\right)}\left(1+O(z)\right).

(5.83)

\phi_{\pm}
are the possible primary operators in the OPE determined by the BPZ equation (
2.167
),
and
C_{\pm}
are their respective OPE coefficients.
Let the solutions to (
5.80
) corresponding to each intermediate state be
(\Psi_{+},\Psi_{-})
,
and the solutions after one cycle of
\Psi
be
(\Psi^{\prime}_{+},\Psi^{\prime}_{-})
.
The relationship between these solutions can be expressed using the monodromy matrix
M
as follows:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Psi^{\prime}_{+}\\
\Psi^{\prime}_{-}\\
\end{array}\right)=M\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Psi_{+}\\
\Psi_{-}\\
\end{array}\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ M=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{i\pi\left(1+\sqrt{1-4b^{2}h_{p}}\right)}&0\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{i\pi\left(1-\sqrt{1-4b^{2}h_{p}}\right)}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.84)

Thus, the accessory parameter
C_{2}
is determined by the condition that the monodromy of the solutions to the differential equation is given by (
5.84
).

5.

WKB approximation

To solve the monodromy method, it is necessary to specify the form of the solution to some extent initially.
Perturbation methods or WKB methods are used for this purpose.
Here, we use the WKB method under the condition
\delta_{p}\to\infty
.
Approximating the differential equation (
5.80
) under
\delta_{i}\ll\delta_{p}
, we obtain the following approximate solution:

\Psi_{p}\simeq\exp\left[\pm\sqrt{x(1-x)C_{2}}\int_{z_{0}}^{z}\frac{dz^{\prime}}{\sqrt{z^{\prime}(1-z^{\prime})(z^{\prime}-x)}}\right].

(5.85)

Imposing that this solution is consistent with the monodromy equation (
5.84
), we obtain the following equation for
C_{2}
:

C_{2}\simeq-\frac{\pi^{2}b^{2}h_{p}}{x(1-x)K(x)^{2}}.

(5.86)

From the relationship between the accessory parameter and the Virasoro block (
5.82
), we obtain

\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|x)\simeq\left(16q\right)^{h_{p}},\ \ \ \ \ \ q(x)=\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\frac{K(1-x)}{K(x)}}.

(5.87)

Furthermore, by solving up to higher orders, we finally obtain

Large
h
Virasoro block

\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|x)\simeq(16q)^{h_{p}-\frac{c-1}{24}}(\theta_{3}(q))^{\frac{c-1}{2}-4(h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}+h_{4})}x^{\frac{c-1}{24}-h_{1}-h_{2}}(1-x)^{\frac{c-1}{24}-h_{2}-h_{3}},\ \ \ \ \ \ h_{p}\gg c.

(5.88)

In fact, it can be proven that this result holds for any
c
.
The proof is somewhat complicated, but interested readers should refer to
[
12
]
.
This large
h
limit of the Virasoro block is the leading term of the Zamolodchikov
h
-recursion relation (
3.58
).

6
Holographic CFT and Modular Bootstrap

In Section
5
, we introduced the concept of a holographic CFT.
Although the details of holographic CFTs are not fully understood, there are several known properties, as discussed in Section
5
.
For example:

•

c\gg 1

Especially,
c>1
is important.

•

irrational

This is because the number of primary fields is clearly not finite, as suggested by the fact that the number of states is given by the black hole entropy.

•

compact: discrete spectrum including the vacuum state

This derives from the fact that the Einstein equation has the vacuum solution.

53
53

53

Generally, physical systems are expected to have a discrete spectrum, therefore, we implicitly assume a discrete spectrum from the start.
It might seem contradictory that black hole states are characterized by the continuous parameter
M
,
but this continuity is due to the large
c
limit, where the differences between adjacent energy levels become almost indistinguishable.

•

unitary

In situations where some properties of a given CFT are known,
the conformal bootstrap can be a powerful tool.
As an example, in this section, we introduce the application of the modular bootstrap to holographic CFTs.

6.1
Cardy Formula and Black Hole Entropy

The Cardy formula implies a universal property of the number of states at high energy
[
2
]
.
It is a result derived from the modular invariance and simple calculations,
making it one of the widely known applications of the modular bootstrap equation.

6.1.1
Derivation of Cardy Formula

Assuming a unitary compact CFT, consider the following modular invariant partition function:

Z(\beta)\equiv{\text{tr}}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}=\sum_{\Delta}d_{\Delta}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(\Delta-\frac{c}{12})}.

(6.1)

Here, the sum runs over all states, and
d_{\Delta}
is the number of states with scaling dimension
\Delta=h+\bar{h}
.
Define the density of states as follows:

\rho(\Delta)=\sum_{p}d_{\Delta_{p}}\delta(\Delta-\Delta_{p}),

(6.2)

where
p
is a label for a state in the Hilbert space.
The partition function can be expressed in terms of the density of states as follows:

Z(\beta)=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\Delta\ \rho(\Delta)\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(\Delta-\frac{c}{12})}.

(6.3)

The modular bootstrap equation is expressed as:

\int_{0}^{\infty}d\Delta\ \rho(\Delta)\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(\Delta-\frac{c}{12})}=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\Delta\ \rho(\Delta)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}(\Delta-\frac{c}{12})}.

(6.4)

Consider the
\beta\to 0
limit of this modular bootstrap equation.
This limit corresponds to taking the high-temperature limit on the left-hand side and the low-temperature limit on the right-hand side,
hence it is also called
high-low temperature duality
.
In this limit, the right-hand side can be approximated by the contribution from the vacuum state (
\Delta=0
):

\int_{0}^{\infty}d\Delta\ \rho(\Delta)\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(\Delta-\frac{c}{12})}\simeq\mathrm{e}^{\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}\frac{c}{12}}.

(6.5)

One finds that this is a Laplace transform.
Therefore, by the inverse Laplace transform, we obtain

\rho(\Delta)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}d\beta\ \mathrm{e}^{\beta\left(\Delta-\frac{c}{12}\right)+\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}\frac{c}{12}}\simeq\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c}{3}\left(\Delta-\frac{c}{12}\right)}}.

(6.6)

The second expression comes from the saddle point approximation.
The saddle point is given by:

\beta_{*}=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c}{12}\frac{1}{\Delta-\frac{c}{12}}}.

(6.7)

The approximation by the vacuum state is valid only for
\beta\ll 1
,
so this saddle point approximation is valid only for
\Delta\gg c
.
Thus, we obtain the following
Cardy formula
:

Cardy formula

\log\rho(\Delta)\simeq 2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c}{3}\left(\Delta-\frac{c}{12}\right)},\ \ \ \ \ \Delta\gg c.

(6.8)

6.1.2
Bekenstein-Hawking Entropy

The Cardy formula plays an important role in AdS/CFT.
Consider the BTZ black hole metric with mass
M
,

ds^{2}=-\left(\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}-8G_{N}M\right)dt^{2}+\frac{dr^{2}}{\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}-8G_{N}M}+r^{2}d\phi^{2}.

(6.9)

Here,
\phi\sim\phi+2\pi
. Also,
G_{N}
is the Newton constant and
l
is the AdS radius.
The entropy of this black hole is given by the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy formula:

S=\frac{\mathrm{Area}}{4G_{N}}=\frac{2\pi l}{4G_{N}}\sqrt{8G_{N}M},

(6.10)

where
\mathrm{Area}
represents the area of the event horizon of the black hole.
According to the AdS/CFT correspondence, the BTZ black hole with mass
M
can be interpreted as a state with the following conformal dimension
[
61
]
(see also Section
5.2.4
):

\Delta-\frac{c}{12}=Ml.

(6.11)

With this relation, we can re-express the black hole entropy (
6.10
) as

S=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c}{3}\left(\Delta-\frac{c}{12}\right)}.

(6.12)

Here, we used the Brown-Henneaux formula
c=\frac{3l}{2G_{N}}
.
Compared with the Cardy formula (
6.8
),
we see that the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy perfectly matches the Cardy formula.

Hawking linked the area of the black hole horizon with entropy due to its analogy with thermodynamics,
but it was extremely difficult at the time to clarify the statistical mechanics behind black hole thermodynamics.
The Cardy formula provided evidence that the BTZ black hole entropy indeed corresponds to counting quantum states.

6.1.3
Rotating BTZ Black Hole

The Cardy formula can be generalized to cases with nonzero spin.
In the derivation of the Cardy formula above, we assumed
\beta=\bar{\beta}
, but now we consider removing this assumption,

Z(\beta,\bar{\beta})\equiv{\text{tr}}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\left(L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}\right)-\bar{\beta}\left(\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}\right)}=\sum_{h,\bar{h}}d_{h,\bar{h}}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\left(h-\frac{c}{24}\right)-\bar{\beta}\left(\bar{h}-\frac{c}{24}\right)}.

(6.13)

Performing independent inverse Laplace transforms for
\beta,\bar{\beta}\to 0
, we ultimately obtain the following Cardy formula:

\log\rho(h,\bar{h})\simeq 2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c}{6}\left(h-\frac{c}{24}\right)}+2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c}{6}\left(\bar{h}-\frac{c}{24}\right)},\ \ \ \ \ h,\bar{h}\gg c.

(6.14)

Let us compare this result with the gravity calculation.
The metric of the BTZ black hole with mass
M
and angular momentum
J
is given by

ds^{2}=-\left(\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}+\frac{16G_{N}^{2}J^{2}}{r^{2}}-8G_{N}M\right)dt^{2}+\frac{dr^{2}}{\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}+\frac{16G_{N}^{2}J^{2}}{r^{2}}-8G_{N}M}+r^{2}\left(d\phi-\frac{4G_{N}J}{r^{2}}dt\right)^{2}.

(6.15)

The outer and inner horizons of this black hole are given by

r_{\pm}^{2}=4G_{N}Ml^{2}\left(1\pm\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{J}{Ml}\right)^{2}}\right).

(6.16)

The relation between
(M,J)
and the horizons is

M=\frac{r_{+}^{2}+r_{-}^{2}}{8G_{N}l^{2}},\ \ \ \ J=\frac{r_{+}r_{-}}{4G_{N}l}.

(6.17)

According to the AdS/CFT correspondence, the BTZ black hole with mass
M
and angular momentum
J
corresponds to the following state
[
61
]
:

h-\frac{c}{24}=\frac{1}{2}(Ml-J)=\frac{(r_{+}+r_{-})^{2}}{16G_{N}l},\ \ \ \ \ \bar{h}-\frac{c}{24}=\frac{1}{2}(Ml+J)=\frac{(r_{+}-r_{-})^{2}}{16G_{N}l}.

(6.18)

With these relations, the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy can be expressed as

S=\frac{2\pi r_{+}}{4G_{N}}=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c}{6}\left(h-\frac{c}{24}\right)}+2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c}{6}\left(\bar{h}-\frac{c}{24}\right)}.

(6.19)

This perfectly matches (
6.14
).

6.2
Hellerman Bound and Pure Gravity

Consider Einstein gravity without any matter (called pure gravity),

S_{EH}=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{N}}\int d^{3}x\ \sqrt{g}\left(R+\frac{2}{l^{2}}\right).

(6.20)

The solutions to the Einstein equation include the thermal AdS (i.e. the empty AdS) and the BTZ black holes.
The CFT state corresponding to the empty AdS has a conformal dimension
\Delta=0
.
On the other hand, according to the AdS/CFT correspondence, the BTZ black hole with mass
M
and angular momentum
J
corresponds to the state given by (
6.18
):

h-\frac{c}{24}=\frac{1}{2}(Ml-J),\ \ \ \ \ \bar{h}-\frac{c}{24}=\frac{1}{2}(Ml+J).

(6.21)

The condition for the black hole to have a smooth horizon is simply written by
r_{\pm}\geq 0
.
This can be translated using the relation (
6.17
) as

\left|J\right|\leq Ml.

(6.22)

This condition is called the
cosmic censorship hypothesis
.
The cosmic censorship hypothesis implies that states interpreted as black holes are expected to satisfy the following inequality:

h\geq\frac{c}{24},\ \ \ \ \ \ \bar{h}\geq\frac{c}{24}.

(6.23)

Since no matter exists by assumption, the spectrum of the CFT dual to pure gravity must have a large gap proportional to
c
:

\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{0}=\frac{c}{12}.

(6.24)

Here,
\Delta_{i}
is the
i
-th excited state (
\Delta_{0}<\Delta_{1}<\cdots
).
Note that
\Delta_{0}=0
for the empty AdS, and
\Delta_{1}=\frac{c}{12}
which is the lower bound on the black hole energy (
6.23
).

Pure gravity is classically allowed,
but it is not clear if it can exist when quantum effects are included.
One of the important tasks is examining the possibility of pure gravity as a consistent theory of quantum gravity,
and the modular bootstrap can help with this task.
Consider the following problem:

What is the upper bound on
\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{0}
?

If the modular bootstrap provides an upper bound such that

\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{0}<\frac{c}{12}

(6.25)

(without the equality), then pure gravity cannot satisfy (
6.24
) and thus cannot exist at the quantum level.
On the other hand, if pure gravity does exist at the quantum level,
since the gap in all existing CFTs is smaller than (
6.24
),
pure gravity is expected to saturate the gap bound:

\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{0}\leq\frac{c}{12}.

(6.26)

This problem of exploring the upper bound of the gap was proposed by Hellerman
[
3
]
and has been tackled by many papers such as
[
4
]
and
[
62
]
. However, it remains an open problem. Hellerman’s work
[
3
]
successfully provided a weaker version of the upper bound (
6.26
), and subsequent research has essentially developed Hellerman’s ideas. Therefore, here we will introduce the ideas from
[
3
]
.

The core idea is to extract an infinite number of equations explicitly by expanding the modular bootstrap equation around the fixed point of the modular transformation,
\beta=2\pi
. Specifically, the following equations are obtained:

\left.\left(\beta\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\beta}\right)^{N}Z(\beta)\right|_{\beta=2\pi}=0,\ \ \ \ N\in 2\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}+1.

(6.27)

As a warm-up, let us consider
N=1
,

\sum_{i}d_{i}\left(\Delta_{i}-\frac{c}{12}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\left(\Delta_{i}-\frac{c}{12}\right)}=0.

(6.28)

Here, we set
\Delta_{0}<\Delta_{1}<\cdots
.
Assuming
\Delta_{0}>\frac{c}{12}
,
all terms on the left side of (
6.28
) would be positive, and their sum would be nonzero, leading to a contradiction.
Therefore, the conformal dimension of the lowest energy state
\Delta_{0}
must satisfy the following inequality:

\Delta_{0}\leq\frac{c}{12}.

(6.29)

For unitary compact CFTs, the vacuum state
\Delta_{0}=0
satisfies this inequality,
so this inequality is not very useful, but it is sufficient to understand the basic idea of Hellerman.
The result of
[
3
]
is simply an extension of this idea to higher
N
.

Consider the case of
N=3
.
For brevity, let
E_{i}\equiv\Delta_{i}-\frac{c}{12}
, then we obtain

\sum_{i}d_{i}\left(E_{i}-6\pi E_{i}^{2}+4\pi^{2}E_{i}^{3}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi E_{i}}=0.

(6.30)

From (
6.28
), the contribution of the first term is zero.
To apply the same logic as (
6.29
),
we want the lowest conformal dimension in the sum to be
\Delta_{1}
.
To do this, we consider removing the contribution of
i=0
from the sum using (
6.28
),

\displaystyle\sum_{i}d_{i}E_{i}\left(-3E_{i}+2\pi E_{i}^{2}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi E_{i}}-\sum_{i}d_{i}E_{i}\left(-3E_{0}+2\pi E_{0}^{2}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi E_{i}}

(6.31)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}d_{i}E_{i}(E_{i}-E_{0})\left(3-2\pi(E_{i}+E_{0})\right)=0.

Assume the following:

3-2\pi(E_{1}+E_{0})<0.

(6.32)

In this case, all terms on the left side of (
6.31
) would be negative, leading to a contradiction with zero.
Consequently, we obtain the upper bound on
\Delta_{1}
so-called the
Hellerman bound
,

Hellerman bound

3-2\pi(E_{1}+E_{0})\geq 0\ \ \ \ \Leftrightarrow E_{1}\leq\frac{3}{2\pi}-E_{0}\ \ \ \ \Leftrightarrow\Delta_{1}\leq\frac{c}{6}+\frac{3}{2\pi}.

(6.33)

Here, note that
\Delta_{1}
is the conformal dimension of the first excited state.
Since every CFT has the energy-momentum tensor with
\Delta=2
,
for large
c
, (
6.33
) is trivially satisfied.
In the AdS/CFT correspondence, descendant states correspond to excitations by diffeomorphisms (i.e. boundary gravitons),
and the empty AdS and the BTZ black holes correspond to primary states.
