Therefore, the upper bound we are really interested in is the upper bound on the conformal dimension of the first excited
primary
state.
To provide an upper bound for the first excited primary state,
one can repeat the previous discussion after replacing the function basis with Virasoro characters:

Z(\beta)=\sum_{i:\mathrm{primary}}d_{i}\left|\chi_{i}(\beta)\right|^{2}.

(6.34)

Since Virasoro characters are more complicated than exponential functions,
the calculation is much harder, but the result is almost the same as (
6.33
).
Readers interested in the generalization to primary states should refer to
[
3
]
.

It is natural to expect that considering higher
N
will provide stronger upper bounds.
Achieving this by hand is almost impossible,
but
[
62
]
does similar work numerically.
In
[
62
]
, they provide the improved upper bound:

\Delta_{1}<\frac{c}{8}.

(6.35)

The study in this direction continues to develop even today;
to the best knowledge of the author,
the best analytical bound is given in
[
63
]
, and the best numerical bound is provided in
[
64
]
.

6.3
Hartman-Keller-Stoica Bound and Black Hole Entropy

In Section
6.1
, we saw how the Cardy formula beautifully reproduces the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy. However, there is a crucial difference between the Cardy formula and the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy: their applicable ranges.

Cardy Formula

S\simeq 2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c}{3}\left(\Delta-\frac{c}{12}\right)}\ \ \ \ \ (\Delta\gg c).

(6.36)

Bekenstein-Hawking Entropy

S\simeq 2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c}{3}\left(\Delta-\frac{c}{12}\right)}\ \ \ \ \ (\Delta>\frac{c}{12},\ \ c\gg 1).

(6.37)

The Cardy formula holds for any unitary compact CFT, not limited to holographic CFTs. On the other hand, the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy formula is not expected to hold for general CFTs. Therefore, some specific properties of holographic CFTs must be responsible for the extension of the validity region of the Cardy formula. This condition for the extended validity region was partially investigated by Hartman, Keller, and Stoica
[
65
]
. This section explains their results.

Consider dividing the spectrum into two regions by a small positive real number
\epsilon\ll 1
,

Z_{L}(\beta)\equiv\sum_{-\frac{c}{12}\leq E\leq\epsilon}d_{E}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta E},\ \ \ \ \ Z_{H}(\beta)\equiv\sum_{\epsilon<E}d_{E}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta E}.

(6.38)

From the modular bootstrap equation, we have

Z_{L}(\beta)-Z_{L}\left(\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}\right)=Z_{H}\left(\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}\right)-Z_{H}(\beta).

(6.39)

For
\beta>2\pi
, we obtain the following inequality,

Z_{H}(\beta)=\sum_{\epsilon<E}\mathrm{e}^{\left(\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}-\beta\right)E}d_{E}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}E}\leq\varepsilon Z_{H}\left(\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}\right),

(6.40)

where we define
\varepsilon\equiv\mathrm{e}^{\left(\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}-\beta\right)\epsilon}
, which satisfies
0<\varepsilon<1
for
\beta>2\pi
.
Subtracting
\varepsilon Z_{H}(\beta)
from both sides and using the modular bootstrap equation, we get

(1-\varepsilon)Z_{H}(\beta)\leq\varepsilon\left(Z_{H}\left(\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}\right)-Z_{H}(\beta)\right)=\varepsilon\left(Z_{L}(\beta)-Z_{L}\left(\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}\right)\right)\leq\varepsilon Z_{L}(\beta).

(6.41)

Adding
(1-\varepsilon)Z_{L}(\beta)
to both sides, we finally obtain

Z(\beta)\leq\frac{Z_{L}(\beta)}{1-\varepsilon}.

(6.42)

By adding a trivial lower bound to this inequality, we get

\log Z_{L}(\beta)\leq\log Z(\beta)\leq\log Z_{L}(\beta)-\log(1-\varepsilon).

(6.43)

Rewrite
Z_{L}(\beta)
as

Z_{L}(\beta)=\mathrm{e}^{\beta\frac{c}{12}}\int^{\frac{c}{12}+\epsilon}_{0}d\Delta\ \rho(\Delta)\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta}.

(6.44)

If the density of states satisfies

\rho(\Delta)<\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\Delta},\ \ \ \ \Delta<\frac{c}{12}+\epsilon,

(6.45)

then, for
\beta>2\pi
, we have

\log\left(\int^{\frac{c}{12}+\epsilon}_{0}d\Delta\ \rho(\Delta)\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta}\right)=O(c^{0})

(6.46)

so in the large
c
limit, the partition function can be approximated by the vacuum contribution,

\log Z_{L}(\beta)\simeq\beta\frac{c}{12}.

(6.47)

In (
6.43
), since
\log(1-\varepsilon)=O(c^{0})
, by the squeeze theorem, we get

\log Z(\beta)\simeq\beta\frac{c}{12}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ (\beta>2\pi,\ c\gg 1).

(6.48)

This result indicates that the vacuum approximation region extends from
\beta\gg 1
to
\beta>2\pi
in the large
c
limit, provided (
6.45
) is satisfied. This extension of the vacuum approximation region leads to the extended validity region of the Cardy formula as follows:

HKS formula

S\simeq 2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c}{3}\left(\Delta-\frac{c}{12}\right)}\ \ \ \ \ (\Delta>\frac{c}{6},\ \ c\gg 1).

(6.49)

The validity region
\Delta>\frac{c}{6}
is smaller than the expected range
\Delta>\frac{c}{12}
, but it is significantly extended compared to
\Delta\gg c
. Thus, it is understood that the spectrum of holographic CFTs must at least satisfy (
6.45
). This upper bound on the density of states is called the
Hartman-Keller-Stoica (HKS) bound
. To achieve the extension of the validity region to
\Delta>\frac{c}{12}
, conditions stronger than the HKS bound are required, but the exact conditions that lead to
\Delta>\frac{c}{12}
are still unknown.

Previously we have derived in the
c\to\infty
limit

\log Z(\beta)\sim\begin{cases}\frac{c}{12}\beta\,,&\quad\beta>2\pi\,,\\
\frac{c}{12}\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\beta}\,,&\quad\beta<2\pi.\end{cases}

(6.50)

Thus, the black hole saddle dominates below
\beta=2\pi
and gives rise to the black hole entropy formula (
6.49
) in the microcanonical ensemble. Above
\beta=2\pi
, the thermal AdS dominates.

Now what happens if we turn on both
\beta
and
\bar{\beta}
with
\beta\neq\bar{\beta}
? The partition function
Z(\beta,\bar{\beta})
becomes sensitive to both
h
and
\bar{h}
, particularly, on the spectrum of twist, defined as
2\text{min}(h,\bar{h})
. In this setup, to derive the universality of free energy in the large
c
limit, it is not enough to assume the sparseness condition in (
6.45
). On top of that, one needs to assume the sparseness of the states with twist below
c/12
i.e.

\rho(h,\bar{h})<e^{4\pi\sqrt{h\bar{h}}}\,,\quad\text{min}(h,\bar{h})\leq\frac{c}{24}.

(6.51)

The above implies
\rho(h,\bar{h})<e^{2\pi(h+\bar{h})}
and ensures

\log\left(\int_{0\leq\text{min}(h,\bar{h})\leq c/24}dh\ d\bar{h}\ \rho(h,\bar{h})\mathrm{e}^{-\beta h-\bar{\beta}\bar{h}}\right)=O(c^{0})\,,\quad\beta\bar{\beta}>4\pi^{2}\,.

(6.52)

Under these assumptions, given in (
6.45
) and (
6.51
), HKS conjectured

\log Z(\beta,\bar{\beta})\sim\begin{cases}\frac{c}{24}(\beta+\bar{\beta})\,,&\quad\beta\bar{\beta}>4\pi^{2}\,,\\
\frac{c}{24}\left(4\pi^{2}/\beta+4\pi^{2}/\bar{\beta}\right)\,,&\quad\beta\bar{\beta}<4\pi^{2}.\end{cases}

(6.53)

The proof of this conjecture is non-trivial and has only recently been proven in
[
66
]
. The above produces the Cardy formula for the black hole entropy in the following extended regime:

\left(h-\frac{c}{24}\right)\left(\bar{h}-\frac{c}{24}\right)>\left(\frac{c}{24}\right)^{2}.

(6.54)

7
Holographic CFT and Conformal Bootstrap

In this section, we consider CFTs satisfy the following conditions:

•

c>1

•

compact: discrete spectrum including the vacuum state

•

unitary

•

twist gap:
\min(h,\bar{h})>0
for any non-vacuum state

This particularly implies that there are no symmetries other than the Virasoro symmetry.

The last condition, the twist gap, is a newly added condition.
Note that a CFT satisfying these conditions is necessarily irrational.
In actual research on solving holographic CFTs using conformal bootstrap,
these assumptions are often made.
In the following, we will refer to CFTs with these properties as
pure CFT
.

54
54

54

In Section
4
, we examined specific examples of CFT,
but none of them are pure CFTs.
For example, minimal models and WZW models are not irrational,
the free boson CFT lacks a twist gap,
and Liouville CFT is non-compact.
In fact, no pure CFTs have been found among CFTs whose exact CFT data are known.
Thus, constructing explicit examples of pure CFTs remains one of the significant challenges.
Recently, there has been notable progress on this problem.
Considering the RG flow from a tensor product of
N
copies of unitary minimal models,
Antunes and Behan have provided strong evidence that a CFT obtained at its IR fixed point
satisfies the criteria for being a pure CFT
[
67
,
68
]
.

The advantage of considering pure CFT is that all Verma modules except the vacuum are non-degenerate (see around (
2.63
)).
This fact leads to the following simplification,

\chi_{h}(\tau)=\sum_{n}d(n)\mathrm{e}^{2\pi i\tau\left(h-\frac{c}{24}\right)}=\sum_{n}p(n)\mathrm{e}^{2\pi i\tau\left(h-\frac{c}{24}\right)}=\frac{q^{h-\frac{c-1}{24}}}{\eta(\tau)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{if }h>0.

(7.1)

Also, since the vacuum has a level
1
null state,

\chi_{0}(\tau)=\frac{q^{h-\frac{c-1}{24}}}{\eta(\tau)}(1-q).

(7.2)

In this section, we explain how to analytically solve the conformal bootstrap in the asymptotic region using these facts.

7.1
Warm-up: Cardy Formula

In Section
6.1
, we derived the density of states in high-energy regime.
Let us generalize this to the density of high-energy
primary
states.
There are several ways to derive the Cardy formula for primary states,
a modern method uses the fusion transformations
[
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
]
.

For convenience, we introduce the Liouville notations:

c\equiv 1+6Q^{2},\ \ \ Q\equiv b+\frac{1}{b},\ \ \ h_{i}\equiv\alpha_{i}(Q-\alpha_{i})=\frac{Q^{2}}{4}+\gamma_{i}^{2}.

(7.3)

\gamma
is the imaginary part of the Liouville momentum
\alpha=\frac{Q}{2}+i\gamma
.
Since it is more convenient to use the momentum
\gamma
rather than the Liouville momentum
\alpha
to explicitly express modular transformation properties,
we use
\gamma
in the following.

We write the modular bootstrap equation using the Virasoro character basis,

\int_{0}^{\infty}d\gamma\ \int_{0}^{\infty}d\bar{\gamma}\ \rho(\gamma,\bar{\gamma})\left|\chi_{\gamma}(\tau)\right|^{2}=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\gamma\ \int_{0}^{\infty}d\bar{\gamma}\ \rho(\gamma,\bar{\gamma})\left|\chi_{\gamma}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)\right|^{2}.

(7.4)

Here, the density of primary states
\rho(\gamma,\bar{\gamma})
is defined using the number of primary states
d_{i}
as follows,

\rho(\gamma,\bar{\gamma})=\sum_{i}d_{i}\delta(\gamma-\gamma_{i})\delta(\bar{\gamma}-\bar{\gamma}_{i}).

(7.5)

Taking the
\tau,\bar{\tau}\to i0
limit of the modular bootstrap equation,
the right-hand side can be approximated by the contribution from the vacuum state,

\int_{0}^{\infty}d\gamma\ \int_{0}^{\infty}d\bar{\gamma}\ \rho(\gamma,\bar{\gamma})\left|\chi_{\gamma}(\tau)\right|^{2}\simeq\left|\chi_{\mathbb{I}}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)\right|^{2},\ \ \ \ \ \tau,\bar{\tau}\to i0.

(7.6)

We consider re-expanding both sides in the same functional basis using the fusion transformation.
The Virasoro character with
c>1,h\geq 0
has the following
S
modular transformation property,

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathbb{I}}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\int^{\infty}_{0}d\gamma^{\prime}\ S_{\mathbb{I}\gamma^{\prime}}\chi_{\gamma^{\prime}}\left(\tau\right),\ \ \ S_{\mathbb{I}\gamma^{\prime}}=4\sqrt{2}\sinh\left(2\pi\gamma^{\prime}/b\right)\sinh\left(2\pi\gamma^{\prime}b\right),

(7.7)

\displaystyle\chi_{\gamma}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\int^{\infty}_{0}d\gamma^{\prime}\ S_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}\chi_{\gamma^{\prime}}\left(\tau\right),\ \ \ S_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}=2\sqrt{2}\cos\left(4\pi\gamma\gamma^{\prime}\right).

S_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}
is a fusion matrix, specifically called the
modular
S
matrix
.
It should be noted that the above formula for the modular
S
matrix only holds for non-degenerate Verma modules,
and it does not hold in minimal models.
Using this modular
S
transformation to rewrite (
7.6
), we obtain the following asymptotic equation,

\int_{0}^{\infty}d\gamma\ \int_{0}^{\infty}d\bar{\gamma}\ \rho(\gamma,\bar{\gamma})\left|\chi_{\gamma}(\tau)\right|^{2}\simeq\int_{0}^{\infty}d\gamma\ \int_{0}^{\infty}d\bar{\gamma}\ S_{\mathbb{I}\gamma}S_{\mathbb{I}\bar{\gamma}}\left|\chi_{\gamma}(\tau)\right|^{2}.

(7.8)

By comparing the coefficients, we get

\rho(\gamma,\bar{\gamma})\simeq S_{\mathbb{I}\gamma}S_{\mathbb{I}\bar{\gamma}},\ \ \ \ \ \gamma,\bar{\gamma}\gg Q.

(7.9)

The asymptotic form of the modular
S
matrix is given by

S_{\mathbb{I}\gamma}\simeq\sqrt{2}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi Q\gamma}=\sqrt{2}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c-1}{6}\left(h-\frac{c-1}{24}\right)}},\ \ \ \ \gamma,\bar{\gamma}\gg Q.

(7.10)

Thus, (
7.9
) can be rewritten in terms of the conformal dimensions as follows.

55
55

55

It may seem strange that the Cardy formula becomes a continuous spectrum even though we assumed a discrete spectrum for pure CFT.
This is because we ignored all approximation errors.
One way to handle this accurately is to interpret that this asymptotic formula treats the spectrum averaged over a width
2\delta
,
\rho_{\delta}(h)\equiv\int_{h-\delta}^{h+\delta}dh\rho(h)
,
and explicitly write the error due to this averaging.
This can be investigated using the Tauberian theorem, and it can be shown that the error in
\log\rho
is
O(1)
[
73
]
.

Cardy formula for primary states

\log\rho(h,\bar{h})\simeq 2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c-1}{6}\left(h-\frac{c-1}{24}\right)}+2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c-1}{6}\left(\bar{h}-\frac{c-1}{24}\right)},\ \ \ \ \ h,\bar{h}\gg c.

(7.11)

While the original Cardy formula (
6.14
) represented the total number of states with conformal dimensions
(h,\bar{h})
,
The formula (
7.11
) represents the number of
primary
states with conformal dimensions
(h,\bar{h})
.
From this expression, we see that the only difference from the original Cardy formula is the shift
c\to c-1
.
In particular, both match in the limit
c\to\infty
.
The reason for this is clear when we recall that the number of descendant states is given by the Hardy-Ramanujan formula (
2.42
).
Since the asymptotic form of the Hardy-Ramanujan formula does not depend on
c
, the number of descendants can be overwhelmingly small compared to the number of primaries in the large
c
limit.
This is why the original Cardy formula (
6.14
) and the Cardy formula for primary fields (
7.11
) coincide in the large
c
limit.

7.2
Generalized Cardy Formula

Although the definition of pure CFT included “twist gap”,
the discussions so far have not actually used the assumption of twist gap at all.
What can we gain by assuming the twist gap?
This section explains that.

Twist is a parameter defined as follows,

t=2\min(h,\bar{h}).

(7.12)

When every primary state except the vacuum state in the CFT spectrum satisfies
t>0
, we say that the CFT has a twist gap.
This implies the absence of primary fields with conformal dimensions
(h>0,0)
or
(0,\bar{h}>0)
(called the
chiral primaries
).
Since currents are chiral primary, the presence of a twist gap particularly means the absence of symmetries other than the Virasoro symmetry.

If there is a twist gap,
\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{I}}
only couples with
\bar{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbb{I}}
, allowing a vacuum approximation in the limit
\bar{\tau}\to i0
.

\int_{0}^{\infty}d\gamma\ \int_{0}^{\infty}d\bar{\gamma}\ \rho(\gamma,\bar{\gamma})\left|\chi_{\gamma}(\tau)\right|^{2}\simeq\left|\chi_{\mathbb{I}}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)\right|^{2},\ \ \ \ \ \bar{\tau}\to i0.

(7.13)

The difference from (
7.6
) is that the limit with respect to
\tau
is not taken.
By comparing the coefficients, we get the Cardy formula again
[
69
,
71
]
,

Generalized Cardy formula

\log\rho(h,\bar{h})\simeq 2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c-1}{6}\left(h-\frac{c-1}{24}\right)}+2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c-1}{6}\left(\bar{h}-\frac{c-1}{24}\right)},\ \ \ \ \ \bar{h}\gg c.

(7.14)

This formula holds for any
h
.
In this sense, it is sometimes called the
generalized Cardy formula

[
50
]
.
Moreover, fixing
h
and taking the limit
\bar{h}\to\infty
is equivalent to taking the limit of infinite spin
s=\left|h-\bar{h}\right|\to\infty
,
which is called the
large spin limit
.
In short, while the Cardy formula was for the density of states at high energy,
the generalized Cardy formula is for the density of states at high spin.

7.3
Conformal Bootstrap of Four-Point Functions

Here, we use the Liouville momentum
\alpha
again.
Unlike Liouville CFT, the spectrum can include primary fields with conformal dimensions
h<\frac{c-1}{24}
.
Therefore, we analytically continue the range of possible values for the Liouville momentum to the following region:

\alpha\in\left\{\frac{Q}{2}+i\mathbb{R}_{+}\right\}\cup\left[0,\frac{Q}{2}\right).

(7.15)

Let us consider the conformal bootstrap equation for four-point functions.
As in (
7.5
), we define the
OPE coefficient density
to rewrite the sum as an integral,

\sum_{p}C_{12p}C_{34p}\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{p}|z)\overline{\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}}(\bar{h}_{p}|{\bar{z}})=\int d\alpha\int d\bar{\alpha}\mathcal{C}^{21}_{34}(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(\alpha|z)\overline{\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}}(\alpha|{\bar{z}}),

(7.16)

where the OPE coefficient density
\mathcal{C}^{21}_{34}(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})
is defined as follows using the number of primary states
d_{i}
,

\mathcal{C}^{21}_{34}(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})\equiv\sum_{i}d_{i}C_{12i}C_{34_{i}}\delta(\alpha-\alpha_{i})\delta(\bar{\alpha}-\bar{\alpha}_{i}).

(7.17)

In this way, the conformal bootstrap equation is expressed as follows.

\int d\alpha\int d\bar{\alpha}\mathcal{C}^{21}_{34}(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(\alpha|z)\overline{\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}}(\alpha|{\bar{z}})=\int d\alpha^{\prime}\int d\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}^{23}_{14}(\alpha^{\prime},\bar{\alpha}^{\prime})\mathcal{F}^{23}_{14}(\alpha^{\prime}|1-z)\overline{\mathcal{F}^{23}_{14}}(\bar{\alpha}^{\prime}|1-{\bar{z}}).

(7.18)

The solution method is basically the same as for the Cardy formula.
In the following, to make the vacuum approximation, we consider the case (
1=4
and
2=3
) where the vacuum is possible in the OPE.
With the vacuum approximation on the right-hand side of the conformal bootstrap equation in the limit
z,{\bar{z}}\to 1
,
we consider the following fusion transformation (
4.45
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{23}_{14}(h_{\alpha}|1-z)=\int_{\frac{Q}{2}+i0}^{\frac{Q}{2}+i\infty}d\alpha{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{3}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{\alpha^{\prime}}|z).

(7.19)

Then we obtain the asymptotic conformal bootstrap equation,

56
56

56

{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},\alpha}
can be simply obtained by analytically continuing
{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}
to
\alpha^{\prime}\to i\frac{Q}{2}
.
As a side note, when performing a similar analytic continuation for the modular
S
matrix, care must be taken to properly handle the divergence from null states.

\int d\alpha\int d\bar{\alpha}\mathcal{C}^{21}_{21}(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})\left|\mathcal{F}^{21}_{21}(\alpha|z)\right|^{2}\simeq\int d\alpha\int d\bar{\alpha}{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},\alpha}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},\bar{\alpha}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}&\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}\\
\bar{{\alpha}}_{1}&\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}\\
\end{array}\right]\left|\mathcal{F}^{21}_{21}(\alpha|z)\right|^{2}.

(7.20)

Thus, we obtain the asymptotic formula for the OPE coefficient density,

\mathcal{C}^{21}_{21}(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})\simeq{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},\alpha}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},\bar{\alpha}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}&\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}\\
\bar{{\alpha}}_{1}&\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}\\
\end{array}\right],\ \ \ \ \ \left|{\alpha}\right|,\left|\bar{{\alpha}}\right|\gg Q.

(7.21)

Using this result and the density of states (
7.9
), we can define the average value of the OPE coefficient as follows,

\overline{\left(C_{123}\right)^{2}}\equiv\frac{\mathcal{C}^{21}_{21}(\alpha_{3},\bar{\alpha}_{3})}{\rho(\alpha_{3},\bar{\alpha}_{3})}.

(7.22)

Since the holomorphic and anti-holomorphic parts are separated, it is convenient to introduce the quantity with only the holomorphic part extracted as follows,

C_{0}({\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{2},{\alpha}_{3})\equiv\frac{{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},{\alpha}_{3}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]}{S_{\mathbb{I}{\alpha}_{3}}},

(7.23)

which satisfies
\overline{\left(C_{123}\right)^{2}}=\left|C_{0}({\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{2},{\alpha}_{3})\right|^{2}
.

Let us consider how this quantity is specifically expressed.
In fact, it is known that the fusion matrix we are considering has a simple form,
and as a result,
C_{0}({\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{2},{\alpha}_{3})
can be expressed as follows
[
71
]
.

\displaystyle C_{0}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\alpha_{b}(2Q)}{2\sqrt{2}\pi\alpha_{b}(Q)}\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1,2,3}\Gamma_{b}(2\alpha_{i})\Gamma(2Q-2\alpha_{i})}

(7.24)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Upsilon_{b}^{\prime}(0)}{\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-Q)\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}.

This formula is similar to the DOZZ formula (
4.29
).
Recalling that the DOZZ formula was determined by the fusion matrix (see Section
4.2.1
),
this similarity is inevitable.
The relationship between the two is specifically expressed as follows:

Asymptotic formula for OPE coefficient density

C_{0}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})=\frac{C_{123}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}}{\mathcal{E}\prod_{i=1,2,3}\sqrt{S_{\mathbb{I}\gamma_{i}}B_{i}}}.

(7.25)

Here,
S_{\mathbb{I}{\gamma}}
is the modular
S
matrix, and
B_{i}
is the normalization constant of the two-point function in Liouville CFT (
4.36
).
Also,
\mathcal{E}
is a constant independent of the conformal dimensions:

\mathcal{E}\equiv 2^{\frac{3}{4}}\pi\left(b^{\frac{2}{b}-2b}\lambda\right)^{-\frac{Q}{2}}\frac{\Gamma_{b}(Q)}{\Gamma_{b}(2Q)}.

(7.26)

The detailed derivation of this relationship is described in Appendix
C.2
.

7.4
Lightcone Conformal Bootstrap

Let us apply what we did in the derivation of the generalized Cardy formula (Section
7.2
) to the four-point conformal bootstrap
[
70
,
71
]
.
Again, we assume a twist gap.
In this case, the conformal bootstrap equation can be approximated as follows in the limit
{\bar{z}}\to 1
,

\int d\alpha\int d\bar{\alpha}\mathcal{C}^{21}_{21}(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})\left|\mathcal{F}^{21}_{21}(\alpha|z)\right|^{2}\simeq\int d\alpha\int d\bar{\alpha}{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},\alpha}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},\bar{\alpha}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}&\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}\\
\bar{{\alpha}}_{1}&\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}\\
\end{array}\right]\left|\mathcal{F}^{21}_{21}(\alpha|z)\right|^{2}.

(7.27)

By comparing both sides, we get

\mathcal{C}^{21}_{21}(\alpha,\bar{\alpha})\simeq{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},\alpha}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},\bar{\alpha}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}&\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}\\
\bar{{\alpha}}_{1}&\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}\\
\end{array}\right],\ \ \ \ \ \left|\bar{{\alpha}}\right|\gg Q.

(7.28)

This seems correct at first glance,
but in fact, it is only valid when
\Re(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})>\frac{Q}{2}
.
The reason is that when the fusion transformation is analytically continued to the region
\Re(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})<\frac{Q}{2}
,
the poles of the fusion matrix cross the integral contour
\frac{Q}{2}+0i\sim\frac{Q}{2}+i\infty
.
As a result of the contribution of these residues appearing in the integral transformation (
7.27
),
the OPE spectrum consists of not only a continuous spectrum with
h>\frac{c-1}{24}
but also a discrete spectrum with
h<\frac{c-1}{24}
.
This is explained below.

7.4.1
Poles of Fusion Matrix

Figure 20:
The region where simple poles of the fusion matrix exist on the
\alpha_{t}
plane

Let us specifically look at the pole structure of the fusion matrix

{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},{\alpha}_{t}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]
.
The fusion matrix we are interested in is given by (
7.23
) and (
7.24
).
Since the Barnes double gamma function has simple poles at
x=-mb-\frac{n}{b},\ \ (n,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
,
the fusion matrix has simple poles at the following points on the
\alpha_{t}
plane.

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{t}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=({\alpha}_{1}+{\alpha}_{2})-Q-Q_{m,n},

(7.29)

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{t}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=-({\alpha}_{1}+{\alpha}_{2})+2Q+Q_{m,n},

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{t}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\pm({\alpha}_{1}-{\alpha}_{2})-Q_{m,n},

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{t}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=\pm({\alpha}_{1}-{\alpha}_{2})+Q+Q_{m,n},

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{t}^{(5)}

\displaystyle=-({\alpha}_{1}+{\alpha}_{2})+Q-Q_{m,n},

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{t}^{(6)}

\displaystyle={\alpha}_{1}+{\alpha}_{2}+Q_{m,n}.

Here,
Q_{m,n}\equiv-mb-\frac{n}{b},\ \ (n,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
.
Since the Liouville momentum in unitary theories satisfies
0\leq\Re\alpha_{i}\leq\frac{Q}{2}
,
the possible range of each pole location is restricted as shown in Figure
20
.

From this pole structure,
when the fusion transformation is analytically continued to the region
\Re(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})<\frac{Q}{2}
,
it can be seen that the poles of the fusion matrix cross the integral contour,
resulting in the contribution of residues appearing in the integral transformation (see Figure
21
).
Taking into account the contribution of these residues,
the OPE spectrum (
7.28
) is modified to include the following discrete spectrum,

\mathcal{C}^{21}_{21}(\alpha_{m},\bar{\alpha})\simeq-2\pi\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha=\alpha_{m}}{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},\alpha}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{2}\\
{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},\bar{\alpha}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}&\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}\\
\bar{{\alpha}}_{1}&\bar{{\alpha}}_{2}\\
\end{array}\right],\ \ \ \ \ \left|\bar{{\alpha}}\right|\gg Q.

(7.30)

Here,
\alpha_{m}\equiv\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+mb\ \ \ \ (m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
.
Also, assuming
b\leq\frac{1}{b}
, since
\frac{1}{b}>\frac{Q}{2}
, the poles with
n>0
do not contribute.

Figure 21:
When the fusion transformation is analytically continued to
\Re(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})<\frac{Q}{2}
,
the poles of the fusion matrix cross the integral contour,
resulting in the contribution of residues appearing in the integral transformation.

7.4.2
Interpretation on the Gravity Side

States created by two local operators correspond to two-particle systems on the gravity side.
The asymptotic formula for the OPE spectrum (
7.30
) predicts how the energy spectrum of two-particle systems with large spin is given,

OPE spectrum at large spin

h_{m}=h_{1}+h_{2}+m+\delta h_{m},\ \ \ \ \ \delta h_{m}\equiv-2(\alpha_{1}+mb)(\alpha_{2}+bm)+m(m+1)b^{2}<0,\ \ \ (m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}).

(7.31)

The characteristic point of this energy spectrum is that in addition to the rest masses of the two particles
h_{1}+h_{2}
, there is a binding energy
\delta h_{m}
.
Recalling that the OPE spectrum was determined purely by kinematic information,
it will be clear that this binding energy comes from the exchanging of descendants between the two particles.
Moreover, recalling that the descendants correspond to gravitons on the gravity side,
we can see that this binding energy represents gravitational interaction.

It may seem strange that a large gravitational interaction exists between two particles in the large spin limit (equivalently the long-distance limit).
In fact, the intuition that gravitational interaction disappears in the large spin limit is correct in AdS
d≥4

[
74
,
75
]
.
However, it can be shown that the gravitational potential does not decay as a function of distance in AdS
3
.
As a result, in the limit of infinite spin, finite gravitational interaction remains as in (
7.31
).
Furthermore, the characteristic
\delta_{m}<0
is also important.
This reflects the fact that gravitational interaction is attractive.

The continuous OPE spectrum (
7.28
) reflects the fact that states with
\min(h,\bar{h})>\frac{c}{24}
correspond to the BTZ black holes.
The condition
\Re(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})>\frac{Q}{2}
becomes the BTZ threshold when expressed as the lowest energy of the OPE spectrum,

h_{0}=(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})(Q-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})>\frac{c-1}{24}.

(7.32)

The most challenging part of deriving this asymptotic formula is extracting the necessary information from the complicated special function, i.e. the Virasoro block.
In
[
60
]
, they approximate the Virasoro block in the limit
c\to\infty
and investigate the behavior near
z\to 1
,
identifying the leading term in
c
for (
7.31
).
Since the limit
(z,{\bar{z}})\to(0,1)
that appears in this derivation corresponds to the limit
t\to x
when analytically continued to Lorentzian time as
z\equiv x+i\tau
,
it is called the
lightcone limit
.
In the present derivation, we only took the limit
{\bar{z}}\to 0
without taking the limit
z\to 1
,
but it is conventionally called the
lightcone conformal bootstrap
.

7.5
General Conformal Bootstrap and Asymptotic Formula for OPE Coefficients

Here, we derive various asymptotic formulas for OPE coefficients in different asymptotic regions by considering more general conformal bootstrap equations.
While the derivation method using the inverse Laplace transformation as in Section
6.1
provided separate formulas for each asymptotic region
[
76
,
15
,
77
,
14
,
78
,
79
,
80
,
81
,
82
]
,
the derivation using the fusion transformation can handle them uniformly
[
72
]
.
The key to solving general conformal bootstrap equations using the fusion transformation is the Moore-Seiberg construction
[
18
]
.
The Moore-Seiberg construction asserts that any fusion can be constructed from modular transformations of one-point functions on the torus and fusion transformations of four-point functions.

Modular transformation of one-point functions on the torus

\parbox{113.81102pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=113.81102pt]{Zs.pdf}}}=\int^{\infty}_{0}d\gamma_{q}S_{\gamma_{p},\gamma_{q}}[{\gamma}_{i}]\parbox{113.81102pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=113.81102pt]{Zt.pdf}}}.

(7.33)

Fusion transformation of four-point functions

\parbox{113.81102pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=113.81102pt]{Fs.pdf}}}=\int^{\infty}_{0}d\gamma_{q}{\mathbb{F}}_{{\gamma}_{p},{\gamma}_{q}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\gamma}_{j}&{\gamma}_{i}\\
{\gamma}_{k}&{\gamma}_{l}\end{array}\right]\parbox{113.81102pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=113.81102pt]{Ft.pdf}}}.

(7.34)

Here, the red lines represent the projection operator onto a Verma module.

As an example, we show how the fusion transformation of two-point functions on the torus is generated,

\displaystyle\parbox{142.26378pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=142.26378pt]{FZss.pdf}}}=\int^{\infty}_{0}d\gamma_{q^{\prime}}S_{\gamma_{q},\gamma_{q^{\prime}}}[{\gamma}_{p}]\parbox{142.26378pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=142.26378pt]{FZst.pdf}}}

(7.35)

\displaystyle=\int^{\infty}_{0}d\gamma_{q^{\prime}}\int^{\infty}_{0}d\gamma_{p^{\prime}}S_{\gamma_{q},\gamma_{q^{\prime}}}[{\gamma}_{p}]{\mathbb{F}}_{{\gamma}_{p},{\gamma}_{p^{\prime}}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\gamma}_{j}&{\gamma}_{i}\\
{\gamma}_{q^{\prime}}&{\gamma}_{q^{\prime}}\end{array}\right]\parbox{142.26378pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=142.26378pt]{FZtt.pdf}}}.

From this fusion transformation and the following asymptotic conformal bootstrap equation

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{0}d\gamma_{p^{\prime}}\int^{\infty}_{0}d\bar{\gamma}_{p^{\prime}}\int^{\infty}_{0}d\gamma_{q^{\prime}}\int^{\infty}_{0}d\bar{\gamma}_{q^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}_{ii}({\gamma}_{p^{\prime}},\bar{{\gamma}}_{p^{\prime}},{\gamma}_{q^{\prime}},\bar{{\gamma}}_{q^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\left|\parbox{113.81102pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=113.81102pt]{twos.pdf}}}\right|^{2}

(7.36)

\displaystyle\simeq\left|\parbox{113.81102pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=113.81102pt]{twot.pdf}}}\right|^{2},

we obtain the following asymptotic formula for the OPE coefficient density,

\mathcal{C}_{ii}({\gamma}_{p},\bar{{\gamma}}_{p},{\gamma}_{q},\bar{{\gamma}}_{q})\simeq\left|S_{\mathbb{I}\gamma_{q}}{\mathbb{F}}_{{\gamma}_{\mathbb{I}},{\gamma}_{p}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\gamma}_{i}&{\gamma}_{i}\\
{\gamma}_{q}&{\gamma}_{q}\end{array}\right]\right|^{2},\ \ \ \ \ {\gamma}_{p},\bar{{\gamma}}_{p},{\gamma}_{q},\bar{{\gamma}}_{q}\gg Q.

(7.37)

Here,
\mathcal{C}_{ii}({\gamma}_{p},\bar{{\gamma}}_{p},{\gamma}_{q},\bar{{\gamma}}_{q})
is defined as follows:

\mathcal{C}_{ii}({\gamma}_{p},\bar{{\gamma}}_{p},{\gamma}_{q},\bar{{\gamma}}_{q})\equiv\sum_{j,k}d_{j}d_{k}C_{ijk}^{2}\delta({\gamma}_{p}-{\gamma}_{j})\delta({\gamma}_{q}-{\gamma}_{k})\delta(\bar{{\gamma}}_{p}-\bar{{\gamma}}_{j})\delta(\bar{{\gamma}}_{q}-\bar{{\gamma}}_{k}).

(7.38)

As with (
7.22
), we define the average value of the OPE coefficient as follows:

\overline{C_{123}^{2}}\equiv\frac{\mathcal{C}_{11}({\gamma}_{2},\bar{{\gamma}}_{2},{\gamma}_{3},\bar{{\gamma}}_{3})}{\rho(\gamma_{2},\bar{\gamma_{2}})\rho(\gamma_{3},\bar{\gamma_{3}})}.

(7.39)

One can find that the asymptotic formula is exactly the same as (
7.23
), except for the range of applications,

\overline{C_{123}^{2}}\simeq\left|C_{0}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\right|^{2},\ \ \ \ \ {\alpha}_{2},\bar{{\alpha}}_{2},{\alpha}_{3},\bar{{\alpha}}_{3}\gg Q.

(7.40)

Here,
\gamma
is rewritten as the Liouville momentum
\alpha
using
h_{i}\equiv\alpha_{i}(Q-\alpha_{i})=\frac{Q^{2}}{4}+\gamma_{i}^{2}
.

By performing the same procedure with the genus 2 correlation function, the following asymptotic formula can be obtained,

\overline{C_{123}^{2}}\simeq\left|C_{0}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\right|^{2},\ \ \ \ \ {\alpha}_{1},\bar{{\alpha}}_{1},{\alpha}_{2},\bar{{\alpha}}_{2},{\alpha}_{3},\bar{{\alpha}}_{3}\gg Q.

(7.41)

Moreover, assuming a twist gap, the asymptotic formula for the OPE coefficients can be generalized as we derived the generalized Cardy formula in Section
7.2
.
To summarize these discussions, all the asymptotic formulas for the OPE coefficients can be summarized as follows:

Unified asymptotic formula for OPE coefficients

\overline{C_{123}^{2}}\simeq\left|C_{0}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\right|^{2},\ \ \ \ \ \max({\alpha}_{1},\bar{{\alpha}}_{1},{\alpha}_{2},\bar{{\alpha}}_{2},{\alpha}_{3},\bar{{\alpha}}_{3})\gg Q.

(7.42)

In short,
the question ‘‘What is universal and what depends on the theory in CFT data?”,
has the answer ‘‘All OPE coefficients in the high-energy region are universal.”

57
57

57

Strictly speaking, it assumes
{\alpha}_{1}\neq{\alpha}_{2}\neq{\alpha}_{3}
.
If one considers a limit such as
{\alpha}_{1}={\alpha}_{2}\to\infty
,
the asymptotic form of the OPE coefficients depends on the theory
[
78
]
.

Below, we summarize comments on this result.

•

As in the result of HKS (see Section
6.3
),
it is expected that the applicable range of the asymptotic formula for the OPE coefficients (
7.42
) extends in holographic CFTs.
However, unlike the case of the Cardy formula,
it is already known that simply providing an upper bound on the OPE coefficients of light operators (i.e. an analog of the HKS bound) is insufficient
[
83
]
.
Clarifying under what assumptions the applicability region expands remains an open question.

•

It can be shown that (
7.42
) is consistent with the Eigenstate Thermalization Hypothesis (ETH).
This is considered to reflect the chaotic nature of holographic CFTs.
Refining this ETH in holographic CFTs is one of the challenges in this field.

7.6
Application of Asymptotic OPE Coefficients: AdS/CFT and Averaging

In this section, we discuss the role of the asymptotic formula for OPE coefficients in AdS/CFT.

It has been conjectured that

The holographic CFT is not a single CFT, but rather an average over a certain ensemble of CFTs (so-called
averaged CFT
).

This conjecture stems from the relationship between quantum gravity on AdS
2
and random matrix theory
[
84
]
.
Another basis for this conjecture comes from the following:
When considering an AdS spacetime with two asymptotic boundaries,
a wormhole solution exists connecting these two boundaries.
In other words, there is a correlation between the two asymptotic boundaries.
However, if we regard the asymptotic boundaries as CFTs,
then this should simply be the product of two CFTs,
and the asymptotic boundaries should be completely decoupled,

Z_{\mathrm{grav}}({\partial}M=\Sigma_{1}\times\Sigma_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}Z_{\mathrm{CFT}}(\Sigma_{1})\times Z_{\mathrm{CFT}}(\Sigma_{2}).

(7.43)

Here,
Z_{\mathrm{grav}}({\partial}M)
represents the partition function of quantum gravity on AdS with the asymptotic boundary
{\partial}M
,
and
Z_{\mathrm{CFT}}(\Sigma)
represents the partition function of the CFT defined on the manifold
\Sigma
.
The issue that the wormhole solution contained in
Z_{\mathrm{grav}}({\partial}M)
breaks the equality (
7.43
) is known as the
factorization problem
.
One proposed resolution to this issue is through averaging.
In other words, by averaging, correlations are induced between the two CFTs,

Z_{\mathrm{grav}}({\partial}M=\Sigma_{1}\times\Sigma_{2})=\overline{Z_{\mathrm{CFT}}(\Sigma_{1})\times Z_{\mathrm{CFT}}(\Sigma_{2})}.

(7.44)

However, the AdS/CFT constructed from a top-down approach via string theory clearly does not include any averaging operation.
Therefore, it is natural to consider that this averaging appears from the coarse-graining in the effective theory in the classical limit.
However, the mechanism remains unknown and is actively studied as one of the mysteries of the AdS/CFT correspondence.

In the above discussion, averaging was introduced abstractly,
but clarifying which specific averaging of CFTs produces the effective theory is likely one of the first tasks to be undertaken.
Thus, we consider the following as a concrete problem:

How are solutions beyond the thermal AdS and BTZ, particularly wormhole solutions, reproduced from averaged CFT?

Let us define the OPE coefficients of averaged CFT as random variables.
First, assume that the correlation between conical defects on AdS is only through gravitational interaction.
In other words, we consider pure AdS where there are no particles mediating between conical defects.
In the CFT language, this assumption translates to the following:

\overline{C_{ijk}}=0.

(7.45)

Next, since the OPE coefficients satisfy the conformal bootstrap equation,
they need to be consistent with (
7.42
) in particular.
Since we are now taking the large
c
limit, the applicable range of the asymptotic formula for OPE coefficients extends to
\{h_{i}\}=O(c)
.
However, as mentioned in Section
6.3
, extending the applicable range for the asymptotic formula is technically challenging
[
85
,
14
]
,
and the exact range of applicability remains unclear.
Therefore, we assume the following instead:

58
58

58

To avoid technical explanations, some assumptions have been omitted.
Readers interested in the precise assumptions should refer to
[
86
]
.

(
7.42
) holds if all three weights satisfy
h>\frac{c}{32}
.

Here,
\frac{c}{32}
corresponds to the BTZ threshold of
\alpha_{1}
when
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
in (
7.32
).
In other words, we assume that (
7.42
) holds for the OPE coefficients corresponding to black hole formation.
This is a natural extension of the expectation (”expected” HKS formula) that the Cardy formula holds up to the BTZ threshold.
Thus, we obtain the following conjecture for the variance of the OPE coefficients:

\overline{C_{ijk}C_{lmn}}=\left|C_{0}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})\right|^{2}\times\left(\delta_{il}\delta_{jm}\delta_{kn}\pm\mathrm{permutations}\right).

(7.46)

Here, we assume that the OPE coefficients follow the Gaussian distribution.
It is important to note that the ensemble introduced here is an ensemble of CFT data, not an ensemble of CFTs.
Ultimately, it will be necessary to clarify what kind of ensemble of CFTs leads to the OPE coefficient random variables introduced here,
but for now, we assume the existence of some ensemble of CFTs that leads to the random variables (
7.46
).
It is worth emphasizing that assuming the existence of such an ensemble is not so unnatural, given that any large
c
CFT satisfies (
7.42
).

Let us reproduce the wormhole solution between two asymptotic boundaries from CFT.
As a simple example, consider the case where the asymptotic boundaries are spheres.
A wormhole solution is possible when more than two local operators are inserted at the asymptotic boundary.
By calculating the Euclidean action of the wormhole solution with three conical defects, it can be shown that the following equality holds:

C_{0}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})^{2}=\parbox{113.81102pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=113.81102pt]{worma.pdf}}}.

(7.47)

In other words, the average of the product of three-point functions coincides with the wormhole solution.
Here, the black line in the diagram represents the conical defect propagating through the wormhole.
As a slightly nontrivial example, let us consider the four-point function,

\braket{\phi_{4}(\infty)\phi_{3}(1)\phi_{2}(z)\phi_{1}(0)}\braket{\phi_{4}(\infty)\phi_{3}(1)\phi_{2}(z^{\prime})\phi_{1}(0)}=\sum_{p,q}C_{12p}C_{34p}C_{12q}C_{34q}\left|\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(p|z)\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(q|z^{\prime})\right|^{2}.

(7.48)

Taking the average of the product of two correlation functions, only the term with
p=q
remains due to the statistical properties of the OPE coefficients.

\overline{\braket{\phi_{4}(\infty)\phi_{3}(1)\phi_{2}(z)\phi_{1}(0)}\braket{\phi_{4}(\infty)\phi_{3}(1)\phi_{2}(z^{\prime})\phi_{1}(0)}}=\sum_{p}\overline{C_{12p}^{2}}\overline{C_{34p}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(p|z)\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(q|z^{\prime})\right|^{2}.

(7.49)

The large
c
limit of this result matches the calculation of the four-point wormhole solution in gravity.

\sum_{p}\overline{C_{12p}^{2}}\overline{C_{34p}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(p|z)\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(q|z^{\prime})\right|^{2}=\parbox{113.81102pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[width=113.81102pt]{wormb.pdf}}}.

(7.50)

Similarly, it has been confirmed that wormhole solutions between genus 2 partition functions can also be computed from averaged CFT and are consistent with gravity-side calculations
[
86
]
.
In other words, the averaged CFT constructed here effectively describes pure AdS, and in this sense, we have successfully identified the ”averaging” we were seeking.
It is a future challenge to further develop a microscopic understanding while confirming the physical significance of this toy model of averaged CFT,
ultimately leading to a better understanding of the factorization problem.

Ising CFT data

conformal dimension

(h_{\sigma},\bar{h}_{\sigma})=\left(\frac{1}{16},\frac{1}{16}\right),\ \ \ (h_{\epsilon},\bar{h}_{\epsilon})=\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right).\ \ \

(A.1)

OPE coefficient

C_{\sigma\sigma 0}=C_{{\epsilon}{\epsilon}0}=1,\ \ \ C_{\sigma\sigma{\epsilon}}=\frac{1}{2}.

(A.2)

fusion matrix

(+permutation)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{F}}_{0,0}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma&\sigma\\
\sigma&\sigma\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=2{\mathbb{F}}_{0,{\epsilon}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma&\sigma\\
\sigma&\sigma\\
\end{array}\right]=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{F}}_{{\epsilon},0}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma&\sigma\\
\sigma&\sigma\\
\end{array}\right]=-{\mathbb{F}}_{{\epsilon},{\epsilon}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma&\sigma\\
\sigma&\sigma\\
\end{array}\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},

(A.3)

{\epsilon}&{\epsilon}\\
\end{array}\right]

{\epsilon}&{\epsilon}\\
\end{array}\right]=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{F}}_{\sigma,0}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\epsilon}&\sigma\\
{\epsilon}&\sigma\\
\end{array}\right]=-{\mathbb{F}}_{\sigma,\sigma}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\epsilon}&\sigma\\
\sigma&{\epsilon}\\
\end{array}\right]=1.

conformal block

\mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}(0|z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\sqrt{1+\sqrt{1-z}}}{\left(z(1-z)\right)^{\frac{1}{8}}},\ \ \ \mathcal{F}^{\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma\sigma}({\epsilon}|z)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\sqrt{1-\sqrt{1-z}}}{\left(z(1-z)\right)^{\frac{1}{8}}},\ \ \ \mathcal{F}^{{\epsilon}{\epsilon}}_{{\epsilon}{\epsilon}}(0|z)=\frac{1-z+z^{2}}{z(1-z)}.

(A.4)

Appendix B
Special Functions

B.1
Theta function

Here, let
q\equiv\mathrm{e}^{2\pi i\tau}
.

Definition

\displaystyle\theta_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}},

(B.1)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}q^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}},

\displaystyle\theta_{4}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{n}q^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}},

\displaystyle\eta(\tau)

\displaystyle=q^{\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n}).

Modular
T
-transformation

\displaystyle\theta_{2}(\tau+1)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi}{4}i}\theta_{2}(\tau),

(B.2)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(\tau+1)

\displaystyle=\theta_{4}(\tau),

\displaystyle\theta_{4}(\tau+1)

\displaystyle=\theta_{3}(\tau),

\displaystyle\eta(\tau+1)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi}{12}i}\eta(\tau).

Modular
S
-transformation

\displaystyle\theta_{2}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-i\tau}\theta_{4}(\tau),

(B.3)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-i\tau}\theta_{3}(\tau),

\displaystyle\theta_{4}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-i\tau}\theta_{2}(\tau),

\displaystyle\eta\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-i\tau}\eta(\tau).

Relation

\displaystyle\eta(\tau)^{3}=\frac{1}{2}\theta_{2}(\tau)\theta_{3}(\tau)\theta_{4}(\tau),

(B.4)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(\tau)^{4}=\theta_{2}(\tau)^{4}+\theta_{4}(\tau)^{4}.

B.2
Barnes Double Gamma Function

Definition

\log\Gamma_{b}(x)\equiv\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{dt}{t}\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-xt}-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{Qt}{2}}}{(1-\mathrm{e}^{-bt})(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t}{b}})}-\frac{\left(\frac{Q}{2}-x\right)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-t}}{2}-\frac{\frac{Q}{2}-x}{t}\right).

(B.5)

Asymptotics

\Gamma_{b}(x)\xrightarrow[x\to 0]{}\frac{\Gamma_{b}(Q)}{2\pi x}+O(x^{0}).

(B.6)

\log\Gamma_{b}(x)\xrightarrow[\left|x\right|\to\infty]{}-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}\ln x+\frac{3}{4}x^{2}+\frac{Q}{2}x\log x-\frac{Q}{2}x-\frac{Q^{2}+1}{12}\ln x+\ln\Gamma_{0}(b)+O(x^{-1}).

(B.7)

Poles

\Gamma_{b}(x)
has simple poles at the following points:

x=-mb-\frac{n}{b},\ \ (n,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}).

(B.8)

Also, it has no zeros.

Appendix C
Liouville CFT

C.1
Notations in Liouville CFT

Here, we summarize the notations and functions introduced for convenience in Liouville CFT.

Notation

c=1+6Q^{2},\ \ \ \ \ Q=b+\frac{1}{b},\ \ \ \ \ h_{i}={\alpha}_{i}(Q-{\alpha}_{i})=\frac{Q^{2}}{4}+\gamma_{i}^{2}.

(C.1)

\gamma
function

\gamma(x)\equiv\frac{\Gamma(x)}{\Gamma(1-x)}.

(C.2)

This satisfies the following identity.

\gamma(x+1)=-x^{2}\gamma(x).

(C.3)

Upsilon function

\Upsilon_{b}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{\Gamma_{b}(x)\Gamma_{b}(Q-x)}.

(C.4)

This satisfies the following
shift identity
,

\displaystyle\frac{\Upsilon_{b}(x+b)}{\Upsilon_{b}(x)}

\displaystyle=b^{1-2bx}\gamma(bx),

(C.5)

\displaystyle\frac{\Upsilon_{b}\left(x+\frac{1}{b}\right)}{\Upsilon_{b}(x)}

\displaystyle=b^{\frac{2x}{b}-1}\gamma\left(\frac{x}{b}\right).

Double Sine function

S_{b}(x)\equiv\frac{\Gamma_{b}(x)}{\Gamma_{b}(Q-x)}.

(C.6)

This satisfies the following
shift identity
,

\displaystyle\frac{S_{b}(x+b)}{S_{b}(x)}

\displaystyle=2\sin(\pi bx),

(C.7)

\displaystyle\frac{S_{b}\left(x+\frac{1}{b}\right)}{S_{b}(x)}

\displaystyle=2\sin\left(\pi\frac{x}{b}\right).

In particular, the following holds,

\frac{S_{b}(2i\gamma+Q)}{S_{b}(2i\gamma)}=4\sinh\left(2\pi\gamma/b\right)\sinh\left(2\pi\gamma b\right)=\frac{S_{\mathbb{I}\gamma}}{\sqrt{2}}.

(C.8)

Here,
S_{\mathbb{I}\gamma}
is the modular
S
matrix.

Normalization

\displaystyle\braket{V_{\alpha_{i}}(z)V_{\alpha_{j}}(0)}

\displaystyle=\left(iB_{\alpha_{i}}\delta(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\delta(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j})\right)\frac{1}{|z|^{4h_{i}}},

(C.9)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}=Q}C_{123}

\displaystyle=1.

Here, the normalization constant of the two-point function is defined as follows:

B_{\alpha}\equiv\left(b^{\frac{2}{b}-2b}\lambda\right)^{Q-2\alpha}\frac{\Upsilon_{b}(Q-2\alpha)}{\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha-Q)}.

(C.10)

In addition,
B_{\alpha}
satisfies the following reflection formula:

C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}=B_{\alpha_{1}}C_{Q-\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}.

(C.11)

Some references (e.g.
[
33
]
) rewrite
B_{\alpha}
using the
\gamma
function as:

B_{\alpha}=\frac{\left(\pi\mu\gamma(b^{2})\right)^{\frac{Q-2\alpha}{b}}}{b^{2}}\frac{\gamma\left(2b\alpha-b^{2}\right)}{\gamma\left(2+\frac{1}{b^{2}}-\frac{2\alpha}{b}\right)}.

(C.12)

Here,
4\pi\mu=\lambda^{\prime}
was used in conjunction with (
4.43
).

C.2
DOZZ Formula and Fusion Matrix

The OPE coefficients in Liouville CFT are given by the DOZZ formula.

\displaystyle C_{123}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(b^{\frac{2}{b}-2b}\lambda\right)^{Q-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}}\Upsilon_{b}^{\prime}(0)\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha_{2})\Upsilon_{b}(2\alpha_{3})}{\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-Q)\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}.

(C.13)

On the other hand, the fusion matrix that appears in (
7.21
) is expressed as

{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{I},{\gamma}_{3}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\gamma}_{2}&{\gamma}_{2}\\
{\gamma}_{1}&{\gamma}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]=S_{\mathbb{I}\gamma_{3}}C_{0}({\gamma}_{1},{\gamma}_{2},{\gamma}_{3}).

(C.14)

Here,
C_{0}({\gamma}_{1},{\gamma}_{2},{\gamma}_{3})
is defined as

\displaystyle C_{0}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\Gamma_{b}(2Q)}{2\sqrt{2}\pi\Gamma_{b}(Q)}\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1,2,3}\Gamma_{b}(2\alpha_{i})\Gamma(2Q-2\alpha_{i})}

(C.15)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Upsilon_{b}^{\prime}(0)}{\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-Q)\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1})\Upsilon_{b}(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})}.

Let us calculate the ratio between the two,

\displaystyle\frac{C_{123}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}}{C_{0}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})}=2\sqrt{2}\pi\left(b^{\frac{2}{b}-2b}\lambda\right)^{Q-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}}\frac{\Gamma_{b}(Q)}{\Gamma_{b}(2Q)}\prod_{i=1,2,3}\frac{\Gamma_{b}(2Q-2\alpha_{i})}{\Gamma_{b}(Q-2\alpha_{i})}.

(C.16)

Here, the content of the product can be rewritten using the normalization factor of the two-point function (
C.10
) and the modular
S
matrix as follows:

\frac{\Gamma_{b}(2Q-2\alpha)}{\Gamma_{b}(Q-2\alpha)}=\sqrt{\frac{S_{b}(2{\alpha})}{S_{b}(2{\alpha}-Q)}}\sqrt{\frac{\Upsilon_{b}(Q-2{\alpha})}{\Upsilon_{b}(2{\alpha}-Q)}}=\sqrt{\frac{S_{\mathbb{I}\gamma}}{\sqrt{2}}}\sqrt{\frac{B_{\alpha}}{\left(b^{\frac{2}{b}-2b}\lambda\right)^{Q-2\alpha}}}.

(C.17)

In the second equation, we used (
C.8
).

Thus, the ratio between the two is expressed as

\frac{C_{123}^{\mathrm{DOZZ}}}{C_{0}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})}=\mathcal{E}\prod_{i=1,2,3}\sqrt{S_{\mathbb{I}\gamma_{i}}B_{i}}.

(C.18)

Here, the constant
\mathcal{E}
independent of the conformal dimensions is defined by

\mathcal{E}\equiv 2^{\frac{3}{4}}\pi\left(b^{\frac{2}{b}-2b}\lambda\right)^{-\frac{Q}{2}}\frac{\Gamma_{b}(Q)}{\Gamma_{b}(2Q)}.

(C.19)

Appendix D
Fusion Transformation

The fusion transformation of four-point functions is expressed as follows
[
33
]
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{21}_{34}(h_{{\alpha}_{s}}|z)=\int_{\mathbb{S}}d{\alpha}_{t}{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{s},{\alpha}_{t}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]\mathcal{F}^{23}_{14}(h_{{\alpha}_{t}}|1-z).

(D.1)

The integral contour is from
\frac{Q}{2}+i0
to
\frac{Q}{2}+i\infty
.
The integral kernel
{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{s},{\alpha}_{t}}
is called the
fusion matrix
or
crossing matrix
.
The exact form of the fusion matrix is expressed as follows
[
36
,
33
]
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{F}}_{{\alpha}_{s},{\alpha}_{t}}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{1}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]=\frac{N({\alpha}_{4},{\alpha}_{3},{\alpha}_{s})N({\alpha}_{s},{\alpha}_{2},{\alpha}_{1})}{N({\alpha}_{4},{\alpha}_{t},{\alpha}_{1})N({\alpha}_{t},{\alpha}_{3},{\alpha}_{2})}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc|c}{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{s}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{4}&{\alpha}_{t}\\
\end{array}\right\}_{b}.

(D.2)

Here,
N({\alpha}_{3},{\alpha}_{2},{\alpha}_{1})
is defined by

N({\alpha}_{3},{\alpha}_{2},{\alpha}_{1})\equiv\frac{{\Gamma}_{b}(2{\alpha}_{1}){\Gamma}_{b}(2{\alpha}_{2}){\Gamma}_{b}(2Q-2{\alpha}_{3})}{{\Gamma}_{b}(2Q-{\alpha}_{1}-{\alpha}_{2}-{\alpha}_{3}){\Gamma}_{b}(Q-{\alpha}_{1}-{\alpha}_{2}+{\alpha}_{3}){\Gamma}_{b}({\alpha}_{1}+{\alpha}_{3}-{\alpha}_{2}){\Gamma}_{b}({\alpha}_{2}+{\alpha}_{3}-{\alpha}_{1})}.

(D.3)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc|c}{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{s}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&\bar{{\alpha}_{4}}&{\alpha}_{t}\\
\end{array}\right\}_{b}

is the Racah–Wigner coefficient of the quantum group
U_{q}(sl(2,\mathbb{R}))
and is expressed as follows
[
87
]
,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc|c}{\alpha}_{1}&{\alpha}_{2}&{\alpha}_{s}\\
{\alpha}_{3}&{\alpha}_{4}&{\alpha}_{t}\\
\end{array}\right\}_{b}

(D.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{S_{b}({\alpha}_{1}+{\alpha}_{4}+{\alpha}_{t}-Q)S_{b}({\alpha}_{2}+{\alpha}_{3}+{\alpha}_{t}-Q)S_{b}({\alpha}_{3}-{\alpha}_{2}-{\alpha}_{t}+Q)S_{b}({\alpha}_{2}-{\alpha}_{3}-{\alpha}_{t}+Q)}{S_{b}({\alpha}_{1}+{\alpha}_{2}-{\alpha}_{s})S_{b}({\alpha}_{3}+{\alpha}_{s}-{\alpha}_{4})S_{b}({\alpha}_{3}+{\alpha}_{4}-{\alpha}_{s})}

\displaystyle\times\left|S_{b}(2{\alpha}_{t})\right|^{2}\int^{2Q+i\infty}_{2Q-i\infty}du\frac{S_{b}(u-{\alpha}_{12s})S_{b}(u-{\alpha}_{s34})S_{b}(u-{\alpha}_{23t})S_{b}(u-{\alpha}_{1t4})}{S_{b}(u-{\alpha}_{1234}+Q)S_{b}(u-{\alpha}_{st13}+Q)S_{b}(u-{\alpha}_{st24}+Q)S_{b}(u+Q)}.

Here,
\bar{{\alpha}}\equiv Q-{\alpha}
,
{\alpha}_{ijk}\equiv{\alpha}_{i}+{\alpha}_{j}+{\alpha}_{k}
, and
{\alpha}_{ijkl}\equiv{\alpha}_{i}+{\alpha}_{j}+{\alpha}_{k}+{\alpha}_{l}
.

\Gamma_{b}(x)
is the Barnes double gamma function (
B.5
), and
S_{b}(x)
is the double sine function (
C.6
).

Since the Barnes double gamma function has simple poles at
x=-mb-\frac{n}{b},\ \ (n,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
,
the fusion matrix has zeros and poles on both the
\alpha_{s}
plane and the
\alpha_{t}
plane.
In particular, when
\alpha_{s}=0
, the position of the simple poles on the
\alpha_{t}
plane is given as follows (generalization of (
7.29
)),

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{t}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=({\alpha}_{2}+{\alpha}_{3})-Q-Q_{m,n},

(D.5)

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{t}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=-({\alpha}_{1}+{\alpha}_{4})+2Q+Q_{m,n},

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{t}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\pm({\alpha}_{1}-{\alpha}_{4})-Q_{m,n},

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{t}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=\pm({\alpha}_{2}-{\alpha}_{3})+Q+Q_{m,n},

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{t}^{(5)}

\displaystyle=-({\alpha}_{2}+{\alpha}_{3})+Q-Q_{m,n}.

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{t}^{(6)}

\displaystyle={\alpha}_{1}+{\alpha}_{4}+Q_{m,n}.

Here,
Q_{m,n}\equiv-mb-\frac{n}{b},\ \ (n,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
.
Since the Liouville momentum in unitary theories satisfies
0\leq\Re\alpha_{i}\leq\frac{Q}{2}
,
the possible range of each pole coordinate is restricted as shown in Figure
20
.
